The longest increasing subsequence of Brownian separable permutons
Arka Adhikari University of Maryland Jacopo Borga MIT Thomas Budzinski ENS de Lyon William Da Silva University of Vienna Delphin Sénizergues Université Paris Nanterre

Abstract
We establish a scaling limit result for the length LIS ⁡ ( σ n ) \mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n}) of the longest increasing subsequence of a permutation σ n \sigma_{n} of size n n sampled from the Brownian separable permuton 𝝁 p \bm{\mu}_{p} of parameter p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) , which is the universal limit of pattern-avoiding permutations. Specifically, we prove that LIS ⁡ ( σ n ) n α → n → ∞ a . s . X , \frac{\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{a.s.}}X, where α = α ⁡ ( p ) \alpha=\alpha(p) is the unique solution in the interval ( 1 / 2 , 1 ) (1/2,1) to the equation 1 4 1 2 ​ α ​ π ​ Γ ⁡ ( 1 2 − 1 2 ​ α ) Γ ⁡ ( 1 − 1 2 ​ α ) = p p − 1 , \frac{1}{4^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}=\frac{p}{p-1}, and X = X ⁡ ( p ) X=X(p) is a non-deterministic and a.s. positive and finite random variable, which is a measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton. Notably, the exponent α ⁡ ( p ) \alpha(p) is an increasing continuous function of p p with α ⁡ ( 0 + ) = 1 / 2 \alpha(0^{+})=1/2 , α ⁡ ( 1 − ) = 1 \alpha(1^{-})=1 and α ⁡ ( 1 / 2 ) ≈ 0.815226 \alpha(1/2)\approx 0.815226 , which corresponds to the permuton limit of uniform separable permutations. We prove analogous results for the size of the largest clique of a graph sampled from the Brownian cographon of parameter p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) .



1
Introduction

The Brownian separable permuton is the universal random limiting permuton describing the scaling limit of many families of random pattern-avoiding permutations. The Brownian cographon is the universal random limiting graphon describing the scaling limit of various dense random graphs sampled from graph classes. In this paper, we solve the following open problems:

•

What is the length
\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})
of the longest increasing subsequence of a random permutation
\sigma_{n}
of size
n
sampled from the Brownian separable permuton?

•

What is the size
\mathrm{LCL}(G_{n})
of the largest clique of a random graph
G_{n}
on
n
vertices sampled from the Brownian cographon?

Specifically, we prove a scaling limit result for both quantities, that is,
1
1

1

The natural couplings underlying the almost sure convergence will be described in detail in Sections
1.1
and
1.2
.

\frac{\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{a.s.}}X\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\mathrm{LCL}(G_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{a.s.}}X,

where
\alpha
is explicitly determined through a simple equation and the limiting random variable
X
is non-deterministic, almost surely positive and finite, and is a deterministic measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton and the Brownian cographon, respectively. In particular, our main results – Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
– solve
[
BDSG24
, Conjecture 1.5]

and go significantly further, as they establish precise scaling limits.

In the remainder of this section, we introduce both the Brownian separable permuton and the Brownian cographon, present precise statements of our results, and discuss the motivations behind these questions, including relevant references.

branching structures in random geometry” (DOI: 10.55776/ESP534).

1.1
Scaling limit for the length of the longest increasing subsequence

A
permuton
is a Borel probability measure
\mu
on the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
with two uniform marginals, that is,
\mu([a,b]\times[0,1])=\mu([0,1]\times[a,b])=b-a
for all
0\leq a<b\leq 1
. Permutons are natural objects to describe the scaling limits of random permutations and have been studied quite intensively in the past decade; see
[
Bor21
, Section 2.1]
for an introduction to the theory of permutons and, in particular, to the notion of permuton limit. The Brownian separable permutons
\bm{\mu}_{p}
are a one-parameter family of
random
permutons, indexed by
p\in(0,1)
, which are known to describe the permuton limit of many families of pattern-avoiding permutations
[
BBF+18
,
Maa20
,
BBF+22b
]
. Moreover, the Brownian separable permutons represent the critical regime for the skew Brownian permutons introduced in
[
Bor23
]
, a larger universality class for generalized pattern-avoiding permutations which enjoy several connections with classical objects studied in random geometry, such as Liouville quantum gravity and Schramm–Loewner evolutions
[
Bor23
,
BHSY23
,
BGS25
]
.

Given a (possibly random) permuton
\mu
, there is a natural way to sample permutations of size
n
from it: conditional on
\mu
, sample a sequence
(Z_{i})_{i\geq 1}
of independent points in the unit
square
[0,1]^{2}
with distribution
\mu
. The first
n
points
(Z_{i})_{i\leq n}
induce a random permutation
\Perm[\mu,n]
: for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\Perm[\mu,n](i)=j
if the point with
i
-th lowest
x
-coordinate has
j
-th lowest
y
-coordinate. Note that
\Perm[\mu,n]
are naturally coupled for different values of
n
.

We are interested in studying the longest increasing subsequence
\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})
of the permutations

\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{n}(p)\coloneqq\Perm[\bm{\mu}_{p},n]

(1.1)

sampled from the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
of parameter
p\in(0,1)
. The precise definition of the Brownian separable permuton is given in
Section

3.2
. We stress that the definition is given for the sake of completeness, but for the purposes of this paper, it will be enough to know the distribution of
\sigma_{n}
, which we now describe.

Let
T_{n}=T_{n}(p)
be a uniform planar rooted binary tree with
n
leaves, decorated (given the tree) with i.i.d. Bernoulli(
p
)
\oplus/\ominus
signs on its nodes,
i.e.

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\oplus}}\right)=1-\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ominus}}\right)=p
. We will say that
T_{n}
is a
p
-
signed uniform binary tree
.
See, for instance, the left-hand side of
Figure

1
.
Given
T_{n}
, we construct
\pi_{n}
via the following procedure:

•

Number the leaves of
T_{n}
from
1
to
n
from left to right.

•

For each node
v
of
T_{n}
decorated by
\ominus
, swap the order of the two children of
v
(together with their subtrees), but keep the subtrees oriented in the same way.

•

Finally, define
\pi_{n}=\pi_{n}(1)\dots\pi_{n}(n)
as the sequence of numbers for the leaves of the reordered tree from left to right.

Figure 1:
Left:
A planar rooted binary tree with
6
leaves, decorated with
\oplus/\ominus
signs on its nodes.
Right:
(1) The tree is obtained from the tree on the left by swapping the children of each
\ominus
decorated node as explained below (
1.1
). The corresponding permutation
\pi_{n}
is obtained by reading the labels of the leaves from left to right. (2) The graph is obtained from the tree on the left by following the strategy detailed below (
1.4
). (3) The rooted planar map is obtained from the tree on the left by operating parallel compositions at
\ominus
nodes and series compositions at
\oplus
nodes. Here the leaves of the tree on the left correspond to the edges of the map. The map has a natural orientation from the
source
to the
sink
, inherited from the tree.
Note that the tree leaves highlighted in red on the tree on the left induce a largest positive subtree (see
Section

2.1
for terminology). These three leaves induce a longest increasing subsequence of the corresponding permutation
\pi_{n}
, a largest clique of the corresponding graph, and a longest directed path of the corresponding planar map.

Then, as explained for instance in
[
BBF+20
, Section 5.1]
, the permutation
\pi_{n}
has the same law as
\sigma_{n}
in (
1.1
). An example of this construction is given in
Figure

1
. Moreover, as we will carefully explain in
Remark

3.2
, if the trees
T_{n}
are coupled for different values of
n
via the Rémy algorithm introduced in
Section

2.1
, then the corresponding permutations
\sigma_{n}
are coupled exactly as in (
1.1
).

Our interest in studying the longest increasing subsequence
\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})
is motivated by various factors, including the general study of longest increasing subsequences in non-uniform permutation models and the connections between the longest increasing subsequence of permutations sampled from skew Brownian permutons and certain models of directed metrics on random planar maps. We refer the reader to
[
BDSG24
, Sections 1.1.2 and 1.3.1]
for further details.
Our main result on the Brownian separable permutons is the following. We denote by
\Gamma
the standard Gamma function.

Theorem 1.1

(Scaling limit for the length of the longest increasing subsequence of the Brownian separable permutons)
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
, let
\sigma_{n}=\Perm[\bm{\mu}_{p},n]
be a random permutation of size
n
sampled from the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
of parameter
p
, as introduced in (
1.1
). Recall that the permutations
\sigma_{n}
are naturally coupled for different values of
n
. We have the almost sure convergence

\frac{\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{a.s.}X,

(1.2)

where
\alpha=\alpha(p)
is the unique solution in the interval
(1/2,1)
to the equation

\frac{1}{4^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}=\frac{p}{p-1},

(1.3)

and the limiting random variable
X=X(p)
is non-deterministic and almost surely positive and finite.
Moreover,
X
is a deterministic measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
.

Setting
\zeta_{\mathbb{Z}}(s)\coloneqq\frac{1}{4^{s}\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{\Gamma(1/2-s)}{\Gamma(1-s)}
, we can write the equation in (
1.3
) as
\zeta_{\mathbb{Z}}(\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})=\frac{p}{p-1}
. The function
\zeta_{\mathbb{Z}}(s)
is the spectral zeta function of
\mathbb{Z}
introduced in
[
FK17
]
. The beautiful formula in
[
KP22
, Theorem 2]
allows us to rewrite our equation in (
1.3
) in the following elegant product form:

\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{(i-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha})^{2}}{i(i-\tfrac{1}{\alpha})}=\frac{p}{p-1}.

We refer the reader to
[
Kar20
,
KP22
]
for more background on spectral zeta functions.
The graph of
\alpha(p)
is shown in
Figure

2
. A heuristic explanation for why the exponent
\alpha
satisfies the equation (
1.3
) is given in
Section

2.3
. However, at present, we have no explanation for the appearance of the number-theoretic function
\zeta_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Further details on the fact that
X
is a deterministic measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton are given in
Section

9.5
.

Figure 2:
The graph of the function
\alpha(p)
from
1.1
for
p\in(0,1)
. The values of
\alpha(i/10)
for
i=0,1,\dots,10
are shown on top of the function, where
\alpha(0)=\alpha(0^{+})
and
\alpha(1)=\alpha(1^{-})
.

We believe that the law of
X(p)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and that for all
p\neq p^{\prime}
, the laws of
X(p)
and
X(p^{\prime})
are distinct – although we do not prove this here. This belief is also supported by the numerical simulations in
Appendix

A
.

We recall that the study of the longest increasing subsequence of Brownian separable permutons was initiated in
[
BBD+22
]
and the best previously known result on the behavior of
\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})
was the following.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
BDSG24
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

There exist two explicit functions
\alpha_{*}:(0,1)\to(1/2,1)
and
\beta^{*}:(0,1)\to(1/2,1)
such that for all
p\in(0,1)
:

•

1/2<\alpha_{*}(p)\leq\beta^{*}(p)<1
.

•

For all
\varepsilon>0
, it holds with probability tending to one as
n\to\infty
that

n^{\alpha_{*}(p)-\varepsilon}\leq\LIS(\sigma_{n})\leq n^{\beta^{*}(p)+\varepsilon}.

We emphasize that, prior to the present work, even the existence of the exponent
\alpha(p)
was an open problem. The graphs of
\alpha_{*}(p),

\alpha(p)
and
\beta^{*}(p)
are shown on the left-hand side of
Figure

3
for comparison.
We refer to
[
BDSG24
, Remark 1.2]
for the exact equations characterizing
\alpha_{*}(p)
and
\beta^{*}(p)
.
It is interesting to note that the previously known lower bound
\alpha_{*}(p)
for the exponent
\alpha(p)
is very close to the exact value for all
p\in(0,1)
, even though
\alpha_{*}(p)<\alpha(p)
for all
p\in(0,1)
as shown on the right-hand side of
Figure

3
. We also note that the values of
\alpha(p)
given in
Figure

2
are consistent with the numerical simulations in
[
BDSG24
, Appendix A]
.

Figure 3:
Left:
The graph of the functions
\alpha_{*}(p),

\alpha(p)
and
\beta^{*}(p)
from
1.1
and
1.2
.
Right:
The graphs of
\alpha(p)-\alpha_{*}(p)
.
The maximum is reached at
p\approx 0.202
where it takes the value
\alpha(p)-\alpha_{*}(p)\approx 0.0063
.
Moreover,
\alpha(p)-\alpha_{*}(p)>0
for all
p\in(0,1)
.

Uniform separable permutations vs. permutations sampled from the permuton.

For the reader familiar with the notion of separable permutation (a beautiful introduction, including connections to algebraic geometry and Kontsevich’s polynomial interchange problem, can be found in Ghys’s book
[
Ghy16
]
), we stress that
\sigma_{n}(p)
is a.s. a separable permutation
[
BBF+20
, Definition 5.1]
for all
p\in(0,1)
, but is
not
a uniform separable permutation, even when
p=1/2
. Indeed, there are multiple rooted binary trees with
n
leaves decorated with
\oplus/\ominus
signs on their nodes that are mapped to the same separable permutation by the construction described below (
1.1
) and in
Figure

1
. Nevertheless, when
p=1/2
, the random permutations
\sigma_{n}
have
the same
permuton limit as uniform separable permutations
[
BBF+18
]
, and the two models are conjectured to display exactly the same LIS behavior; see
1.4
below and
[
BDSG24
, Conjecture 1.6]
.

Moreover, for all
p\in(0,1)
, the permutation
\sigma_{n}(p)
defined as in (
1.1
) has the following consistency property: for
k<n
, the permutation induced
by
k
uniform indices in
\sigma_{n}
has the same law as
\sigma_{k}
; see
[
BBF+20
, Proposition 2.9]
. In addition, the model
\sigma_{n}(1/2)
can be viewed as the unique model in the universality class of uniform separable permutations that satisfies this consistency property, which makes it perhaps more natural than the uniform model.

Largest positive subtrees: local convergence and scaling limit.

Due to the connection presented in
Figure

1
between trees and permutations,
1.1
has a natural translation in terms of the
p
-signed uniform binary tree
T_{n}
. With a slight abuse of notation, let
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
denote the maximal number of leaves of a subtree
2
2

2

See
Section

2.1
for the precise notion of subtrees.
of
T_{n}
whose pairwise highest common ancestors all carry
\oplus
signs. We will refer to this as the size of the “largest positive subtree” of
T_{n}
.
Then, by construction of the permutation
\pi_{n}
from
T_{n}
, we have
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})=\mathrm{LIS}(\pi_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}\mathrm{LIS}(\sigma_{n})
, so that
1.1
may be interpreted as a scaling limit result for
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
. This is precisely the point of view we adopt to prove
1.1
, as explained in the proof strategy outlined in Section
2
. As a key intermediate step, we will prove in
6.2
the local convergence of a largest positive subtree in a variant of the model
T_{n}
. As a by-product of our arguments, we will also obtain in Theorem
2.6
a description of the limiting
shape
of a largest positive subtree in the same variant of
T_{n}
. See
Section

2.4
for a more detailed discussion.

1.2
Scaling limit for the size of the largest clique and independent set

A
graphon
is an equivalence class of symmetric measurable functions
W:[0,1]^{2}\to\{0,1\}
(
i.e.

W(x,y)=W(y,x)
for all
x,y\in[0,1]
) under the equivalence relation
\sim
, where
W\sim U
if there exists a measurable, invertible, Lebesgue measure preserving function
\phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
W(\phi(x),\phi(y))=U(x,y)
for almost every
x,y\in[0,1].
A graphon can be thought of as a continuous analog of the adjacency matrix of a graph, viewed up to relabeling its continuous vertex set. Graphons have been used for many different purposes, with the original goal of having a notion of scaling limit for dense graphs
[
Lov12
]
.

The
Brownian cographon

\bm{W}_{p}
of parameter
p\in(0,1)
is a
random
universal graphon describing, for instance, the graphon limit of uniform random cographs when the number of vertices tends to infinity

[
BBF+22a
,
Stu21
]
– in this case
p=1/2
. In
[
Len23a
,
Len23b
]
, the author proves that
\bm{W}_{p}
is also the graphon limit of many other natural families of graphs, showing the universality of
\bm{W}_{p}
.

Let
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1]
. Given a graphon
W
, the random graph
\Graph[W,n]
induced by
W
of size
n
is defined as follows: consider the first
n
random variables
(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
and consider
n
vertices

\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}\}
.
We connect the vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
with an edge if and only if
W(U_{i},U_{j})=1
.
This definition can be naturally extended to the case of random graphons (see
[
BBF+22a
, Section 3.2]
for further details) and gives a coupling for the graphs
\Graph[W,n]
for different values of
n
.

An
independent set
of a graph
G
is a subset of its vertices such that any two distinct vertices in the subset are not adjacent, and a
clique
of a graph
G
is a subset of its vertices such that every two distinct vertices in the subset are adjacent.

We are interested in the size of the largest independent set
\LIN(G_{n})
and the size of the largest clique
3
3

3

These two quantities are usually denoted by
\alpha(\cdot)
and
\omega(\cdot)
in the literature, but we preferred to adopt a different notation since it is more consistent with the one used for permutations.

\LCL(G_{n})
of the graphs

G_{n}=G_{n}(p)\coloneqq\Graph[\bm{W}_{p},n]

(1.4)

sampled from the
Brownian cographon

\bm{W}_{p}
of parameter
p\in[0,1]
.
Our main motivation comes from connections with the
Erdős–Hajnal conjecture
, as explained in
[
BDSG24
, Section 1.2.2]
.

As in the case of permutons, the exact law of
\bm{W}_{p}
is not important for our results and is provided in
Section

3.2
for completeness, but the law of
G_{n}
can be described in terms of a uniform rooted binary tree
T_{n}
with
n
leaves, decorated with i.i.d. Bernoulli(
p
)
\oplus/\ominus
signs on its nodes. Specifically, numbering the leaves of
T_{n}
from
1
to
n
from left to right, the law of
G_{n}
is described as follows: there is an edge between the vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
if and only if the highest common ancestor of the
i
-th and
j
-th leaves in
T_{n}
is decorated by a
\oplus
.
Note that by flipping the
\oplus/\ominus
signs,
\LIN(G_{n}(p))
has the same law as
\LCL(G_{n}(1-p))
for all
p\in(0,1)
.

Theorem 1.3

(Scaling limit for the size of the largest clique and independent set of the Brownian cographon)
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
, let
G_{n}=\Graph[\bm{W}_{p},n]
be a random graph of size
n
sampled from the Brownian cographon
\bm{W}_{p}
of parameter
p
, as explained in (
1.4
). Recall that the graphs
G_{n}
are naturally coupled for different values of
n
. We have the almost sure convergence

\frac{\LCL(G_{n})}{n^{\alpha(p)}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{a.s.}X(p)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\LIN(G_{n})}{n^{\alpha(1-p)}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{a.s.}X(1-p),

where
\alpha(p)\in(1/2,1)
and the law of
X(p)
are as in
1.1
. Moreover,
X(p)
and
X(1-p)
are deterministic measurable functions of the Brownian cographon
\bm{W}_{p}
.

The study of the largest clique and independent sets of the Brownian cographons was initiated in
[
BBD+22
]
and the best previously known result on the behavior of
\LCL(G_{n})
and
\LIN(G_{n})
was
[
BDSG24
, Theorem 1.3]
, which is the counterpart of
1.2
above. We note right away that just like for permutations, if
G_{n}
is built from
T_{n}
as in Figure
1
, then
\LCL(G_{n})=\LIS(T_{n})
by construction. In particular, the convergence statements of Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
are equivalent.

1.3
Some conjectures and additional comments

In this final section, we outline a few conjectures and related comments that extend and contextualize our main results.

Uniform separable permutations and cographs.

We believe that our results can be extended to the length of the longest increasing subsequence in uniform separable permutations and the size of the largest clique and independent set in uniform cographs (and, more generally, to most natural models known to converge to the Brownian separable permuton or the Brownian cographon).

Conjecture 1.4
.

Let
\alpha(1/2)
and
X(1/2)
be as in
1.1
. Let
\overline{\sigma}_{n}
and
\overline{G}_{n}
be a uniform separable permutation and a uniform separable cograph of size
n
, respectively. Then there is a deterministic constant
c>0
such that we have the convergences in distribution

\frac{\LIS(\overline{\sigma}_{n})}{n^{\alpha(1/2)}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{d}}c\cdot X(1/2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\LIN(\overline{G}_{n})}{n^{\alpha(1/2)}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{d}}c\cdot X(1/2).

The numerical simulations in
Appendix

A
suggest that
c\approx 0.901
. Note that
1.4
is more precise than
[
BDSG24
, Conjecture 1.6]
.

We also expect that some of the ideas in our paper could be adapted to study the length of the longest increasing subsequence in random recursive separable permutations
[
FRL23
]
. We emphasize that these permutations have a random permuton limit that differs from the Brownian separable one.

The limiting random variable
X(p)
.

In this work, we do not determine the exact law of the limiting random variable
X(p)
appearing in
1.1
. It would be interesting to identify the distribution of
X(p)
or at least establish further properties. For instance, three natural questions are whether the laws of
X(p)
and
X(p^{\prime})
are distinct for all
p\neq p^{\prime}
, what the expectation of
X(p)
is, or what the tail behavior of
X(p)
is. Some numerical simulations of these random variables are presented in
Appendix

A
.

Hausdorff dimension of the largest increasing subset of the Browian separable permuton.

The (closed) support of the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
is the (random) set
\text{supp}(\bm{\mu}_{p})
defined as the intersection of all closed sets
K\subset[0,1]^{2}
with
\bm{\mu}_{p}(K)=1
.
We say that a set
A\subset[0,1]^{2}
is increasing if

A\subset\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([0,t]\times[0,s]}}\right)\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left([t,1]\times[s,1]}}\right)\qquad\text{for all }(t,s)\in A.

An example of an increasing set is the graph of a non-decreasing function
[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
, or a subset of such a graph.
Let
\mathcal{I}_{p}
denote the collection of increasing subsets of
\text{supp}(\bm{\mu}_{p})
. Then, we conjecture that for all
p\in(0,1)
, almost surely,

\underset{A\in\mathcal{I}_{p}}{\sup}\;\{\dim A\}=\alpha(p),

where
\alpha(p)
is defined as in
1.1
, and
\dim(\cdot)
denotes the Hausdorff dimension.
We also believe that the limiting random variable
X(p)
of
1.1
is connected to the maximal
\alpha(p)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure (with a potential gauge correction) of such a largest increasing subset.

A directed version of the Liouville quantum gravity metric.

Our results also describe the length of the longest directed path from the source to the sink in a natural model
4
4

4

As in the case of separable permutations, this natural model of series–parallel maps is the unique one within the universality class of uniform series–parallel maps that satisfies a certain consistency property; recall the discussion on uniform separable permutations on page
1.1
. Nevertheless, we expect the asymptotic behavior of the longest directed path to be the same in both the uniform and non-uniform models.
of rooted series–parallel maps
[
BBMF11
, Proposition 6]
; see also
Figure

1
.
Rooted series-parallel maps are graphs with two distinguished vertices called source and sink, formed recursively by two simple composition operations (
parallel composition
and
series composition
).
They have been used to model series and parallel electric circuits, and are a natural model of last-passage percolation in random geometry.
The scaling limit of different models of series-parallel maps (in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense) has been studied in
[
Stu20
,
ABS+25
]
.
See the discussion in
[
BDSG24
, Section 1.3.1]
for more connections between longest increasing subsequences in the skew Brownian permuton and models of last-passage percolation in random geometry, including Baxter permutations and bipolar orientations
[
BM22
]
.

In the continuum,
skew Brownian permutons were constructed in
[
Bor23
]
using Liouville quantum gravity and a pair of Schramm–Loewner evolutions. This connection was
recently further strengthened in
[
BG24
]
.
In this correspondence, it is conjectured that Brownian separable permutons can be constructed using
\text{SLE}_{4}/\text{CLE}_{4}
and critical Liouville quantum gravity (
\gamma=2
). In connection to the aforementioned discrete picture, we expect the exponent
\alpha(p)^{-1}
to coincide with the Hausdorff dimension of a directed version of the critical Liouville quantum gravity metric space; a space whose construction still represents a major challenge but which, at least in the critical regime, appears significantly more approachable in light of the results presented in this paper.

2
Proof strategy

We now explain how we prove
1.1
and
1.3
. Readers only interested in a rough idea of the proof strategy can simply browse through
Section

2.1
and take a look at the diagram in
Figure

6
. Reading through this entire section will provide a good overview of all main ideas that go into the proof of our main results.

2.1
Main results for the size of the largest positive subtree of a
p
-signed uniform binary tree

From the description of the law of
\sigma_{n}
and
G_{n}
in terms of a uniform rooted binary tree
T_{n}
with i.i.d. Bernoulli(
p
)
\oplus/\ominus
signs on its internal vertices, the convergences of
1.1
and
1.3
immediately follow if we prove an analogous scaling limit result for the size of the “largest positive subtree” of
T_{n}
, when the trees
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are coupled via the Rémy algorithm. We are now going to precisely define the “largest positive subtree” and introduce the Rémy algorithm. Then, we will state our main results on the “largest positive subtree” of
T_{n}
.

Figure 4:
Left:
A sign-decorated binary tree
t
with a subtree
t|_{\mathcal{L}}
induced by the three leaves
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
. The uncontracted version
\mathrm{u}(t|_{\mathcal{L}})
of
t|_{\mathcal{L}}
is highlighted by the red curve on
t
.
Right:
The same sign-decorated binary tree
t
with all (
i.e.
two) maximal positive (uncontracted) subtrees of
t
highlighted in red and blue. We colored in green the leaves in
\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(t)
.

Notation for trees.

We always consider planar rooted (planted) trees, drawn with the root at the bottom. By planted, we mean that the root has an additional edge extending below it, which is not connected to any other vertex; see
Figure

4
. All trees in this paper are planted (sometimes without explicit mention). The
degree

\deg(v)
of a vertex
v
is the number of edges incident to
v
(in particular, provided the tree is not a single leaf, the root of a rooted (planted) binary tree has degree
3
).
The vertices of a tree are of two types: the
leaves
, which are the vertices of degree one, and the
nodes
, which are all the other vertices (
i.e.
the internal vertices).
The
size

|t|
of a tree
t
is equal to the number of leaves in
t
.
Moreover, all the
subtrees
of any tree
t
considered in this paper are obtained by first selecting a subset
\mathcal{L}
of the leaves of
t
, then removing from
t
all the vertices that do not have a descendant in
\mathcal{L}
, and finally contracting all the vertices of degree 2 that may appear.
We denote such a subtree by
t|_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Moreover, we denote by
\mathrm{u}(t|_{\mathcal{L}})
the
uncontracted version
of
t|_{\mathcal{L}}
,
i.e.
the tree obtained by only removing from
t
all the vertices that do not have a descendant in
\mathcal{L}
. Both notions of subtrees are seen as planar rooted (planted) trees, where the root is given by the vertex with the lowest height in the original tree
t
.

A
sign-decorated tree

t
is a planar rooted (planted) tree such that all nodes are decorated with a
\oplus
or
\ominus
sign.
5
5

5

The signs
\oplus
or
\ominus
will be the only signs that we use to decorate the vertices of all trees considered in this paper, so we will often simply refer to the signs without explicitly saying the
\oplus
or
\ominus
signs.
Given a sign-decorated tree
t
and one of its nodes
v
, we denote by
s(v)
the sign decorating
v
.
Nodes carrying
\oplus
(resp.
\ominus
) signs are sometimes called positive (resp. negative).
A subtree of a sign-decorated tree
t
is still a sign-decorated tree where all the signs are inherited from
t
.
A sign-decorated tree
t
is
positive
if all the nodes are decorated with
\oplus
signs.
For any finite sign-decorated tree
t
, we write
\mathrm{LIS}(t)
for the
maximal size
(
i.e.
the maximal number of leaves) of a positive subtree of
t
. A
maximal positive subtree
of
t
is a subtree of
t
of size
\mathrm{LIS}(t)
. The notation
\mathrm{LIS}(t)
is motivated by the following fact:
\mathrm{LIS}(t)
is equal to the size of the longest increasing subsequence of the permutation obtained from
t
using the procedure described below (
1.1
) and in
Figure

1
. Similarly,
\mathrm{LIS}(t)
is equal to the size of the largest clique of the graph obtained from
t
using the procedure described below (
1.4
) and the length of the longest directed path from the source to the sink of the corresponding series-parallel planar map.
Finally, we denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(t)
the set of leaves in the intersection of
all
maximal positive subtrees of
t
.

The Rémy algorithm.

We describe a coupling between the
p
-signed uniform binary trees
T_{n}=T_{n}(p)
for all
n\geq 1
, which will be of fundamental importance in this paper. This coupling is a natural adaptation of the Rémy algorithm
[
Rém85
]
to sign-decorated trees. To build this coupling, we define
T_{n}
by induction on
n
as follows (
cf.

Figure

5
):

•

The tree
T_{1}
is just a single leaf with an additional edge extending below it (recall that our trees are planted).

•

Conditionally on
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
, let
E_{n}
be chosen uniformly at random among the
2n-1
edges of
T_{n}
, and let
S_{n}
be an independent Bernoulli
(p)

\oplus/\ominus
sign. We build
T_{n+1}
by adding in the middle of
E_{n}
a new vertex with sign
S_{n}
, and grafting a new leaf on the left of this vertex with probability
1/2
or on the right of this vertex with probability
1/2
. We see
T_{n+1}
as a rooted (planted) tree: when
E_{n}
is the special edge extending below the root of
T_{n}
, the root of
T_{n+1}
is the new vertex; otherwise it is the same as that of
T_{n}
.

We also note that the transitions of the Rémy algorithm are even simpler when it is run in reverse: in the above coupling, for any
1<m\leq n
, the law of
T_{m-1}
conditionally on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{m},T_{m+1},\dots,T_{n}}}\right)
is obtained by removing a uniform random leaf
L_{m}
of
T_{m}
and contracting the corresponding node.

Figure 5:
Our version of the Rémy algorithm for
p
-signed uniform binary trees.
In this realization, we start with a
p
-signed uniform binary tree
T_{n}
with
n
leaves, then we select a uniform edge
E_{n}
, sample a
\ominus
sign (this happens with probability
1-p
) and finally, we graft a new leaf to the
left
(this happens with probability
1/2
) of the new vertex on
E_{n}
decorated by
\ominus
.

We are now ready to state our three main results on
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
.

Theorem 2.1

(Existence of the exponent)
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
, there exists
\alpha=\alpha(p)\in(1/2,1)
such that we have the convergence in probability

\frac{\log\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})}{\log n}\xlongrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\alpha.

(2.1)

Moreover,

\alpha=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})}}\right]}{\log n}.

(2.2)

The fact that
\alpha(p)\in(1/2,1)
, provided the exponent exists, is a simple consequence of
1.2
. The latter actually yields
the better bounds
\alpha(p)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{*}(p),\beta^{*}(p)}}\right)
, but the bounds
\alpha(p)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2,1}}\right)
are sufficient for our arguments. Another important note is that the description of
\alpha
in (
2.2
) and Markov’s inequality provide a quantitative upper bound on the probability that
\LIS(T_{n})
exceeds
n^{\alpha}
by a large amount. This fact will be helpful later to obtain certain important estimates (
5.1
) used to prove the next main result (more explanations are given in
Section

2.4
).

Theorem 2.2

(Determination of the exponent)
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
, let
\alpha=\alpha(p)\in(1/2,1)
be as in
2.1
. Then
\alpha
is the unique solution in the interval
(1/2,1)
to the equation in (
1.3
), that is,

\frac{1}{4^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2\alpha}\big)}=\frac{p}{p-1}.

Our third main result gives the scaling limit of
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
.
Recall that the trees
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are coupled via the Rémy algorithm.

Theorem 2.3

(Scaling limit of
\LIS(T_{n})
)
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
, we have the almost sure convergence

\frac{\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\xlongrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{a.s.}X,

where the value
\alpha=\alpha(p)
is as in Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
and the limiting random variable
X=X(p)
is non-deterministic and almost surely positive and finite.

Note that Theorems
2.2
and
2.3
immediately imply Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
due to the description of the law of
\sigma_{n}
and
G_{n}
. The only fact that does not immediately follow from the above results is that the random variable
X
in
1.1
(resp.
1.3
) is a deterministic measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton (resp. the Brownian cographon). This fact will be justified in
Section

9.5
.

The rest of this section is organized as follows. We first explain our strategy to prove the first main result, that is, the existence of the exponent
\alpha
in
2.1
. This is done via a supermultiplicativity argument explained in
Section

2.2
.
Then, in a second phase, we determine the exact value of
\alpha(p)
by showing that
\alpha(p)
is the unique solution to the equation in (
1.3
) in the interval
(1/2,1)
. The latter step is the most involved part of the paper and will be explained in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
.
At the end of
Section

2.4
, we will also explain how we prove our last main result, that is, the scaling limit in
2.3
.

We invite the reader to compare the explanations in the next sections with the diagram in
Figure

6
which should be helpful to visualize the structure of the full proof.

Important note
. From now on, we consider
p\in(0,1)
as a fixed parameter and omit its dependency in our notation. We note that our constants may still depend on
p
.

Figure 6:

A diagram for the strategy of the proof of the three main results of this paper,
i.e.
Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
. The existence of the exponent
\alpha
(
2.1
) is proved in
Section

4
via a supermultiplicativity argument.

The gray path:
It is relatively simple to deduce from the existence of
\alpha
that
q(k)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\LIS(T)=k}}\right)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}
(
5.1
). To determine the exact value of
\alpha
(
2.2
), we first find an appropriate recursive relation (
2.4
) for the sequence
q(k)
. Assuming certain optimal regularity estimates (
2.9
), one can determine the value of
\alpha
from the recursive relation for
q(k)
; but unfortunately such optimal regularity estimates are out of reach with our methods.
The green path:
To circumvent this issue, in
Section

5
, we first deduce from the existence of the exponent
\alpha
certain rough regularity estimates (
5.1
). Then, from these estimates, we obtain in
Section

6
a local convergence result for a maximal positive subtree seen from a uniformly chosen leaf in the related tree model
T^{k}
(
6.2
) and in
Section

7.1
a corresponding law of large numbers for
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
(
7.2
). Finally, in
Section

7.2
, we obtain from this law of large numbers certain good regularity estimates (
7.1
).
The latter estimates, combined with a new technique which converts the recursive relation for
q(k)
into certain auxiliary inequalities (Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
), allow us to determine
\alpha
in
Section

8
.
The orange path:
Starting from the good regularity estimates and running again part of the proof of the local convergence theorem, we obtain in
Section

9
a quantitative version of the law of large numbers for
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
(
9.1
) and finally deduce our scaling limit result for
X_{n}=\LIS(T_{n})
(
2.3
).

2.2
Existence of the exponent
\alpha
via supermultiplicativity

We introduce the compact notation

X_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n}).

(2.3)

Our first objective will be to show the existence of the exponent
\alpha
, proving
2.1
.
This is done in
Section

4
using a supermultiplicativity argument.
The basic idea here would be to decompose the trees
T_{n}
in some subregions to obtain a supermultiplicativity equation for the random variables
X_{n}
of the form

X_{m\cdot n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{n}^{(i)},

(2.4)

where the
X_{n}^{(i)}
are i.i.d. copies of
X_{n}
and are independent of
X_{m}
.
Here
\succeq
denotes stochastic domination (see
Section

3.1
for further details if necessary). This domination could be interpreted as a “super-branching property” for the sequence
(X_{2^{k}})_{k\geq 0}
. Comparison with branching processes could then be applied to get that
(X_{2^{k}})^{\frac{1}{k}}
converges in probability to some constant, and hence by monotonicity we would have

\frac{\log X_{n}}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\alpha.

The issue is that we will not be able to write an equation as simple as (
2.4
) because most sensible ways to decompose uniform binary trees into several regions result in regions with
random
and
different
sizes (
3.8
). This will have the consequence that our supermultiplicativity equation (see (
4.3
)) will be more complicated than (
2.4
). However, we can exactly describe the joint law of such random regions (
3.8
). This will enable us to compare our supermultiplicativity equation with a more standard one (see (
4.7
)) and to prove
2.1
.

2.3
The sequences
q
and
Q
and a heuristic derivation of the exponent
\alpha

The next step is to determine the exact value of
\alpha
by showing that
\alpha
is the unique solution to the equation (
1.3
) in the interval
(1/2,1)
.
For the remainder of
Section

2
, we omit most technical details and make frequent use of the symbol
\approx
to indicate that two quantities behave similarly, in a deliberately imprecise sense.

2.3.1
The sequences
q
and
Q

Here, our first idea is to randomize the size of
T_{n}
by introducing a critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
with i.i.d. Bernoulli(
p
) signs on its nodes (sampled independently of the tree), so that
T
conditioned to have size
n
is distributed as
T_{n}
.
We introduce the two fundamental sequences
q=(q(k))_{k\geq 1}
and
Q=(Q(k))_{k\geq 1}
, defined for all
k\geq 1
by

q(k)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad Q(k)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)\geq k}}\right)=\sum_{i\geq k}q(i).

(2.5)

We first explain intuitively why the sequences
q
and
Q
are related to the exponent
\alpha
that we aim to compute.
Write
Q(k)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=n}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\LIS(T_{n})\geq k}}\right)
.
Using Theorem
2.1
, we get that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\LIS(T_{n})\geq k}}\right)
is very small if
k\gg n^{\alpha}
and very close to
1
if
k\ll n^{\alpha}
.
This suggests that

Q(k)\approx\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|\geq k^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}}\right)\approx k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}},

using the standard fact that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|\geq n}}\right)\approx n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
(
3.3
).
A rigorous version of this argument (
5.1
) yields

q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+o(1)}\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(2.6)

To simplify the notation, it will be convenient to set

\gamma\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\alpha}+1\in\Big(\frac{3}{2},2\Big),

(2.7)

so that
q(k)=k^{-\gamma+o(1)}
and
Q(k)=k^{-\gamma+1+o(1)}
by (
2.6
). To determine
\gamma
(or equivalently
\alpha
), we write a recursion for the sequence
q
by decomposing the critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
at the root. We obtain the following equation.

Lemma 2.4

(Recursive relation for the sequence
q
)
.

Let
q(k)
be as in (
2.5
). Then for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle q(k)

\displaystyle=(1-p)q(k)\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}q(i)+p\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\,\mathrm{even}\}}\frac{p}{2}q(k/2)^{2}.

(2.8)

Proof.

Decompose the critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
at the root.
The four summands correspond to the following four cases: the root (which is necessarily a node because
k\geq 2
)
is decorated by a
\ominus/\oplus
sign and the left and right subtrees have maximal positive subtrees of different/equal sizes.
∎

Note that one can explicitly compute that
q(1)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\LIS(T)=1}}\right)=(1+\sqrt{p})^{-1}
and then use (
2.8
) to numerically compute the first few values of the sequence
q
.
Unfortunately, although the recursive relation in (
2.8
) entirely determines the sequence
q
, it is very sensitive to initial conditions, see for instance
Figure

7
.
As a result, it seems quite hard to deduce anything accurate on the sequence
q
directly from (
2.8
), without any additional input.

Figure 7:
We fix
p=1/2
. In blue, the first 100 values of the sequence
q(k)
computed using the recursive relation in (
2.8
) with initial condition
q(1)=2-\sqrt{2}
. In orange, the first 100 values of the sequence
\widetilde{q}(k)
computed using the same recursive relation in (
2.8
) with initial condition
\widetilde{q}(1)=2-\sqrt{2}+0.005
. The first values of each sequence are not shown because they are too large.

2.3.2
A heuristic calculation for the exponent
\alpha
assuming optimal regularity estimates

We now explain how one could determine the value of
\gamma
assuming the following
optimal regularity estimates
about the sequences
q
and
Q
(which we will never be able to establish): For some constant
A>0
, assume

\displaystyle Q(k)\sim\frac{A}{\gamma-1}\cdot k^{-\gamma+1},\qquad q(k)\sim Ak^{-\gamma},\qquad q(k-1)-q(k)\sim\gamma Ak^{-\gamma-1}\qquad\text{as }k\rightarrow\infty.

(2.9)

Rearranging the terms of (
2.8
), we get

\displaystyle q(k)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\text{ even}\}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right),

so that

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)q(k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)}}\right)-q(k)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\text{ even}\}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right).

(2.10)

Then we could determine the value of
\gamma
by inserting the asymptotics (
2.9
) into the equation (
2.10
) and equating their leading term as
k\rightarrow\infty
.
First, the left-hand side would be

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)q(k)=\frac{1-p}{p}\frac{A^{2}}{\gamma-1}k^{-2\gamma+1}+o(k^{-2\gamma+1}).

Note already that some terms on the right-hand side will not contribute to the leading term of order
k^{-2\gamma+1}
, because

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\text{ even}\}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}=O(k^{-2\gamma})=o(k^{-2\gamma+1}).

By Abel summation, the sum on the right-hand side of (
2.10
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}Q(i)q(k-i)-\sum_{i=2}^{(k+1)/2}Q(i)q(k-i+1)

\displaystyle=q(k-1)-Q\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\Big\rfloor\Big)q\Big(\Big\lceil\frac{k+1}{2}\Big\rceil\Big)+\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}Q(i)(q(k-i)-q(k-i+1)).

We use this to express the right-hand side of (
2.10
) and
allow ourselves to replace any of the terms
q(k)
,
Q(k)
by their asymptotics given in (
2.9
) and neglect any term that would be
o(k^{-2\gamma+1})
.
We get (the entire argument could be turned into an actual proof with some additional details that we spare to the reader)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)}}\right)-q(k)

\displaystyle\approx q(k-1)-q(k)-\frac{A}{\gamma-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}}}\right)^{-\gamma+1}\cdot A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}}}\right)^{-\gamma}+\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}\frac{A}{\gamma-1}i^{-\gamma+1}\cdot\gamma A(k-i)^{-\gamma-1}

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{A^{2}\cdot 2^{2\gamma-1}}{\gamma-1}\cdot k^{-2\gamma+1}+\frac{\gamma A^{2}}{\gamma-1}\cdot k^{-2\gamma+1}\cdot\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}\frac{1}{k}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma-1}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{A^{2}}{\gamma-1}\cdot k^{-2\gamma+1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-2^{2\gamma-1}+\gamma\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{-\gamma+1}(1-x)^{-\gamma-1}\mathrm{d}x}}\right),

where the integral comes as the limit of a Riemann sum. Equating the leading term on the left-hand side and right-hand side of (
2.10
) would ensure that

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}=-2^{2\gamma-1}+\gamma\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{-\gamma+1}(1-x)^{-\gamma-1}\mathrm{d}x,

(2.11)

which after the substitution
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2(\gamma-1)}
from (
2.7
) and some manipulations
6
6

6

We will formally do these computations in the second part of the proof of
8.3
on page
8.8
.
would lead to the equation in (
1.3
).

Remark 2.5
.

It is possible to run a different version of the above argument and identify the exponent
\gamma
(and
\alpha
) from a set of assumptions that are weaker than (
2.9
), namely that there exists a slowly varying function
\varphi
such that

\displaystyle q(k)=k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)\qquad\text{and that}\qquad\frac{q(k+1)}{q(k)}=1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right).

We will be able to prove that the first asymptotic holds thanks to
7.1
below, but the second one will remain out of reach using our methods. Hence, we will need a different strategy explained in the next subsection.

2.4
Our proof strategy: good but not optimal regularity estimates

We now describe the strategy used in this paper.
Most of the paper will be devoted to proving some
good regularity estimates
for the sequences
q
and
Q
. Specifically, we show that there exist slowly varying functions
\varphi
and
\Phi
(see
Section

3.1
for definitions) such that

\displaystyle q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\varphi(k)\qquad\text{and}\qquad Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\Phi(k),

(2.12)

where
\alpha
is the exponent of
2.1
.
Although these estimates are not as precise as (
2.9
), they will still be sufficient to conclude with some additional effort.

The rest of this section is dedicated to outlining the key ideas behind the structure of this paper.

The dynamical picture.

The control (
2.12
) on the sequences
q
and
Q
will come from understanding a dynamical version of the problem.
We will work on a probability space where the whole sequence
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is coupled under Rémy’s algorithm described in Section
2.1
:
under this construction, conditionally on
(T_{i})_{i\geq n}
,
the tree
T_{n-1}
can be obtained by removing a uniform leaf
L_{n}
from
T_{n}
and contracting the corresponding node.
This ensures that the corresponding sequence
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
defined in (
2.3
) is non-decreasing and changes at each step by either
0
or
1
.
We denote by
\tau_{k}
the first time that
X_{n}
attains any number
k\geq 1
.
Note that studying the asymptotic behavior of the process
(\tau_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is equivalent to studying that of
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}

since

X_{n}=k\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathopen{\llbracket}\tau_{k}\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\tau_{k+1}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}.

(2.13)

It just happens that the sequences
q
and
Q
are more naturally controlled in terms of the stopping times
(\tau_{k})_{k\geq 1}
, as we can see below.

How the sequence
(\tau_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is related to
q
and
Q
.

Let the
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
be defined on the same probability space as, and independent of, the sequence
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.
Since
T
conditioned to have size
n
is distributed as
T_{n}
, we have that
\LIS(T)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}X_{|T|}
.
Combining this with the asymptotics
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|\geq n}}\right)\approx cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}
that holds for some constant
c>0
(
3.3
), we get that

Q(k)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\LIS(T)\geq k}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{|T|}\geq k}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:sttm-X-rel}}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|\geq\tau_{k}}}\right)\approx c\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right].

(2.14)

Similarly, we have
q(k)=Q(k)-Q(k+1)\approx c\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}-\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]
.

Regularity of
q
,
Q
and
(\tau_{k})_{k\geq 1}
.

By Karamata’s characterization theorem
[
BGT89
, Theorem 1.4.1]
for slowly varying functions, in order to prove the estimates (
2.12
), it is enough to show that, for any
x\geq 1
, we have
\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}\to x^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
as
k\to\infty
and the analogous estimate for
q
.
Our objective is hence to control the ratios
\frac{q(xk)}{q(k)}
and
\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}
.
From the previous paragraph, this could be obtained by showing that in some sense, when
k
is large, we have

\tau_{\lceil xk\rceil}\approx x^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\tau_{k}.

(2.15)

We start by controlling what happens between the two consecutive increase times
\tau_{k}
and
\tau_{k+1}
.

Controlling the time
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k})

between successive increases of
X
.

First note that for a given
n\geq 2
,
we have
X_{n-1}=X_{n}-1
if, and only if, the leaf
L_{n}
removed from
T_{n}
to obtain
T_{n-1}
belongs to the set
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})
of leaves that are in
all
positive subtrees of
T_{n}
of maximal size.
Hence, we can write

\displaystyle X_{n-1}=X_{n}-\mathds{1}_{L_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})},

(2.16)

where conditionally on
(T_{i})_{i\geq n}
, the probability of the event
\{L_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})\}
is
\frac{1}{n}\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})
.

Now let us apply this argument in a cascading manner: we start at time
n=\tau_{k+1}-1
, so that
X_{n}=k
, and remove one leaf after another from the tree until its
\LIS
drops below
k
, that is, until we reach
\tau_{k}-1
.
Heuristically, we get for all
j\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}>j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}T_{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{\tau_{k+1}-1}\notin\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-1}),\dots,L_{\tau_{k+1}-j}\notin\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-j})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}T_{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-1})}{\tau_{k+1}-1}}}\right)\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-j})}{\tau_{k+1}-j}}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}T_{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\,\,\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-1})}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{j},

which suggests the approximation in distribution

\mathrm{Law}(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}|T_{\tau_{k+1}})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\approx}\mathrm{Geom}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-1})}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right).

(2.17)

A key insight.

The key insight (justified in the next paragraphs) underlying the strategy that we follow for the rest of the paper is that, in a suitable probabilistic sense, we have

\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})\approx\lambda X_{n}\qquad\text{and hence }\qquad\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{\tau_{k+1}-1})\approx\lambda k,

(2.18)

for some
a priori
unknown
\lambda\in(0,1)
depending only on
p
.
Hence, from (
2.17
) we can compare the law of
\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
conditional on
\tau_{k+1}
with a geometric random variable of parameter
\frac{\lambda k}{\tau_{k+1}}
, which has expectation
\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}
.
From this, we can deduce that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{j}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{j+1}}}\right]\approx\tau_{j+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda j}}}\right)
. By the Markov property (and assuming some concentration of
\tau_{k}
conditionally on
\tau_{\lceil xk\rceil}
), for
x\geq 1
, we get

\displaystyle\tau_{k}\approx\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{\lceil xk\rceil}}}\right]\approx\tau_{\lceil xk\rceil}\cdot\prod_{j=k}^{\lceil xk\rceil-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda j}}}\right)\approx\tau_{\lceil xk\rceil}\cdot x^{-\frac{1}{\lambda}}.

(2.19)

Since from (
2.6
) we expect that
\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}\approx x^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
and from (
2.14
) that
Q(k)\approx c\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]
, the last display implies in particular that the constant
\lambda
is actually equal to
\alpha
(see
Remark

7.9
).
Moreover, recalling (
2.15
), this is exactly what we need to derive the regularity estimates for
q
and
Q
.
The justification for the key insight in (
2.18
) will be developed in the next paragraphs.

A first rough estimate.

It is possible to use the above line of reasoning using the inequality
\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})\leq X_{n}
, valid deterministically, instead of the more precise (
2.18
).
This (together with a symmetric argument with time going forwards)
leads to the following estimates that hold for some constant
C>0
, for all
k\geq 1
and all
x\geq 1
:

\displaystyle Cx^{-C}\leq\frac{q(xk)}{q(k)}\leq C\qquad\text{and}\qquad(1+o_{k\rightarrow\infty}(1))x^{-C}\leq\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}\leq 1.

(2.20)

These, together with the asymptotics (
2.6
), are referred to as the
rough regularity estimates
and constitute the content of
5.1
, proved in Section
5
.
These estimates are not precise enough to obtain (
2.12
), nor to extract the exponent
\gamma
from (
2.8
).
However, they will still prove useful in the next part of the argument, which is itself used to prove (
2.18
).

A local convergence argument.

In order to get (
2.18
), we rely on a local convergence argument developed in
Section

6
.
There, we work with a slightly different tree model, denoted by
T^{k}
, which has the distribution of the
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
conditioned on the event
\{\LIS(T)=k\}
.
7
7

7

Beware that
T^{k}
and
T_{n}
stand for two very different conditionings; to avoid any potential risk of confusion we shall always stick to
k
for the conditioning on the LIS and
n
for that on the number of leaves.

More precisely, for any finite sign-decorated binary tree
t
, we write
t^{\lmax}
for the
leftmost
maximal positive subtree of
t
. This is the maximal positive subtree of
t
informally defined by going left at any negative node
v
where the left and right subtrees above
v
have equal
\LIS
. We will study the local convergence of
T^{k}
around a leaf
L^{k}
chosen uniformly at random among the
k
leaves of
(T^{k})^{\lmax}
; see
6.2
and the discussion below it.

The main tool that we use is some
exploration process
that records (among other things) the LIS of the subtree above node
v
, as
v
travels along the spine of the tree from the root to the marked leaf
L^{k}
.
It turns out that this process is a Markov chain and this is precisely our reason for studying the conditioned model
T^{k}
with fixed LIS instead of
T_{n}
.

The proof of local convergence relies on the construction of a coupling between the exploration processes associated with
T^{k}
and
T^{k^{\prime}}
, for different large values of
k
and
k^{\prime}
, ensuring that the two processes coalesce very fast as
k,k^{\prime}\to\infty
.
Since the transitions of the Markov chain are given in terms of the sequence
q(k)
, the rough regularity estimates (
2.20
) play an important role in this proof.

Motivation for the local convergence.

The point of the former result is to prove that the probability that the uniform leaf
L^{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T^{k})
tends to some deterministic limit
\lambda\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},1\mathclose{)}
, which can be interpreted as the probability of some event happening in the local limit.
This in turn ensures that, as
k\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T^{k})}{\LIS(T^{k})}=\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T^{k})}{k}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}\lambda.

(2.21)

This is the content of Theorem
7.2
proved in
Section

7.1
.
With some additional work, it is possible to transfer this result to our original model
T_{n}
, which has the same distribution as
T
conditionally on
\{|T|=n\}
.
This ensures that for any large
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})\approx\lambda\LIS(T_{n})=\lambda X_{n},

(2.22)

obtaining the key insight from (
2.18
).
While the existence of the local limit is motivated by the concentration result in (
2.21
), it also provides valuable insights into the landscape around a uniformly chosen leaf in a maximal positive subtree.

Obtaining the good regularity estimates and the exponent
\alpha
.

To summarize, we used the rough regularity estimate (
2.20
), obtained earlier, to derive (
2.22
).
This, in turn, allows us to rigorously justify the arguments leading to
(
2.19
), and thereby obtain the
good regularity estimates
in (
2.12
) for the sequences
q
and
Q
.
This last step actually turns out to be quite challenging and will
take up the whole of
Section

7.2
.

Once (
2.12
) is established, we will need a new technique in
Section

8
to extract the value of the exponent
\alpha
.
Here the main idea is to
compare the recursive relation for
q
from
2.4
with two related
inequalities
, each satisfied by a more regular test sequence.
These test sequences, combined with (
2.12
), will then yield sharp enough upper and lower bounds for the sequence
q
to extract the exact exponent
\alpha
.

As a by-product: the scaling limit result.

Using as an input the newly obtained good regularity estimates (
2.12
), we can revisit the local convergence argument and obtain in
9.1
a more quantitative version of (
2.21
).
We find that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for
k
large we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T^{k})-\alpha k|}}\right]\leq k^{-\varepsilon}.

(2.23)

Going back to (
2.16
) and assuming that (
2.23
) can be leveraged to get some quantitative control over
|\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})-\alpha X_{n}|
, we get that roughly

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{n-1}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}(T_{i})_{i\geq n}}}\right]=X_{n}-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})}{n}\approx X_{n}-\frac{\alpha X_{n}}{n}\approx\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{n}}}\right)X_{n}.

If the last display held with an equal sign, it would be easy by some (backward) martingale argument to show that
n^{-\alpha}X_{n}
converges almost surely to some random variable.
The proof of
2.3
in
Section

9
follows this idea, by writing
n^{-\alpha}X_{n}
as some (backward) martingale part plus some error term, and showing that the estimates that we have are sufficient to control the error term.
Some additional work will then be needed to ensure that all the properties of the limit
X
claimed in
2.3
hold.

Another by-product: scaling limit of largest positive subtrees of
T^{k}
.

Let us finally mention that another by-product of our proof is a scaling limit result for the shape of
the leftmost maximal positive subtree of
T^{k}
, which complements the local convergence mentioned before (
6.2
).
If
t
is a finite sign-decorated binary tree, we recall that
t^{\lmax}
stands for its leftmost maximal positive subtree. We can equip it with the metric coming from the restriction to
t^{\lmax}
of the graph distance on
t
(equivalently, this is the graph distance on the uncontracted version of
t^{\lmax}
). If
a>0
and
S
is a metric space, we also denote by
a\cdot S
the metric space obtained by multiplying the distances in
S
by a factor
a
.

Theorem 2.6

(Scaling limit of the leftmost positive subtree of
T^{k}
)
.

Let
\alpha
be the unique solution in the interval
(1/2,1)
to the equation in (
1.3
) and recall that
\gamma-1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:gamma-alpha-rel}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2\alpha}\in(1/2,1)
.
Recall also the slowly varying function
\varphi
of (
2.12
).
Then the following convergence in distribution holds for the Gromov–Hausdorff topology:

\frac{\varphi(k)}{k^{\gamma-1}}\cdot(T^{k})^{\lmax}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma},

where
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is the self-similar fragmentation tree with self-similarity index
1-\gamma
and with binary dislocation measure
\nu_{\gamma}
given by

\int f(s_{1},s_{2})\nu_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s})=\int_{0}^{1/2}f(1-x,x)\,x^{-\gamma}(1-x)^{-\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(2.24)

for any measurable function
f:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Since this theorem is not needed anywhere in the proof of the main results, we omit precise definitions of continuous fragmentation trees and refer to
[
HM12
]
instead. We will give a short proof of Theorem
2.6
in Section
9.6
as an easy consequence of the good regularity estimates on
q
, together with a general convergence criterion established by Haas and Miermont in
[
HM12
]
. Since Theorem
2.6
is the result that comes naturally as a by-product of our arguments, we do not try to show the analogous convergence for
(T_{n})^{\lmax}
, which appears to be more challenging.

3
Notation and preliminary results

3.1
Basic notation and definitions

We denote by
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
the set of non-negative integers, and for all real numbers
a<b
, we write the integer interval
[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
as
\mathopen{\llbracket}a\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},b\mathclose{\rrbracket}
.
The set of positive real numbers is denoted by
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, while the set of non-negative real numbers is denoted by
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Moreover, we often write
a\wedge b
for
\min(a,b)
and
a\vee b
for
\max(a,b)
.

For sums, we use the convention
\sum_{k=x}^{y}\coloneqq\sum_{k=\lceil x\rceil}^{\lfloor y\rfloor}
, for all
x<y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. The same convention is naturally extended to the sequences
q=(q(k))
and
Q=(Q(k))
defined in (
2.5
) for which we write
Q(x)
instead of
Q(\lceil x\rceil)
when
x\geq 1
.

For two real-valued functions
f
and
g
with domain
D
, we write

f(x)=o(g(x))

if

\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\xrightarrow[x\to\infty]{}0.

We write

f(x)=O(g(x))

if there exists a constant
C>0
such that

f(x)\leq C\,g(x)

for all
x\in D
.
Moreover, given a parameter
K>0
, we write

f(x)=o_{K}(g(x))

if

\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\xrightarrow[x\to\infty]{}0,

where the rate of convergence depends on
K
(but not on
x
).
Similarly, given a parameter
K>0
, we write

f(x)=O_{K}(g(x))

if there exists a constant
C_{K}>0
depending on
K
(but not on
x
) such that

f(x)\leq C_{K}\,g(x)

for all
x\in D
.

We recall that a
slowly varying function
is a measurable function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
x>0
, we have
\frac{\varphi(xz)}{\varphi(z)}\to 1
as
z\to\infty
.
A measurable function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is called
regularly varying
of index
\beta
if for all
x>0
, we have
\frac{\varphi(xz)}{\varphi(z)}\to x^{\beta}
as
z\to\infty
.
A comprehensive reference for the theory of regular variation is
[
BGT89
]
. In particular, we will need the following standard results on slowly varying functions. The first item is the Uniform Convergence Theorem
[
BGT89
, Theorem 1.2.1]
and the second is a particular case of the Potter bounds
[
BGT89
, Theorem 1.5.6, item (ii)]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a slowly varying function.

1.

For any constant
K>0
, the ratio
\frac{\varphi(a)}{\varphi(b)}
converges to
1
as
a,b\to\infty
uniformly in
a,b
as long as
K^{-1}\leq\frac{a}{b}\leq K
.

2.

For any
\delta>0
, there is an absolute constant
K=K(\delta)
such that for all integers
1\leq a<b
, we have
\frac{\varphi(a)}{\varphi(b)}\leq K\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{-\delta}
.

For a sequence of real-valued random variables, we write
Y_{n}\xlongrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathbb{P}}Y
to denote convergence in probability to the random variable
Y
, we write
Y_{n}\xlongrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathrm{a.s.}}Y
to denote almost sure convergence, and
Y_{n}\xlongrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathrm{d}}Y
to denote convergence in distribution. When two random variables
Y
and
Z
have the same law, we write
Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}Z
.

We also recall that a real-valued random variable
X

stochastically dominates
another real-valued random variable
Y
if there exists a coupling of
X
and
Y
such that
X\geq Y
a.s., and we write
X\succeq Y
.

A summary of the main notation used throughout this paper can be found in the index on page
Index of notation
.

3.2
The Brownian separable permuton and the Brownian cographon

For completeness, we describe the constructions of the Brownian separable permutons and the Brownian cographons. We refer the reader to
[
Maa20
,
BBF+20
,
BBF+22a
]
for more details.

Under
\mathbb{P}
, we call
signed Brownian excursion
a triplet
(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p)
consisting of a (normalized) Brownian excursion
\mathfrak{e}
, together with an independent sequence
\mathfrak{s}
of i.i.d. Bernoulli
(p)

\oplus/\ominus
signs,
i.e.

\mathbb{P}(\oplus)=1-\mathbb{P}(\ominus)=p
.
One should think of the sequence
\mathfrak{s}
as being indexed by the local minima of
\mathfrak{e}
.

We define the following random relation
\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}
on
[0,1]
:
conditional on
\mathfrak{e}
, if
x,y\in[0,1]
with
x<y
, and if
\min_{[x,y]}\mathfrak{e}
is reached at a unique point which is a strict local minimum
m_{x,y}\in(x,y)
, then

\begin{cases}x\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}y&\quad\text{if}\quad\mathfrak{s}(m_{x,y})=\oplus,\\
y\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}x&\quad\text{if}\quad\mathfrak{s}(m_{x,y})=\ominus.\\
\end{cases}

(3.1)

Standard properties of the Brownian excursion (see, for instance,
[
BDSG24
, Lemma 2.1]
) ensure the existence of a random subset
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}\subset[0,1]
such that:

•

Almost surely, the complement of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}
has Hausdorff dimension
1/2
.

•

For every
x,y\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}
with
x<y
, the minimum
\min_{[x,y]}\mathfrak{e}
is reached at a unique point which is a strict local minimum.

In particular, the restriction of the relation
\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}
to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}
is a total order.
Introducing the function

\forall t\in[0,1],\quad\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}(t)\coloneqq\Leb\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\big\{x\in[0,1]\mid x\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}t\big\}}}\right),

(3.2)

then the (biased)
Brownian separable permuton
of parameter
p\in(0,1)
is the push-forward of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
via the mapping
(\mathbb{I},\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p})
, where
\mathbb{I}
denotes the identity. That is,

\bm{\mu}_{p}(\cdot)\coloneqq(\mathbb{I},\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p})_{*}\Leb(\cdot)=\Leb\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\{t\in[0,1]\mid(t,\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}(t))\in\cdot\,\}}}\right).

(3.3)

Heuristically speaking,
\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}
is the
continuum permutation
of the elements in the interval
[0,1]
induced by the order
\vartriangleleft_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}
and
\bm{\mu}_{p}
is the diagram of
\psi_{\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p}
. We highlight that
\bm{\mu}_{p}
is a
random
permuton.

Remark 3.2
.

Recall from the discussion around (
1.1
) that, given
\bm{\mu}_{p}
, one can sample a corresponding permutation
\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{n}(p)=\Perm[\bm{\mu}_{p},n]
of size
n
. We also described the law of
\sigma_{n}
in terms of a
p
-signed uniform binary tree
T_{n}=T_{n}(p)
. We now explain how to obtain
T_{n}
directly from
\bm{\mu}_{p}
, or more precisely, how one can couple
T_{n}
with the signed excursion
(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p)
used in the construction of
\bm{\mu}_{p}
. As a corollary, we will see that the trees
T_{n}
are coupled for different values of
n
exactly as in the Rémy algorithm from
Section

2.1
.

For this, let
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables in
[0,1]
. For each
n\geq 1
, consider the first
n
points
(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
in
[0,1]
and define the tree
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(U_{i})_{i\leq n})
recursively as follows. Let
(\overline{U}_{i})_{i\leq n}
be the order statistic of
(U_{i})_{i\leq n}
and let
m_{i}
be the minimum value of
\mathfrak{e}
on
[\overline{U}_{i},\overline{U}_{i+1}]
for all
i<n
. If
n=1
, then
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;U_{1})
is a leaf.
Otherwise, let
m=\min_{i}\{m_{i}\}
and denote by
j
the index such that
m_{j}=m
. Then
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(U_{i})_{i\leq n})
is the tree whose root is decorated by
\mathfrak{s}(m)
and whose children are
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(\overline{U}_{i})_{i\leq j})
and
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(\overline{U}_{i})_{j+1\leq i\leq n})
.
Then
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(U_{i})_{i\leq n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}T_{n}(p)
and the trees
t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;(U_{i})_{i\leq n})
are coupled for different values of
n
exactly as in the Rémy algorithm – see for instance
[
Le 05
, Section 2.6]
or
[
Pit06
, Exercise 7.4.11]
.

We now turn to the Brownian cographon. Given the signed excursion
(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p)
, the
Brownian cographon

\bm{W}_{p}
of parameter
p\in[0,1]
is defined (following
[
BBF+22a
]
) as the graphon equivalence class of the random function

\displaystyle\bm{W}_{p}:

\displaystyle[0,1]^{2}\to\{0,1\},

(3.4)

\displaystyle(x,y)\mapsto\,\,\,\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{s}(m_{x,y})=\oplus},

where, as in (
3.1
), if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}
and
x<y
, we denote by
m_{x,y}\in[x,y]
the unique strict local minimum
\min_{[x,y]}\mathfrak{e}
. If
x
or
y
is not in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}
then we arbitrarily set
\mathfrak{s}(m_{x,y})=\oplus
. This choice does not change the law of
\bm{W}_{p}
because a.s.
\Leb([0,1]^{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{e}}^{2})=0
. We highlight that
\bm{W}_{p}
is a
random
graphon.

3.3
A simple estimate for critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson trees

Recall that
T
denotes a critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree with i.i.d. Bernoulli(
p
) signs on its nodes, so that
T
conditioned to have size
n
is distributed as
T_{n}
. We will often use the following simple estimate on the size of
T
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all
n\geq 1
, set
r(n)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=n}}\right)
. Then

r(n)=\frac{1}{2^{2n-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}n^{-3/2}+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{-5/2}}}\right).

(3.5)

In particular,
r(n)
is decreasing in
n
.

Proof.

There are
\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}
binary trees with
n
leaves,
i.e.
the
(n-1)
-th Catalan number. Moreover, each binary tree with
n
leaves has
n-1
nodes.
Since
T
is a critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree, we immediately get the first equality in (
3.5
). The second one is then deduced by a standard application of Stirling’s formula.
The fact that
r(n)
is decreasing in
n
follows by a simple direct calculation.
∎

3.4
Decomposing
p
-signed uniform binary trees

Recall from
Section

2.1
that given a subset
\mathcal{L}
of the set of leaves of a sign-decorated binary tree
t
, we denote by
t|_{\mathcal{L}}
the sign-decorated binary subtree with
\#\mathcal{L}
leaves obtained from
t
by removing all vertices that do not have a descendant in
\mathcal{L}
and contracting all vertices of degree
2
that may appear.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
n\geq m\geq 1
and let
T_{n}
be a
p
-signed uniform binary tree with
n
leaves. Conditionally on
T_{n}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{m}=\{L_{1},\dots,L_{m}\}
be a uniform random subset of the set of leaves of
T_{n}
with cardinality
m
.
Then
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
has the same law as
T_{m}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence, for instance, of
Remark

3.2
.
∎

We also note that
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
has
m-1
nodes
which can be seen as nodes of the larger tree
T_{n}
. There is a natural correspondence between, on the one hand, the connected components of the complement of those vertices in
T_{n}
and, on the other hand, the edges of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
.
For any edge
e
of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
, we denote by
T_{n}^{e}
the corresponding connected component in
T_{n}
;
cf.

Figure

8
.

Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{2m-1}
be the edges of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
, labelled so that
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
are the edges incident to the leaves
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
. For all
i\in\{1,\dots,2m-1\}
, we set

N_{i}\coloneqq\text{the number of leaves of $T_{n}$ that belong to the region $T_{n}^{e_{i}}$},

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}_{i}\coloneqq N_{i}-1

\displaystyle\quad\text{ if }i\in\{1,\dots,m\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}_{i}\coloneqq N_{i}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ if }i\in\{m+1,\dots,2m-1\}.

That is,
\widetilde{N}_{i}
counts the number of leaves of
T_{n}
in each region, excluding the leaves
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
.

Figure 8:
A diagram for the notation introduced in
Section

3.4
.
The blue notation
refers to the proof of
4.2
.

Left:
The tree
T_{n}
, from which we select a subset of leaves
\mathcal{L}_{m}=\{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\}
and form the uncontracted tree
\text{u}(T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}})
highlighted in red.

Middle:
The tree
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
with the two leaves in the set
\mathcal{L}
corresponding to a maximal positive subtree marked by two blue arrows. To each edge
e
of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
corresponds a region
T^{e}_{n}
highlighted in green on the left part. We select a positive maximal subtree
\hat{T}^{e}_{n}
in any such region adjacent to the subset
\mathcal{L}
.

Right:
The corresponding maximal positive subtree.

We recall the definition of the
Dirichlet
distribution and of the
Dirichlet-multinomial
distribution, which will describe the law of the vector
\big(\widetilde{N}_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
n\geq 0,k\geq 1
be integers, let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}>0
and
\alpha_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}
.

•

The Dirichlet distribution
\mathrm{Dir}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}}\right)
is the law on
k
-tuples of non-negative real numbers
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
which sum up to
1
given by the probability density function

\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\cdots\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\prod_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}-1}.

•

The Dirichlet-multinomial distribution
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}}\right)
is the law on
k
-tuples of non-negative integers
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})
which sum up to
n
given by the probability mass function

\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{0})\Gamma(n+1)}{\Gamma(n+\alpha_{0})}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\Gamma(n_{i}+\alpha_{i})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})\Gamma(n_{i}+1)}.

In other words, a
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}}\right)
-distributed random variable
\mathbf{N}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{1},\dots,N_{k}}}\right)
can be obtained as follows: let
\mathbf{D}=(D_{1},\dots,D_{k})
follow the Dirichlet distribution
\mathrm{Dir}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}}\right)
; then, conditionally on
\mathbf{D}
, sample the vector
\mathbf{N}
as a multinomial vector with parameters
(n;\mathbf{D})
.

Note that, by the law of large numbers, we immediately obtain the following result, stated in a convenient form for later use.

Lemma 3.6
.

Fix an integer
m\geq 1
. Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{i})_{i\leq 2m-1}
be a
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2m-1}}}\right)
-distributed random vector. Then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N_{i}}{n}}}\right)_{i\leq 2m-1}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathrm{d}}(D^{m}_{i})_{i\leq 2m-1},

where
(D^{m}_{i})_{i\leq 2m-1}
is a Dirichlet random vector with parameter
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2m-1})
.

We are making explicit the dependence on
m
of the random variable
D^{m}_{i}
because later on we will use this result in the case when
(D^{m}_{i})_{i\leq 2m-1}
is a Dirichlet random vector with parameter
(\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2})
and the dependence on
m
will play a key role.

We also record a simple result about Dirichlet random variables.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(D_{1},\dots D_{n})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1/2,\dots,1/2)
. Then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{2}D_{1},\frac{n}{2}D_{2}}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathrm{d}}(G_{1},G_{2}),

where

(G_{1},G_{2})
is a pair of independent of
\mathrm{Gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
-distributed
random variables.

Proof.

Let
(G_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be independent
\mathrm{Gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
-distributed random variables. For any
n\geq 1
, we have the equality in distribution
[
Dev06
, Chapter XI.4]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}}\cdot(G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{n})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(D_{1},\dots D_{n}).

Since
\frac{n/2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}}
converges to
1
in probability by the law of large numbers, the claimed result follows from Slutzky’s lemma.
∎

We finally state our last preliminary result on the decomposition of
p
-signed uniform binary trees.

Lemma 3.8
.

Conditionally on
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
, the vector
\big(\widetilde{N}_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
follows the Dirichlet-multinomial distribution
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-m;\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
.
Moreover, conditionally on
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
and
(\widetilde{N}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
,
for any
i
with
1\leq i\leq m
, the region
T_{n}^{e_{i}}
corresponding to edge
e_{i}
(adjacent to the leaf
L_{i}
) has the same distribution as
T_{N_{i}}
, and the regions
(T_{n}^{e_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq m}
are jointly independent.

In particular, since the laws of
\big(\widetilde{N}_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
and
(T_{n}^{e_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq m}
conditionally on
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
do not depend on
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
, then
\big(\widetilde{N}_{i}\big)_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
and
(T_{n}^{e_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq m}
are independent of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
.

Proof.

The result follows, for instance, by combining the two facts stated in
[
Pit06
, Exercises 2.2.2 and 7.4.13]
.
∎

4
Existence of the exponent
\alpha
via supermultiplicativity

Recall that
X_{n}=\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
denotes the maximal size (number of leaves) of a positive subtree of the
p
-signed uniform binary tree
T_{n}
with
n
leaves. The main goal of this section is to prove
2.1
,
i.e.
the existence of the exponent
\alpha
.

The proof of
2.1
consists of a supermultiplicativity argument. We first state a result of Dekking and Grimmett
[
DG88
]
which, although not directly applicable to our setting, has a similar flavor.

Proposition 4.1

(
[
DG88
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
(Y_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random variables with values in the set of positive integers. We assume that for any
m,n\geq 1
, we have the stochastic domination

Y_{m+n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{Y_{m}}Y_{n}^{(i)},

(4.1)

where the
Y_{n}^{(i)}
