are i.i.d. copies of
Y_{n}
and are independent of
Y_{m}
. Let
\lambda=\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}]^{1/n}
. If
\lambda<+\infty
, then we have the convergence in probability

Y_{n}^{1/n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{\mathbb{P}}\lambda.

In
[
DG88
]
, processes satisfying (
4.1
) are called
superbranching processes
, by analogy with the branching property enjoyed by e.g. Bienaymé–Galton–Watson processes. Since we are interested in a quantity
X_{n}
with polynomial growth, it would be natural to try and obtain a superbranching equation of the form

X_{m\cdot n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{n}^{(i)}.

(4.2)

This would mean breaking a tree of size
m\cdot n
into
m
subtrees of size
n
. However, the most natural ways to decompose uniform binary trees into several regions result in regions with
random
and
different
sizes (recall
3.8
in
Section

3.4
), which is why our supermultiplicativity equation (
4.3
) will be more complicated than (
4.2
).

Recall the definition of the
Dirichlet-multinomial
distribution from
3.5
in
Section

3.4
.
We now state the superbranching-like equation that will be useful to us.

Lemma 4.2

(Supermultiplicativity equation for
X_{n}
)
.

For all
n\geq m\geq 1
, we have the stochastic domination

X_{n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{N_{i}}^{(i)},

(4.3)

where:

•

The vector
(N_{i}-\mathds{1}_{i\leq m})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
follows the
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-m;\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
distribution and is independent of
X_{m}
.

•

Conditionally on
(N_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
, the random variable
X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}
has the law of
X_{N_{i}}
for all
i\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},m\mathclose{\rrbracket}
and the

(X_{N_{i}}^{(i)})_{1\leq i\leq m}
are jointly independent and are independent of
X_{m}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Note that since
X_{m}\leq m
, the random variables
N_{i}
involved in (
4.3
) are all among the first
m
coordinates of the vector
(N_{i}-\mathds{1}_{i\leq m})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
. That is, when the indicator function is equal to 1.

Proof.

Let
n\geq m\geq 1
.
We will build a subtree of
T_{n}
with only
\oplus
nodes, whose total number of leaves will have the same distribution as the right-hand side of (
4.3
).

Conditionally on
T_{n}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{m}=\{L_{1},\dots,L_{m}\}
be a uniform random subset of the set of leaves of
T_{n}
with size
m
. We consider the subtree
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
as in
3.4
. Then
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
has the same law as
T_{m}
.

Recall the notation introduced above
3.8
and in
Figure

8
.
Then, thanks to
3.8
, the vector
(\widetilde{N}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}=(N_{i}-\mathds{1}_{i\leq m})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
follows the
\mathrm{DirMult}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-m;\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
distribution and is independent of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
. Moreover, conditionally on
(N_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
, the regions
T_{n}^{e_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq m
, corresponding to the edges
e_{i}
, are independent copies of
T_{N_{i}}
and are independent of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
.

Now consider the largest positive subtree of
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
(if there is more than one such maximal subtree, pick one arbitrarily), and denote by
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{L}_{m}=\{L_{1},\dots,L_{m}\}
the set of its leaves as on Figure
8
. Since
T_{n}|_{\mathcal{L}_{m}}
has the same law as
T_{m}
, the size of
\mathcal{L}
has the same law as
X_{m}=\mathrm{LIS}(T_{m})
. Moreover, for each
L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}
, consider a maximal positive subtree
\widehat{T}^{e_{i}}_{n}
of
T_{n}^{e_{i}}
.
Since
T_{n}^{e_{i}}
has the same law as
T_{N_{i}}
by
3.8
, the size
\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n}^{e_{i}})
of
\widehat{T}^{e_{i}}_{n}
has the same law as
X_{N_{i}}
.

The subtree of
T_{n}
induced by the leaves of
\bigcup_{L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}}\widehat{T}_{n}^{e_{i}}
has only
\oplus
nodes and, thanks to the previous paragraph, its number of leaves has the same distribution as
\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}
, where
N_{i}
and the
X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}
are as in the statement of the Lemma. This concludes the proof.
∎

With
4.2
in our hands, we can turn to the proof of
2.1
.

Proof of
2.1
.

For
n\geq 1
, we set
\alpha_{n}\coloneqq\frac{\log\mathbb{E}[X_{n}]}{\log n}
so that

\mathbb{E}[X_{n}]=n^{\alpha_{n}}.

Let
\alpha=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{n}
as in (
2.2
).
By Markov’s inequality, it is immediate that for any
\varepsilon>0
, the probability that
X_{n}\geq n^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
goes to
0
as
n\to\infty
.
Therefore, to prove (
2.1
), we only need to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\leq n^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}}\right)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(4.4)

Once we have this, the fact that
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
is an immediate consequence of
1.2
.
We divide the proof of (
4.4
) into five main steps.

Step 1:

Turning (
4.3
) into an equation closer to (
4.2
).
We first turn (
4.3
) into an equation closer to (
4.2
) in order to compare
X_{n}
with a branching process. This goal is reached in (
4.7
). We fix
m\leq n
. The precise choice of
m
will be decided towards the end of the proof in Step 4.

Roughly speaking,
3.6
ensures that the random variables
N_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 2m-1
appearing in (
4.3
) behave like
n\cdot D_{i}^{m}
, where
(D_{1}^{m},D_{2}^{m},\dots,D_{2m-1}^{m})\sim\mathrm{Dir}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2}}}\right)
, so that they are of order
\frac{n}{m}
and are random at that scale. In order to obtain a version of (
4.3
) with i.i.d. terms on the right-hand side, we will keep only the values of
i
for which
N_{i}>\frac{n}{m}
.

With the notation of
4.2
, we can write the stochastic domination

X_{n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{N_{i}\geq\frac{n}{m}\}}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{Y}_{m}}X_{\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\rfloor}^{(i)},

(4.5)

where

\widetilde{Y}_{m}=\widetilde{Y}_{m}(n)\overset{\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{N_{i}\geq\frac{n}{m}\}},

and the random variables
X_{\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\rfloor}^{(i)}
are independent copies of
X_{\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\rfloor}
, also independent of
\widetilde{Y}_{m}
. Note that the last domination in (
4.5
) is possible because
4.2
guarantees that, conditional on
(N_{i})
, the
X_{N_{i}}^{(i)}
are all independent and are also independent of
X_{m}
.

The domination in (
4.5
) is closer to (
4.2
) but we also need to replace
\widetilde{Y}_{m}(n)
with a random variable
Y_{m}
that does not depend on
n
.
By
3.6
, we have the convergence in distribution

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{N_{i}\geq\frac{n}{m}\}}\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{m}\}},

where
(D^{m}_{1},\dots D^{m}_{2m-1})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1/2,\dots,1/2)
and is independent of
X_{m}
.
We then introduce
8
8

8

Note the extra factor
2
compared to the previous display.

\displaystyle Y_{m}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{2}{m}\}},

(4.6)

so that for any
k\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},m\mathclose{\rrbracket}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{Y}_{m}(n)\geq k}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{N_{i}\geq\frac{n}{m}\}}\geq k}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{m}\}}\geq k}}\right)>\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{m}\geq k}}\right).

Now, because of the strict inequality in the last display, the probability
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{Y}_{m}(n)\geq k}}\right)
is eventually larger than
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{m}\geq k}}\right)
for all
k\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},m\mathclose{\rrbracket}
. Hence, for all
m\geq 1
, there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(m)>0
such that

\widetilde{Y}_{m}(n)\succeq Y_{m}
for
n\geq n_{0}
. Summarizing,

X_{n}\succeq\sum_{i=1}^{Y_{m}}X_{\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\rfloor}^{(i)}\qquad\text{for all $m\geq 1$ and $n\geq n_{0}(m)$},

(4.7)

where
Y_{m}
is as in (
4.6
). This domination is now closer to (
4.2
) as we want.

Step 2:

Lower bounding the expectation of
Y_{m}
.

Note that
Y_{m}
has expectation

\mathbb{E}[Y_{m}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{m}]\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right)=m^{\alpha_{m}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right),

(4.8)

since the vector
(D^{m}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2m-1}
is independent of
X_{m}
.
Using
3.7
, we can set

b\coloneqq\inf_{m\geq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right)}}\right\}>0,

(4.9)

which gives

\mathbb{E}[Y_{m}]\geq m^{\alpha_{m}}b\qquad\text{ for all }m\geq 1.

(4.10)

Step 3:

Lower bounding
X_{n}
by comparing it with an appropriate branching process.

Let
n_{0}=n_{0}(m)
be as in (
4.7
). By iterating (
4.7
), an induction on
k
shows that the process
(X_{n_{0}m^{k}})_{k\geq 0}
stochastically dominates a Bienaymé–Galton–Watson process
(Z_{k})_{k\geq 0}
started with one particle and with offspring distribution given by the law of
Y_{m}
. Therefore, setting

k=k(m,n)\coloneqq\Big\lfloor\frac{\log(n/n_{0})}{\log m}\Big\rfloor,

(4.11)

so that
n_{0}m^{k+1}>n\geq n_{0}m^{k}
, we get
X_{n}\succeq X_{n_{0}\cdot m^{k}}\succeq Z_{k}
.

We now claim that there exists a threshold
m_{0}>0
, such that for every
m\geq m_{0}
, there exist
c=c(m)>0
and
n^{\prime}_{0}=n^{\prime}_{0}(m)>0
such that for all
n\geq n^{\prime}_{0}(m)
:

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\geq c\cdot(m^{\alpha_{m}}b)^{k}}}\right)\geq c,

(4.12)

where
k=k(m,n)
is as in (
4.11
) (and so its value is fixed by
m,n
).
Indeed, since
X_{n}\succeq Z_{k}
, for any choice of the constant
c>0
, we can write

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\geq c\cdot(m^{\alpha_{m}}b)^{k}}}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{k}\geq c\cdot(m^{\alpha_{m}}b)^{k}}}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y_{m}}}\right])^{-k}Z_{k}\geq c}}\right),

where in the last equality we used that the offspring distribution
Y_{m}
of the branching process
(Z_{i})_{i\geq 1}
has expectation at least
m^{\alpha_{m}}b
thanks to (
4.10
).
By standard results (see for instance
[
KS66
]
),
(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y_{m}}}\right])^{-k}Z_{k}
converges almost surely as
k\to\infty
to some
Z_{\infty}(m)
, which is positive on the event of non-extinction. Now, since
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y_{m}}}\right]>1
for
m
large enough, there exists
m_{0}>0
such that the event of non-extinction has positive probability (which depends on
m
) for
m>m_{0}
.
Therefore, we can fix
c=c(m)>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{\infty}(m)\geq c}}\right)\geq 2c
.
This ensures that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y_{m}}}\right])^{-k}Z_{k}\geq c}}\right)\geq c
for large enough
k
(
i.e.
for
n\geq n^{\prime}_{0}(m)
), establishing (
4.12
).

In addition, when the event in (
4.12
) occurs, we have that

\displaystyle X_{n}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\geq\,\,\,c\cdot\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k(\alpha_{m}\log m+\log b)}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:defn_k}}}{{\geq}}c\cdot\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n/n_{0})}{\log m}-1}}\right)(\alpha_{m}\log m+\log b)}}\right),\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log(n/n_{0})}{\log m}(\alpha_{m}\log m+\log b)}}\right)}}\right\},

where the minimum accounts for the fact that
\alpha_{m}\log m+\log b
could
a priori
be negative. In any case,
this implies that when the event in (
4.12
) occurs, we have

\frac{\log X_{n}}{\log n}\geq\alpha_{m}+\frac{\log b}{\log m}+O_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\log n}}}\right).

(4.13)

Step 4:

Getting the right lower bound for
X_{n}
with positive probability.
We will now specify the value of
m
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Recalling that
b\in(0,1)
and
\alpha=\limsup_{m\to\infty}\alpha_{m}
, we choose
m
large enough so that
m\geq m_{0}
and

\frac{\log b}{\log m}\geq-\varepsilon/3\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha_{m}\geq\alpha-\varepsilon/3.

(4.14)

Now that
m
is fixed, (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) imply that there is a constant
c>0
such that for
n
large enough, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\log X_{n}}{\log n}\geq\alpha-\varepsilon}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\geq n^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}}\right)\geq c.

(4.15)

In order to prove (
4.4
), it remains to turn the probability in (
4.15
) from
c
to
1-\varepsilon
. This is the goal of the next (and last) step.

Step 5:

Getting the right lower bound for
X_{n}
with high probability.

The idea will be to use (
4.7
) again and argue that if each term on the right-hand side of (
4.7
) has a positive probability to be large (thanks to (
4.15
)), then the sum is very likely to be large, provided the number
Y_{m}
of terms is high enough.
For this, we first claim that we can fix
m^{\prime}>0
large enough so that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{m^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}}}\right)\leq\varepsilon,

(4.16)

where
c
is as in (
4.15
).
Indeed, recalling that
Y_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{X_{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{2}{m}\}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{m}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{m}\leq\sqrt{m}}}\right)+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{2}{m}\}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}}}\right).

The first term on the right-hand side tends to zero as
m
tends to infinity thanks to
1.2
, so we focus on the second term. Setting
S_{m}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{m}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D^{m}_{i}\geq\frac{2}{m}\}}
and noting that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}}}\right]\geq\lfloor\sqrt{m}\rfloor b
by definition of
b
in (
4.9
), we get that for
m
large enough,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{m}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}}}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{m}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon c\lfloor\sqrt{m}\rfloor b}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}}}\right]}}\right)\geq\Bigg(1-\frac{1}{\varepsilon c\lfloor\sqrt{m}\rfloor b}\Bigg)^{2}\,\,\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}}}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}^{2}}}\right]},

where the last inequality follows from the Paley–Zygmund inequality. The first factor on the right-hand side tends to one as
m
tends to infinity, so we are left to show that
\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}}}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}^{2}}}\right]}\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}1
. Note that, computing the two expectations, we have that

\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}}}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[S_{m}^{2}}}\right]}\geq\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{m}\rfloor^{2}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{m}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right)+m\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D^{m}_{1}\geq\frac{2}{m},D^{m}_{2}\geq\frac{2}{m}}}\right)}.

Thanks to
3.7
, we know that
(mD^{m}_{1},mD^{m}_{2})\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm{d}}(G_{1},G_{2})
,
where
(G_{1},G_{2})
is a pair of independent Gamma random variables of parameter 1/2. This implies that the right-hand side of the above equation tends to one as we wanted, completing the proof of (
4.16
).

With (
4.16
) in our hands we can now return to (
4.7
). For
n
large enough, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}<\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{m^{\prime}}}}\right)^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{m^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}}}\right)+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\forall 1\leq i\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon c},\,X^{(i)}_{\lfloor\frac{n}{m^{\prime}}\rfloor}<\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{m^{\prime}}}}\right)^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-c}}\right)^{1/\varepsilon c}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\exp(-\varepsilon^{-1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon,

where in the second inequality we used (
4.16
) and (
4.15
) with
\frac{n}{m^{\prime}}
in place of
n
. This proves (
4.4
).
∎

5
Rough regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q

Recall the definition of the sequences
q
and
Q
from (
2.5
), and note
from
Section

2.4
that one of the main objectives of this paper is to establish the good regularity estimates (
2.12
) for these sequences. This goal will be accomplished in
7.1
. As explained in
Section

2.4
(see also the diagram in
Figure

6
), the proof of
7.1
relies crucially on the local convergence result established in
Section

6
. To obtain this result, we now prove the following
a priori
rough regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q
.

Proposition 5.1

(Rough regularity estimates)
.

We have

q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+o(1)}\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty,

where
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
is as in
2.1
. Moreover, there is a constant
C>0
such that for any real
x\geq 1
, we have

(1+o(1))\,x^{-C}\leq\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}\leq 1\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty,

(5.1)

and, for any
k\geq 1
,

C\,x^{-C}\leq\frac{q(xk)}{q(k)}\leq C,

(5.2)

where we are using the conventions
Q(z)=Q(\lceil z\rceil)
and
q(z)=q(\lceil z\rceil)
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as in
Section

3.1
.

The proof relies on the coupling of the decorated trees
T_{n}
between various values of
n
obtained through Rémy’s algorithm introduced in
Section

2.1
. Recall that one can run Rémy’s algorithm both forwards and backwards.
For
n\geq 0
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{n}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
(T_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
and by
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
(T_{i})_{i\geq n}
.

We recall that we set
X_{n}=\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
. Note that under this coupling, for all
n\geq 1
, we have
X_{n+1}-1\leq X_{n}\leq X_{n+1}
, and
1.2
guarantees that
X_{n}\to+\infty
in probability. Therefore, for all
k\geq 1
, the stopping time

\tau_{k}\coloneqq\min\{n\geq 1\mid X_{n}=k\}

(5.3)

is almost surely finite (note that we also have the trivial a.s. bound
\tau_{k}\geq k
).
In particular, let
\alpha
be given by
2.1
. Since
X_{n}
is non-decreasing in
n
, for all
\varepsilon>0
and
k\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}>k^{1/\alpha+\varepsilon}}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{\lfloor k^{1/\alpha+\varepsilon}\rfloor}<k}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq k^{1/\alpha-\varepsilon}}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{\lfloor k^{1/\alpha-\varepsilon}\rfloor}\geq k}}\right).

(5.4)

Hence,
2.1
implies
\tau_{k}=k^{1/\alpha+o(1)}
in probability.
We start by making this statement a bit more precise, which will be useful later. We will rely on the description of the exponent
\alpha
as a limsup, as we established in (
2.2
) of
2.1
.

Lemma 5.2

(Control on the negative moments of
\tau_{k}
)
.

Let
0<\xi<\alpha
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-\xi}}}\right]=k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}+o(1)}\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(5.5)

In particular, we have

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+o(1)}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right]=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]}}\right)\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(5.6)

Proof.

The lower bound for (
5.5
) is immediate using
\tau_{k}=k^{1/\alpha+o(1)}
in probability: for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-\xi}}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-\xi}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k}\leq k^{1/\alpha+\varepsilon}}}}\right]\geq k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}-\varepsilon\xi}\,\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}\leq k^{1/\alpha+\varepsilon}}}\right]\sim k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha}-\varepsilon\xi}\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

For the upper bound, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
k\geq 1
. We note that
\tau_{k}\geq k
, so
\tau_{k}^{-\xi}\leq k^{-\xi}
. For any
0<x\leq k^{-\xi}
, using Markov’s inequality and the description (
2.2
) of
\alpha
as a limsup, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}^{-\xi}\geq x}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq x^{-1/\xi}}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{\lfloor x^{-1/\xi}\rfloor}\geq k}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{\lfloor x^{-1/\xi}\rfloor}}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{k}\lfloor x^{-1/\xi}\rfloor^{\alpha+\varepsilon}

if
k
is large enough (which implies that
x^{-1/\xi}
is large enough). Note that this last inequality still holds if
x>k^{-\xi}
, because then the left-hand side is
0
.
By integrating over
x
, since
\frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{\xi}>1
, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-\xi}}}\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}^{-\xi}\geq x}}\right)\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}1\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}x^{-\frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{\xi}}}}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha+\epsilon}}}1\ \mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{k}\int_{k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha+\epsilon}}}^{\infty}x^{-\frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{\xi}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k^{-\frac{\xi}{\alpha+\varepsilon}}}}\right).

Since this is true for any
\varepsilon>0
, this proves (
5.5
) and the first claim in (
5.6
).

For the second claim in (
5.6
), we fix
\varepsilon>0
small enough to have
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon<\alpha
, which is possible because
\alpha>1/2
by
2.1
.
We write, using the fact that
\tau_{k}\geq k
deterministically,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right]=\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{k}{\tau_{k}}\cdot\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{\varepsilon}\cdot\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]=k^{\varepsilon-1}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2-\varepsilon}}}\right].

Using (
5.5
) with
\xi=\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, we get
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right]\leq k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}}\right)\varepsilon+o(1)}
, whereas
\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}
again by (
5.5
). This proves (
5.6
).
∎

5.1
will be an easy consequence of the estimates in the last
5.2
and the following result, which relates the sequences
q
and
Q
to the stopping times
\tau_{k}
in order to compare them.

Lemma 5.3

(Relation between
q
,
Q
and the stopping times
\tau_{k}
)
.

We have

Q(k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty,

(5.7)

and

(1+o(1))\,\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]\leq q(k)\leq(1+o(1))\,\frac{1}{2p\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(5.8)

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{C}\,\frac{Q(k)}{k}\leq q(k)\leq C\,\frac{Q(k)}{k}.

(5.9)

Proof.

We divide the proof into five main steps.

Step 1:

Proof of (
5.7
).
For this, recall that we set
r(n)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=n}}\right)
for all
n\geq 1
and, from
3.3
, we know that

r(n)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}n^{-3/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}}}\right)}}\right)\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

(5.10)

and
r(n)
is decreasing in
n
.
Therefore, summing over all possible values of
|T|
and noting that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\geq k}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq n}}\right)
, we can write as
k\to\infty
:

Q(k)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)\geq k}}\right)=\sum_{n\geq 1}r(n)\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}\geq k}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}r(n)}}\right]=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}}}\right)}}\right)n^{-3/2}}}\right]\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right]}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]}}\right),

where the last equality follows from the second item of (
5.6
) in
5.2
. Thus (
5.7
) holds.

Step 2:

Preparation for the proof of (
5.8
).

The key idea will be to use Rémy’s algorithm forwards for the upper bound and backwards for the lower bound.
As above, we first write

\displaystyle q(k)=\sum_{n\geq 1}r(n)\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{n}=k}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}n^{-3/2}}}\right],

(5.11)

where for the last equality we used (
5.10
) and that
\tau_{k}\geq k
. Bounding every term of the sum by either its largest or smallest term yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k+1}^{3/2}}}}\right]\leq q(k)\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k}^{3/2}}}}\right].

(5.12)

Step 3:

Proof of the upper bound of (
5.8
).

Let
n\geq k\geq 1
, and let us condition on
X_{n}=k
. Recalling the terminology introduced at the beginning of
Section

2.1
, let
T_{n}^{\max}
be a positive uncontracted subtree of
T_{n}
with
k
leaves (if it is not unique, pick one arbitrarily). Let also
E_{n}
be the edge of
T_{n}
that is picked by the Rémy algorithm run forwards at step
n
. Note that
T_{n}^{\max}
is a tree with
k
leaves and vertex degrees
1
,
2
or
3
, so it has at least
2k-1
edges (this would be an equality if we considered the contracted version of
T_{n}^{\max}
since all the vertices would have degree
1
or
3
). If the edge
E_{n}
picked by the Rémy algorithm belongs to
T_{n}^{\max}
and
S_{n}=\oplus
, then the union of
T^{\max}_{n}
and the new leaf
L_{n+1}
forms a positive subtree of size
n+1
, so
\tau_{k+1}=n+1
. It follows that on the event
\{X_{n}=k\}
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n+1\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{n}}}\right)\geq p\frac{2k-1}{2n-1}\geq p\frac{k-1}{n},

(5.13)

since
\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=\oplus)=p
.

From here, we will obtain the upper bound of (
5.8
) by comparing the increment
\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
with a geometric random variable of parameter
p\frac{k}{\tau_{k}}
conditionally on
\tau_{k}
. More precisely, by an immediate induction, for all
j\geq 0
, we obtain from (
5.13
) that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>\tau_{k}+j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)\leq\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-p\frac{k-1}{\tau_{k}+i}}}\right)\leq\exp\bigg(-p(k-1)\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\frac{1}{\tau_{k}+i}\bigg),

where, when
j=0
, we interpret the empty product as
1
.
Moreover, since

\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\frac{1}{\tau_{k}+i}\geq\int_{\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k}+j}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{x}=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{j}{\tau_{k}}}}\right),

we deduce that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>\tau_{k}+j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)\leq\exp\bigg(-p(k-1)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{j}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)\bigg)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{j}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}.

(5.14)

Therefore, we have for
k
large enough so that
p(k-1)>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right]=\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>\tau_{k}+j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)\leq\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{j}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{t}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\frac{\tau_{k}}{pk}+1,

where the constant in the
O
notation is deterministic.
This entails that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k}^{3/2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\frac{1}{pk}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right].

(5.15)

Plugging this back into
(
5.12
), we obtain

q(k)\leq\frac{1}{2p\sqrt{\pi}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-3/2}}}\right]}}\right).

Finally, the last error term is negligible by the right-hand side of (
5.6
) in
5.2
, which proves the upper bound in (
5.8
).

Step 4:

Proof of the lower bound of (
5.8
).

The argument will be similar to the upper bound, but this time we will rely on the lower bound of (
5.12
) and use the Rémy algorithm run backwards.
More precisely, let
n\geq k\geq 2
, and let us condition on
X_{n}=k
, so that
\tau_{k}\leq n
.
We recall from
Section

2.1
that, conditionally on
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, the decorated tree
T_{n-1}
is obtained by removing a uniform leaf
L_{n}
of
T_{n}
.
As before, let
T_{n}^{\max}
be a positive uncontracted subtree of
T_{n}
with maximal size,
i.e.
with
k
leaves.
If
L_{n}\notin T_{n}^{\max}
(which occurs with probability
1-\frac{k}{n}
), then
T_{n}^{\max}\subset T_{n-1}
, which implies
X_{n-1}=X_{n}=k
, and so
\tau_{k}<n
. Therefore, on the event
\{X_{n}=k\}
, we have that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L_{n}\notin T_{n}^{\max}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)=1-\frac{k}{n}.

(5.16)

We refer to the end of the present section for a discussion on why this last inequality is not an equality, and why this matters a lot for the rest of the paper.
For all
0\leq j\leq\tau_{k+1}-k
, we get by induction from (
5.16
) that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<\tau_{k+1}-j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right)\geq\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}-i}}}\right)\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}-j}}}\right)^{j},

(5.17)

where, when
j=0
, we interpret the empty product as
1
.
Our upper bound argument (see (
5.14
)) suggests that the right order of magnitude for
j
is
\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}
. Therefore, we now fix a (large) constant
K>0
, and assume
k>2K
.
By summing over
j
, we find that on the event
\{K\tau_{k+1}/k\leq\tau_{k+1}-k\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<\tau_{k+1}-j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}-\frac{K}{k}\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{K}{k}}}\right)\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{k}{(1-K/k)\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{K}{k}}}\right)\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-K}}}\right),

where the last line just used that
1-K/k\leq 1
and
\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor+1\geq K\tau_{k+1}/k
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k+1}^{3/2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]\,\tau_{k+1}^{-3/2}\,\mathds{1}_{\{K\tau_{k+1}/k\leq\tau_{k+1}-k}\}}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{K}{k}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-K}}}\right)\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}\mathds{1}_{\{K\tau_{k+1}/k\leq\tau_{k+1}-k}\}}}\right].

(5.18)

To conclude, we argue that removing the indicator only costs a factor
1+o(1)
. If the event in the indicator does not occur, then
\tau_{k+1}<\frac{k}{1-K/k}\leq 2k
. Hence, using this with the trivial bound
\tau_{k+1}\geq k+1
, and then Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}\mathds{1}_{\{K\tau_{k+1}/k>\tau_{k+1}-k}\}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(k+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}<2k}}\right)

\displaystyle=(k+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2k-1}\geq k+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(k+1)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{2k-1}}}\right]=k^{-\frac{3}{2}+\alpha+o(1)},

by using (
2.2
) for the last inequality. On the other hand, by
5.2
, we have
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+o(1)}
. Moreover, by
2.1
, we have
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, which implies
-\frac{3}{2}+\alpha<-\frac{1}{2\alpha}
. Therefore, we have

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}\mathds{1}_{\{K\tau_{k+1}/k\leq\tau_{k+1}-k}\}}}\right]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+o(1)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right],

so (
5.18
) becomes

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k+1}^{3/2}}}}\right]\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{K}{k}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-K}}}\right)(1+o(1))\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]

as
k\to\infty
. Since the above inequality is true for any constant
K>0
, combined with the lower bound of (
5.12
), this proves the lower bound of (
5.8
).

Step 5:

Proof of (
5.9
).

The upper bound on
q
from (
5.8
) implies
q(k)=o(Q(k))
. Therefore
Q(k+1)=Q(k)-q(k)=(1+o(1))Q(k)
and again by (
5.8
),

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]=(1+o(1))\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\qquad\text{as $k\to\infty$.}

(5.19)

Hence, we may replace
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]
by
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]
in the lower bound on
q
of
5.3
. Therefore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\frac{1}{C}\frac{Q(k)}{k}\leq q(k)\leq C\frac{Q(k)}{k}
for all
k
, as we wanted.
∎

We can now complete the proof of
5.1
.

Proof of
5.1
.

The estimates
q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}
and
Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+o(1)}
are immediate by combining
5.3
with
5.2
. Moreover, by
5.3
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
k
, we have

\frac{Q(k+1)}{Q(k)}=1-\frac{q(k)}{Q(k)}\geq 1-\frac{C}{k}.

(5.20)

The estimate on
Q
then follows by writing a telescopic product: for all
x\geq 1
,

\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}\geq\prod_{i=k}^{xk-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{C}{i}}}\right)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sum_{i=k}^{xk-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{C}{i}+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{i^{2}}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\\
\geq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-C\log\frac{xk}{k}+O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)=(1+o(1))\,x^{-C}.

Finally, it remains to prove the estimate (
5.2
) on
q
. By (
5.9
) in
5.3
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\frac{1}{C}\frac{Q(k)}{k}\leq q(k)\leq C\frac{Q(k)}{k}
for all
k
,
so we can deduce the estimate on
q
from that on
Q
.
∎

What is still missing?
As can be seen in this last proof, understanding the polynomial decay of
Q
and
q
is closely related to understanding the limit of the ratio
\frac{kq(k)}{Q(k)}
, which will be an important part of the rest of the paper. Note that
5.3
provides both an upper and a lower bound for this ratio, but neither is tight. Our strategy will be to turn the proof of the
lower
bound of
5.3
(Step 4 of the proof) into a more precise argument. We first notice that the “greedy” part of the argument is the discussion leading to (
5.16
), where we pick
one
specific maximal positive subtree
T_{n}^{\max}
. To avoid such a waste, we can notice that
\tau_{k}=n
if and only if the leaf
L_{n}
selected from
T_{n}
by the backward Rémy’s algorithm belongs to
all
the maximal positive subtrees of
T_{n}
. Therefore, on the event
\{X_{n}=k\}
, we may write

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)=1-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n})}{n},

(5.21)

or equivalently,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}=n\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)=\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})}{n}\mathds{1}_{X_{n}=k},

(5.22)

where we recall that
\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(t)
stands for the set of leaves in the intersection of
all
positive subtrees of
t
with maximal size. In particular,
Section

7.1
will be devoted to proving that the ratio
\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n})}{X_{n}}
is concentrated around a certain – non-explicit for now – deterministic constant
\lambda
(this is the law of large numbers in
7.2
). The constant
\lambda
is exactly the multiplicative constant by which the lower bound in (
5.8
) of
5.3
is off. The proof of the law of large numbers will rely on the local convergence of a maximal positive subtree seen from a uniformly chosen leaf, resulting from
Section

6
. Finally, in
Section

7.2
, equipped with this concentration result, we will run again the proof of
5.1
in a more precise way to prove
7.1
. We stress that the proof of the law of large numbers will not provide by itself an explicit value for the constant
\lambda
. However, once
7.1
is proved, we will be able to argue
a posteriori
that
\lambda=\alpha
; see
Remark

7.9
.

Note.
The proofs of the local convergence and the law of large numbers presented in
Section

6
and
Section

7.1
are both intricate and rich in details. A reader encountering these sections for the first time may prefer to initially skip ahead, assuming the validity of the law of large numbers stated in
7.2
, and proceed directly to the proof of
7.1
in
Section

7.2
. Following this approach also has the additional benefit of immediately seeing how the proof of
5.1
lays the ground for the more refined proof of
7.1
.
The reader is then encouraged to visit
Section

6
and
Section

7.1
, as these sections offer deep insights into the landscape around a uniformly chosen leaf in a maximal positive subtree.

6
The local convergence of the leftmost maximal positive subtree of
T^{k}
around a uniformly chosen leaf

In the previous section, we proved the rough regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q
stated in
5.1
.
Along the way, we realized that the problem of finding sharper estimates was closely related to the existence of the local limit of a maximal positive subtree. We have all freedom to decide which maximal positive subtree to consider: to fix ideas, we will look at the
leftmost
maximal subtree, which is defined by going left at each negative node carrying two subtrees with equal
\mathrm{LIS}
(see
Section

6.1.1
for a precise definition).
The main goal of this section is to establish this local convergence (
6.2
) for such leftmost maximal positive subtree around a uniformly chosen leaf. It turns out that the most natural model to state this local convergence result is the
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson model
T
conditioned on
\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k
, denoted by
T^{k}
, in the regime where
k\to\infty
.
This result will be later used in
Section

7
to improve the rough regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q
; completing the proof of
7.1
.
The proof of the local convergence hinges upon a key coupling argument which forms the bulk of this section, and essentially enables us to compare the situations when
\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k
and when
\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k^{\prime}
.

This section is structured as follows. In
Section

6.1
we build the ground to state our local convergence result (
6.2
) by introducing a Markov chain that records the behavior of
T^{k}
and its leftmost maximal positive subtree along the spine from the root to the chosen leaf.
Section

6.2
is devoted to certain preliminary computations on this Markov chain that will be later used in
Section

6.3
to run the main coupling argument.

Note.
All the subtrees considered in
Section

6
are uncontracted (see
Section

2.1
).

6.1
Preliminary results and statement of the local convergence

The main goal of this section is to state our local convergence result (
6.2
) in
Section

6.1.2
. We first introduce some preliminary notation and results in
Section

6.1.1
.

6.1.1
Markovian description of the tree
T^{k}
around a uniformly chosen leaf of the leftmost maximal positive subtree

For any finite sign-decorated binary tree
t
, we denote by
t^{\lmax}
the
leftmost maximal positive subtree
of
t
. More precisely,
t^{\lmax}
can be constructed recursively in the following way (
cf.

Figure

9
):

•

If
t
consists of a single leaf, then
t^{\lmax}=t
.

•

If
t
consists of a root vertex connected to two trees
t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}
on the left and
t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
on the right, then:

–

If the root of
t
is decorated with a
\oplus
sign, then
t^{\lmax}
consists of a
\oplus
node connected to the two trees
t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}^{\lmax}
and
t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}^{\lmax}
.

–

If the root of
t
is decorated with a
\ominus
sign and
\mathrm{LIS}(t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})\geq\mathrm{LIS}(t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})
, then
t^{\lmax}=t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}^{\lmax}
.

–

If the root of
t
is decorated with a
\ominus
sign and
\mathrm{LIS}(t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})<\mathrm{LIS}(t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})
, then
t^{\lmax}=t_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}^{\lmax}
.

Recall that
T^{k}
denotes the
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
conditioned on the event
\{\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k\}
.
Conditionally on
T^{k}
, let
L^{k}
be a leaf chosen uniformly at random from the
k
leaves of the leftmost maximal positive subtree

T^{k,\lmax}\coloneqq(T^{k})^{\lmax}.

(6.1)

We first prove that the exploration of
T^{k}
along the spine directed towards
L^{k}
satisfies a nice Markov property.
We denote by
\rho_{k}
the root of
T^{k}
and by
T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}
and
T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
the left and right subtrees of
\rho_{k}
in
T^{k}
, respectively.
We also denote by
D\in\{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}},\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}\}
the direction such that
L^{k}\in T^{k}_{D}
. For the unconditioned
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
, we analogously define the root
\rho
and the left and right subtrees
T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}
and
T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
when
\deg(\rho)=3
.

Lemma 6.1

(Decomposition of
T^{k}
at the root)
.

Let
k\geq 1
. Then the law of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}),\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}),D}}\right)
is described as follows:

1.

With probability
\frac{1}{2q(1)}\mathds{1}_{k=1}
, the tree
T^{k}
consists of a single vertex, which is a leaf.

2.

For all
1\leq i<k
, with probability
\frac{1}{2}(1-p)q(i)
, the root
\rho_{k}
is negative,
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i
and
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k
.
Moreover, in this case
D=\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}
.

3.

For all
1\leq i\leq k
, with probability
\frac{1}{2}(1-p)q(i)
, the root
\rho_{k}
is negative,
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=k
and
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=i
. Moreover, in this case
D=\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}
.

4.

For all
1\leq i\leq k-1
, with probability
\frac{p}{2}\frac{q(i)q(k-i)}{q(k)}
, the root
\rho_{k}
is positive,
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i
and
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k-i
. Moreover, conditionally on this, the direction
D
is

\leftarrow

with probability
\frac{i}{k}
and

\rightarrow

with probability
\frac{k-i}{k}
.

Moreover, conditionally on
\{(\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}),\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}),D)=(k_{1},k_{2},d)\}
, the trees
T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}
and
T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
are independent trees with respective laws
T^{k_{1}}
and
T^{k_{2}}
, and
L^{k}
is a uniform leaf of
(T^{k}_{d})^{\lmax}
.

Proof.

The first claim simply follows from

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T^{k}=\{\rho_{k}\}}}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T=\{\rho\},\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}=\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T=\{\rho\}}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}\mathds{1}_{k=1}=\frac{1}{2q(1)}\mathds{1}_{k=1}.

For the second claim, fix
1\leq i<k
. We can rewrite
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s(\rho_{k})=\ominus,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k}}\right)
as

\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\deg(\rho)=2,s(\rho)=\ominus,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k,\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}\\
=\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\deg(\rho)=2,s(\rho)=\ominus,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k}}\right)}{q(k)}.

By the branching property at the root vertex
\rho
of
T
, we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s(\rho_{k})=\ominus,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}(1-p)q(i).

Moreover, on the above event, we have
D=\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}
since
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})>\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})
, so
L^{k}
must be in
T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
.

The proof of the third claim is the same as the second one. Note that in this case
D=\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}
since
\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})\geq\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})
and we are considering the leftmost maximal subtree as defined at the beginning of
Section

6.1.1
.

For the fourth claim, fix
1\leq i\leq k-1
so that in particular
k-1\geq 1
. We rewrite the probability
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s(\rho_{k})=\oplus,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T^{k}_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k-i}}\right)
as

\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\deg(\rho)=2,s(\rho)=\oplus,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})=i,\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k-i,\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)}=\frac{p}{2}\frac{q(i)q(k-i)}{q(k)},

where for the equality we used that
\mathrm{LIS}(T)=\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}})+\mathrm{LIS}(T_{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}})=k
is implied by all the other conditions. The claim about the conditional distribution of
D
immediately follows.

The very last claim about the conditional independence and the conditional distribution of
L^{k}
is immediate.
∎

By iterating this Markov property, we can introduce a Markov chain that describes the law of the exploration of
T^{k}
and its leftmost maximal positive subtree along the spine directed towards
L^{k}
.

t
with two maximal positive subtrees highlighted in red and blue. The leftmost maximal positive subtree
t^{\lmax}
is the red tree. The leaves in
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(t)
are highlighted in green.

6.1.2
Description of the Markov chain and statement of the local convergence

For every
h\geq 0
, consider the vertex

in the tree

which is the ancestor of the marked leaf

at height
h\geq 0
;

.
In particular,

is the root of the tree

and, denoting by

the height of

in

, we have that
v_{\eta-1}=L^{k}

and we can interpret

as a fictitious vertex lying above

playing the role of a

. Importantly, we consider
v_{\eta-1}

.

Given the sign-decorated binary tree

, we introduce

9

9

.
the process

,

(Z^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}\coloneqq(S_{h},D_{h},M^{*}_{h},W_{h})_{h\geq 0},

(6.2)

with values in
\{\ominus,\oplus\}\times\{{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}\}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
, defined as follows (and illustrated on

):

1.

Let
(S_{0},D_{0},M^{*}_{0},W_{0})=(\ominus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},k,0)
, by convention.

2.

For any
h\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\eta-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
:

•

S_{h}
is the
sign
in
\{\ominus,\oplus\}
of
v_{h-1}
.

•

is the
direction
in
\{{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}\}
indicating whether
v_{h}
is the left or the right child of
v_{h-1}
.

•

M^{*}_{h}
is the number of leaves of
T^{k,\lmax}

weakly
10
10

10

Note that the

condition is relevant only when

so that
v_{\eta-1}
is a leaf,
i.e.
at the very end of the process.
above
v_{h}
(the size of the

,

(we use the letter

.

3.

We use the convention that the chain is sent at time

to some

(6.3)

and stays there afterwards.
Note that defining the chain in this way allows us to write

\eta=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{h\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{h}=0}}\right\}.

Figure 10:

. The spine from the root to the marked leaf

\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}
and the decreasing Markov chain

on the right will be introduced in (

) and (
6.9
).

We will denote by
S
the process
(S_{h})_{h\geq 0}
in the first component of
Z^{k}
.
Similarly, we denote by
D
,
M^{*}
, and
W
the processes in the other components of
Z^{k}
. Since
Z^{k}
is sent to the absorbing cemetery state
\dagger
from time
\eta
onward, we shall restrict the study of
Z^{k}
to that of
(Z^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
.

It follows from
6.1
that
Z^{k}
is a Markov chain, whose transition probabilities will be given in
Section

6.2
.
Recall that
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(t)
denotes the set of leaves of
t
contained in all the maximal positive subtrees of
t
.
Note that the only way for
T^{k}
to have maximal positive subtrees that do not contain
L^{k}
is to find some
h\geq 1
such that the node
v_{h-1}
is negative and
M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}
. Hence, we can express the event that
L^{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
as a function of the trajectory of the chain, that is,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{L^{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}}\right\}=\Bigg\{\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}\}=0\Bigg\}.

(6.4)

This chain will be used later in
7.2
to establish, through (
6.4
) and the local convergence in
6.2
, that

\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}\to\lambda>0

in probability as
k
tends to infinity. This is why we consider this four-component Markov chain.

We want to prove the convergence of the process
Z^{k}
defined in (
6.2
) “seen from the end”,
i.e.
of the time-reversal of
Z^{k}
, because our interest lies in the behavior of
Z^{k}
near the chosen leaf
L^{k}
.

Theorem 6.2

(Local convergence of the leftmost maximal positive subtree around a uniformly chosen leaf)
.

The process
(Z^{k}_{\eta-h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
converges in distribution as
k\to\infty
to a limiting process
(\overset{\leftarrow}{Z}_{h})_{h\geq 0}
, in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions.

The proof of the theorem above will mainly rely on a coupling argument; see
6.6
and
6.7
.

We conclude this section by explaining why
6.2
can be interpreted as a local convergence result. Indeed, one can deduce
11
11

11

This proof is standard and omitted, as it is not strictly necessary for our purposes.
from
6.2
that the triplet
(T^{k},T^{k,\lmax},L^{k})
, viewed as a tree
T^{k}
marked at the leaf
L^{k}
and equipped with the distinguished subtree
T^{k,\lmax}
, converges in the Benjamini–Schramm sense to a triplet

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T^{\infty},T^{\infty,\lmax},L^{\infty}}}\right).

This limit consists of an infinite tree
T^{\infty}
with a marked leaf
L^{\infty}
and a distinguished infinite “leftmost maximal” positive subtree
T^{\infty,\lmax}
. In particular, the triplet
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T^{\infty},T^{\infty,\lmax},L^{\infty}}}\right)
is constructed as follows:

•

Start by letting
L^{\infty}
be the marked leaf, with an infinite spine below it. Set
u_{0}=L^{\infty}
and for all
h\geq 1
, let
u_{h}
be the
h
-th vertex below
L^{\infty}
along this infinite spine. The infinite tree
T^{\infty}
should be regarded as rooted at the bottom of this spine, in line with our usual convention of rooting trees at their bottom vertex.

•

Then, for all
h\geq 1
, conditioning on the value
\overset{\leftarrow}{Z}_{h}=(\overset{\leftarrow}{S}_{h},\overset{\leftarrow}{D}_{h},\overset{\leftarrow}{M^{*}_{h}},\overset{\leftarrow}{W}_{h})
:

–

Assign the sign
\overset{\leftarrow}{S}_{h}
to
u_{h}
.

–

Attach an independent tree
T^{\overset{\leftarrow}{W}_{h}}
to
u_{h}
on the left
12
12

12

Here left and right refer to the usual notions for the plane where the infinite spine is drawn.
(resp. right) side of the infinite spine
if
\overset{\leftarrow}{D}_{h}={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}
(resp. if
\overset{\leftarrow}{D}_{h}={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}
).

•

Let
T^{\infty}
be the tree obtained through the procedure above regarded as rooted at the bottom of this spine and with the marked leaf
L^{\infty}
.

•

Finally,
T^{\infty,\lmax}
is the union of the spine
\{u_{h},\,h\geq 0\}
and of the trees
T^{\overset{\leftarrow}{W}_{h},\lmax}
for
h\geq 0
such that
\overset{\leftarrow}{S}_{h}=\oplus
. This is also an infinite tree regarded as rooted at the bottom of the spine.

6.2
Transition probabilities and the associated decreasing Markov chain

The first step in the proof of
6.2
is to compute the transition probabilities of the Markov chain
(Z^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
introduced in (
6.2
). This is done in
Section

6.2.1
. Then, in
Section

6.2.2
we will introduce a simpler (single-component) decreasing Markov chain
M
that is a reduced and time-changed version of
Z^{k}
.
The heart of
6.2
will then be to produce a coupling of
M
and
M^{\prime}
started from two values
k
and
k^{\prime}
, in which the two chains merge fast enough as
k,k^{\prime}\to\infty
. This goal is achieved in
Section

6.3
.

6.2.1
Transition probabilities of the Markov chain

Recall that the process
(Z^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
defined in (
6.2
) is a Markov chain.
Let

\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((s,d,m,w);(s^{\prime},d^{\prime},m^{\prime},w^{\prime})}}\right)

denote its transition probabilities. We start by noticing that by the very end of
6.1
, these transition probabilities do not depend on
s,d,w
.

We first determine these transition probabilities when
m\geq 2
. From
Items

2
and
3
of
6.1
, we have

\pi((*,*,m,*);(\ominus,d,m,w^{\prime}))=\frac{1-p}{2}q(w^{\prime})

(6.5)

whenever either
d={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},\,\,m\geq 2,\,\,1\leq w^{\prime}\leq m
, or
d={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}},\,\,m\geq 2,\,\,1\leq w^{\prime}<m
.
Additionally, from
Item

4
of
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\pi((*,*,m,*);(\oplus,d,m^{\prime},m-m^{\prime}))=\frac{p}{2}\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\frac{q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}{q(m)}\,\quad\text{if }d\in\{{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}\},\,\,m\geq 2,\,\,1\leq m^{\prime}\leq m-1.

(6.6)

Note that this indeed defines a Markov kernel, since

\displaystyle\sum_{(s^{\prime},d^{\prime},m^{\prime},w^{\prime})}\pi((*,*,m,*);(s^{\prime},d^{\prime},m^{\prime},w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}\frac{1-p}{2}q(m^{\prime})+\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m}\frac{1-p}{2}q(m^{\prime})+\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}\frac{p}{2}\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\frac{q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}{q(m)}+\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}\frac{p}{2}\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\frac{q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}{q(m)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(m)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-p)q(m)\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}q(m^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}(1-p)q(m)^{2}+\frac{p}{2}\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}\frac{m^{\prime}+m-m^{\prime}}{m}q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(m)}\cdot q(m)=1,

(6.7)

where for the second-to-last equality we used the recursive relation for
q(m)
introduced in
2.4
.

For
m=1
, note that
\pi((*,*,1,*);(\oplus,*,*,*))=0
. Moreover, from
Item

1
of
6.1
, we have probability

\pi((*,*,1,*);(\ominus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},0,0))=\frac{1}{2q(1)}

to jump to the cemetery state
(\oplus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},0,0)
, and complementary probability

\pi((*,*,1,*);(\ominus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},1,1))=1-\frac{1}{2q(1)}

to jump to
(\ominus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},1,1)
.
Since the transition probabilities only depend on the third coordinate
m
, most of the rest of this section will be devoted to the study of the Markov chain
M^{*}=(M^{*}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
.

6.2.2
The associated decreasing Markov chain

The Markov chain
M^{*}
is non-increasing, but it will be convenient for us to make it decreasing. For this,
we consider the stopping times
(\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}})_{t\geq 0}
, where
\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}=0
and for
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}\coloneqq\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{h>\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t-1$}}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{h}<M^{*}_{h-1}}}\right\}

(6.8)

is the
t
-th decreasing ladder epoch;
see the second column on the right-hand side of
Figure

10
for an example.
This means that either
S_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}=\oplus
, so that
v_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}-1}
is the
t
-th vertex along the spine from the root to
L^{k}
that carries a
\oplus
sign, or that we reached the fictitious vertex
v_{\eta}

and that
M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}=0
.
We let

\mathtt{T}\coloneqq\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}=0}}\right\}

be the total number of times that the chain
M^{*}
decreases during the process. Note that
\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}=\eta
.

From now on, we focus our attention on the decreasing Markov chain
M
defined by

(M_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}}}\right)_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}\,,

(6.9)

see the third column on the right-hand side of
Figure

10
for an example.
Indeed, from the transitions of the chain, we can easily check that, conditionally on the trajectory
(M_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}
, the distribution of the trajectory of the whole process
Z^{k}
is given as follows:

•

The waiting times
(\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t-1$}}})_{1\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}-1}
are independent geometric random variables with respective parameters
\theta(M_{t-1})
, where for
m\geq 2
(note that
M_{t-1}\geq 2
when
1\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}-1
),

\displaystyle\theta(m)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{1}=\oplus\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{0}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\{{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{388661}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{106742}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}\}}\pi((*,*,m,*);(\oplus,d^{\prime},m^{\prime},m-m^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=1-(1-p)\bigg(\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{m-1}q(m^{\prime})+\frac{q(m)}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=1-(1-p)\bigg(1-Q(m)+\frac{Q(m)-Q(m+1)}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=p+(1-p)\frac{Q(m)+Q(m+1)}{2},

(6.10)

where in the third equality we used (
6.6
) and the fact that we know that the second line of (
6.2.1
) equals one.
Furthermore, the final waiting time
\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathtt{T}-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{T}-1$}}}
is another independent geometric random variable, but with parameter
\theta(1)=\frac{1}{2q(1)}
.

•

For any
t\in\mathopen{\llbracket}0\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\mathtt{T}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
and
h\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t+1$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
,
we have

M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+h}=M_{t}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad S_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+h}=\ominus.

(6.11)

Moreover, conditionally on
(M_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}
and
(\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}})_{0\leq t\leq{\mathtt{T}}}
, the random variables
(D_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+{h}},W_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+{h}})
are independent for all
t\in\mathopen{\llbracket}0\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\mathtt{T}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
and
{h}\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t+1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t+1$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
. Their conditional distribution is given as follows:
for
d={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},1\leq w\leq m
, or for
d={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}},1\leq w<m
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+{h}}=d,W_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}+{h}}=w\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}{M_{t}}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{1}=d,W_{1}=w\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}S_{1}=\ominus,{M^{*}_{0}}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((*,*,m,*);(\ominus,d,m,w)}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{1}=\ominus\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}{M^{*}_{0}}=m}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{q(w)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{Q(m)+Q(m+1)}{2}}}\right)},

(6.12)

where for the last equality we used (
6.5
) and (
•
‣
6.2.2
).

•

Finally, for all
t\in\mathopen{\llbracket}0\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\mathtt{T}-1\mathclose{\rrbracket}
, we have
S_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}=\oplus
,

W_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}={M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}-1}-M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:between_wait_times}}}{{=}}M_{t-1}-M_{t}},

and

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}S,{M^{*}},W}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t$}}}}={\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{91730}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\rightarrow$}}}}}}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}S,{M^{*}},W}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}.

(6.13)

We now compute the transition probabilities of the decreasing Markov chain
M
. By the branching property, we have

\pi(m;m^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{t+1}=m^{\prime}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M_{t}=m}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.64583pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}=m^{\prime}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{0}=m}}\right),

so
M
is a time-homogeneous Markov chain.
Moreover, the transition probabilities
\pi(\cdot\,;\,\cdot)
can be expressed, for all
m\geq 2
and
1\leq m^{\prime}<m
, as

\displaystyle\pi(m;m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\geq s,M^{*}_{s+1}=m^{\prime}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{0}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\bigg((1-p)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}q(j)+\frac{q(m)}{2}\bigg)\bigg)^{s}\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{s+1}=m^{\prime}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{0}=m,\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\geq s}}\right)\qquad\text{ by \eqref{eq:trans_Mtau}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\bigg((1-p)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}q(j)+\frac{q(m)}{2}\bigg)\bigg)^{s}\cdot\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{1}=m^{\prime}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{0}=m}}\right)\,\quad\text{ by the Markov property}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\bigg((1-p)\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}q(j)+\frac{q(m)}{2}\bigg)\bigg)^{s}\cdot p\cdot\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\cdot\frac{q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}{q(m)}\,\,\qquad\text{ by \eqref{eq:trans2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\frac{(1-p)}{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q(m)-\frac{q(m)}{2}}}\right)}\cdot\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\cdot\frac{q(m^{\prime})q(m-m^{\prime})}{q(m)}.

(6.14)

Moreover
\pi(1;0)=1
and the chain is killed at
0
.

Important discussion.
It can also be useful to write the transition probabilities in the following way:

\displaystyle\pi(m\,;\,m-w)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{t}-M_{t+1}=w\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M_{t}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\frac{(1-p)}{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Q(m)-\frac{q(m)}{2}}}\right)}\cdot\frac{m-w}{m}\cdot\frac{q(m-w)}{q(m)}\cdot q(w),

(6.15)

where it is reasonable to expect that all the factors except
q(w)
are close to
1
when
w
is fixed and
m
is large.
13
13

13

Equation (
5.2
) in
5.1
proves that
\frac{q(m-w)}{q(m)}
is bounded from above and below by positive constants, but not that it is close to
1
.

This suggests comparing the jumps of
M
with those of a decreasing random walk with step distribution
\mathcal{Q}
given by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q(i)}}\right)_{i\geq 1}
. Note from the rough regularity estimates in
5.1
that
q(i)=i^{-\gamma+o(1)}
as
i\to\infty
, with
\gamma\in(3/2,2)
as in (
2.7
). In particular, such a random walk has jumps with infinite mean.

Remark 6.3
.

The decreasing process
(M_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}
was defined in (
6.9
) as a time-change of
(M^{*}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
, which in turn is the third coordinate of the process
(Z^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
defined in (
6.2
).
We will use the notation
\mathbb{P}_{k}
and
\mathbb{E}_{k}
to denote the corresponding probability measures and expectations of the process
Z^{k}
.
In particular, by construction, under
\mathbb{P}_{k}
, the processes
(M_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\mathtt{T}}
and
(M^{*}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\eta}
are both started at
k
because
Z^{k}_{0}=(S_{0},D_{0},M^{*}_{0},W_{0})=(\ominus,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{\smash{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{357266}{131072}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\leftarrow$}}}}}}},k,0)
, by convention.

Remark 6.4
.

It can be useful to think of the set of values taken by the Markov chain
M
under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{k}
as a point process
\mathscr{P}^{k}=\{M_{t},\ t\geq 0\}
on
\mathbb{N}
. Indeed,
6.2
restricted to
M
may be recast as the (vague) convergence of
\mathscr{P}^{k}
, as
k\to\infty
, to a limiting point process
\mathscr{P}
.
A nice feature of
\mathscr{P}^{k}
is that conditioning on
\mathscr{P}^{k}\cap[m,\infty)
