\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{s}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}}\dots\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{s-1}^{\mathrm{c}}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{E}_{s}^{\mathrm{c}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{G}_{\ell^{s}}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1-\frac{c^{5}}{2}\bigg)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}}\dots\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{s-1}^{\mathrm{c}}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1-\frac{c^{5}}{2}\bigg)^{s}=\bigg(1-\frac{c^{5}}{2}\bigg)^{s(A,k)}.

This proves
Item

1
of
6.6
.

We now prove
Item

2
,
i.e.
the one about
\mathtt{T}-\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}
and
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}_{\mer}
, which will be a consequence of the first one. Recall from the statement of
6.6
that
\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}_{\mer}
are the respective first times when the two chains
M
and
M^{\prime}
visit
V_{\mer}
. By symmetry, we may focus on the chain
M
, say, and prove that
\mathtt{T}-\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}
goes to infinity in probability. For all
t>0
, since the chain
M
is decreasing, we have

\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathtt{T}-\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}<t}}\right)=\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}>\mathtt{T}-t}}\right)=\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}<M_{\mathtt{T}-t}}}\right).

We now introduce another parameter
A>0
, that we will tune later, and split the above display as follows:

\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathtt{T}-\mathfrak{t}_{\mer}<t}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}\leq A}}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}\leq A}}\right)+\sup_{\textsf{k}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A}}\right).

(6.55)

By
Item

1
of
6.6
, for any fixed
A>0
, the first term on the right-hand side goes to
0
as
k,k^{\prime}\to\infty
. It remains to prove that
\sup_{\mathsf{k}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A}}\right)\to 0
as
A\to\infty
.
To see this, we use a decomposition into blocks and
Item

1
of
6.14
.
Let
s\geq 1
, and take
A
large enough so that there are at least
s\cdot(t+1)
good scales
\ell
such that
\ell<A
.
We see
t+1
as the number of blocks, each containing
s
consecutive good scales. Then we note that, on the event
\{M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A\}
, the chain
M
only has at most
t
units of time to visit
(t+1)
blocks of good scales. As a consequence, on that event, at least one block of
s
good scales is not visited by
M
. By a union bound and iterating the bound of
Item

1
in
6.14
, this gives that for all
\mathsf{k}>A
,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A}}\right)\leq(t+1)(1-c)^{s}.

The above estimate is actually uniform over
\mathsf{k}\geq 1
since the probability is
0
if
\mathsf{k}\leq A
. This entails that
\sup_{\mathsf{k}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{\mathtt{T}-t}>A}}\right)\to 0
as
A\to\infty
, providing the missing estimate in (
6.55
), and hence
Item

2
.
∎

7
Good regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q

The main goal of this section is to prove the desired good regularity estimates.

Theorem 7.1

(Good regularity estimates:
q
and
Q
are regularly varying)
.

There exist two slowly varying functions
\varphi,\Phi
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\varphi(k)\qquad\text{and}\qquad Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\Phi(k),

where
\alpha\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2,1}}\right)
is as in
2.1
.

Recall that
T^{k}
denotes the
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
conditioned on the event that
\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k
. A key step is to first prove in
Section

7.1
the following law of large numbers for the number
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
of leaves contained in the intersection of all maximal positive subtrees of
T^{k}
.
This will rely on the local convergence in
6.2
(or, rather, on the good merging properties of the coupling in
6.7
).

Theorem 7.2

(Law of large numbers for
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
)
.

We have the convergence in probability

\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}\xlongrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\lambda\quad\text{as }k\to\infty,

where
\lambda
is a constant in
(0,1)
.

Building on this law of large numbers, we will then obtain in
Section

7.2
the good regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q
, proving
7.1
.

7.1
The size of the intersection of all maximal positive subtrees

The goal of this section is to prove the law of large numbers in
7.2
using first and second moment arguments. Controlling the latter moments is the purpose of the next two subsections. We will aim for rather precise estimates, as such refined control will prove useful later in
Section

9
for establishing the full scaling limit result of
2.3
.

7.1.1
The first moment

We begin with the first moment. Recall the Markov chain
(Z^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}=(S_{h},D_{h},M^{*}_{h},W_{h})_{h\geq 0}
introduced in (
6.2
). From (
6.4
), for a leaf
L^{k}
chosen uniformly at random among the
k
leaves of the leftmost maximal positive subtree
T^{k,\lmax}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{L^{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}}\right\}=\Bigg\{\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}\}=0\Bigg\}.

(7.1)

We also note that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(L^{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}}\right).

(7.2)

Therefore, proving the convergence of the first moment of
\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
will crucially rely on controlling the number of
forks

\displaystyle F\coloneqq\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}

(7.3)

along the spine towards the marked leaf
L^{k}
. We also introduce the version of
F
truncated below
A
:

F_{A}\coloneqq\sum_{m\geq A}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}.

(7.4)

The following claim says that
F
is
local
, in the sense that
F_{A}
is small when
A
is large, and so
F
is well-approximated by a random variable that only depends on the finite number of values taken by the non-increasing chain
M^{*}
before extinction. Recall from
Remark

6.3
that under
\mathbb{P}_{k}
, the chain
M^{*}
starts from
k
.

Lemma 7.3

(The number of forks is local)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
A\geq 1
,

\sup_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]\leq C\cdot Q(A),

(7.5)

where
Q(\cdot)
is as in (
2.5
).
Moreover, for all
k\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2q(1)}<1.

(7.6)

Proof.

We have for all
A\geq 2
and
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=A}^{\infty}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=A}^{\infty}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{h-1}=m}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{h}=\ominus,M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M^{*}_{h-1}=m}}\right)

since, on the event
\{S_{h}=\ominus,M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}
, we must have that
M^{*}_{h-1}=m
. By the Markov property and the expression of the transition probabilities (
6.5
), the above display boils down to

\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]=\sum_{m=A}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{h\geq 0}\mathds{1}\{M^{*}_{h}=m\}}}\right]\cdot\frac{1}{2}(1-p)q(m).

Now for fixed
m\geq 2
, conditionally on
M
ever hitting the value
m
, the random variable
\sum_{h\geq 0}\mathds{1}\{M^{*}_{h}=m\}
is geometric with parameter
\theta(m)=p+(1-p)\frac{Q(m)+Q(m+1)}{2}
as shown in (
•
‣
6.2.2
), and is
0
if
M
does not hit
m
. Hence, for all
m\geq 2
, we get the upper bound

\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{h\geq 0}\mathds{1}\{M^{*}_{h}=m\}}}\right]\leq\bigg(p+(1-p)\frac{Q(m)+Q(m+1)}{2}\bigg)^{-1}\leq C,

where
C
is a constant that is independent of
k
and
m
. The same argument carries over to the case
m=1
using that
\theta(1)=\frac{1}{2q(1)}
(see the discussion following (
•
‣
6.2.2
)).
As a result,

\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]\leq\frac{C}{2}(1-p)\sum_{m=A}^{\infty}q(m)=\frac{C}{2}(1-p)Q(A).

This bound is uniform in
k
, and we deduce (
7.5
).

To prove (
7.6
), we follow similar ideas. Fix
k\geq 1
. By a crude bound, we may only look at the term
m=1
in (
7.3
):

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=1\}=0}}\right).

Note that if
\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}<h<\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}
, then
M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=1
and
S_{h}=\ominus
(recall (
6.11
)). Thus we have the inclusion of events:

\bigg\{\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=1\}=0\bigg\}\subset\{\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}=1\}.

But by the discussion following (
•
‣
6.2.2
), we know that
\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T$}}}-\mathchoice{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}{\eta_{\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tt T-1$}}}
is geometric with parameter
\theta(1)=\frac{1}{2q(1)}
. This provides (
7.6
).
∎

From
7.3
and the fast merging property of
6.7
, we deduce the convergence of the first moment. Recall the notation
V_{\mer}
introduced in
6.6
.

Lemma 7.4

(The first moment converges)
.

The following assertions are true:

1.

For
k^{\prime}\geq k
and for all
A>0
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k^{\prime}})}{k^{\prime}}}}\right]}}\right|\leq 2\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}\leq A}}\right).

(7.7)

2.

The first moment converges:

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]\to\lambda\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

3.

The limit
\lambda
is in
(0,1)
.

The quantitative bound in
Item

1
will also be used later in the proof of
9.1
.

Proof.

We first prove the bound in
Item

1
using
6.7
.

Let now
F
and
F^{\prime}
be the number of forks along the trajectories of
(Z_{h}^{k})
and
(Z_{h}^{k^{\prime}})
respectively, as in (
7.3
), and
F_{A}
and
F_{A}^{\prime}
their truncated version, as in (
7.4
).
Under the coupling
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{k,k^{\prime}}
of
6.7
, we can write
F-F^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle F-F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}-\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}^{\prime}=\ominus\text{ and }(M^{*}_{h})^{\prime}=W_{h}^{\prime}=m\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq V_{\mer}}\sum_{h\geq 1}\big(\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}-\mathds{1}\{S_{h}^{\prime}=\ominus\text{ and }(M^{*}_{h})^{\prime}=W_{h}^{\prime}=m\}\big),

(7.8)

where in the last equality we used that
Z^{k}
and
Z^{k^{\prime}}
are identical as soon as
M^{*}
and
(M^{*})^{\prime}
are both strictly smaller than
V_{\mer}
.
To conclude, note from (
7.1
)–(
7.3
) that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k^{\prime}})}{k^{\prime}}}}\right]}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F^{\prime}=0}}\right)}}\right|,

and

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F^{\prime}=0}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathds{1}_{F=0}-\mathds{1}_{F^{\prime}=0}}}\right|}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|F-F^{\prime}}}\right|}}\right]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(1\wedge|F-F^{\prime}|)\mathds{1}_{V_{\mer}}\geq A}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(1\wedge|F-F^{\prime}|)\mathds{1}_{V_{\mer}}<A}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}<A}}\right),

where for the last inequality we used (
7.1.1
). This completes the proof of
Item

1
.

We now prove the convergence in
Item

2
by Cauchy characterization.
The first term on the right-hand side of (
7.7
) is small as
A\rightarrow\infty
because of (
7.5
) in
7.3
, while the second term is small for fixed
A
and
k,k^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
as a result of
Item

2
in
6.7
.
This proves the Cauchy characterization and so
Item

2
.

It remains to check
Item

3
,
i.e.
that the limit
\lambda
is in
(0,1)
. The fact that
\lambda
is not
1
is a straightforward consequence of (
7.6
). We now check that it is non-zero. We fix
A\geq 1
and write

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}

\displaystyle=F_{A+1}+\sum_{m=1}^{A}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\}.

We recall from (
6.28
) the notation
H_{A}=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\geq 1\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}M_{t}\leq A}}\right\}
. Using the strong Markov property of
Z
together with the fact that the transitions only depend on
M
(
Section

6.2.1
), we have

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)=\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{F_{A+1}=0}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{M({H_{A}})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)}}\right]\geq\inf_{1\leq\mathsf{k}\leq A}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{A+1}=0}}\right),

where for the inequality we used that
1\leq M({H_{A}})\leq A
.
But by Markov’s inequality

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{A+1}=0}}\right)=1-\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{A+1}\geq 1}}\right)\geq 1-\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A+1}}}\right],

which is non-zero if
A
is large enough thanks to (
7.5
). Combining the last two displayed equations, we get a uniform lower bound for
\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)
, as we wanted.
∎

7.1.2
The second moment

We now turn to the second moment.
First, let us recall some notation and introduce some more.
Recall from the beginning of
Section

6.1.1
and (
6.2
) that conditionally on
T^{k}
, we let
L^{k}
be a leaf chosen uniformly at random among the
k
leaves of the leftmost maximal positive subtree
T^{k,\lmax}
and denote, for
h\geq 0
, by
v_{h}
the ancestor (if it exists) of
L^{k}
at height
h
; see
Figure

10
.
The process
Z^{k}=(Z^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}=(S_{h},D_{h},M^{*}_{h},W_{h})_{h\geq 0}
defined in (
6.2
) records some useful information about the subtrees above each of the vertices
(v_{h})_{h\geq 0}
.
Now, pick
\widetilde{L}^{k}
a second leaf with the same distribution as
L^{k}
, independently of
L^{k}
conditionally on
T^{k}
.
We introduce the process
\widetilde{Z}^{k}=(\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}
similarly to the process
Z^{k}
, recording information along the ancestral line of
\widetilde{L}^{k}
;
and with
\dagger
as in (
6.3
), we let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}\coloneqq\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{h\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}Z_{h}^{k}\neq\widetilde{Z}_{h}^{k}\ \text{or}\ Z_{h}^{k}=\widetilde{Z}_{h}^{k}=\dagger}}\right\},

(7.9)

so that
v_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}-1}
is the highest common ancestor of
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}
.
Note that as soon as
L^{k}\neq\widetilde{L}^{k}
, then
\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}<\min(\eta,\widetilde{\eta})
.

We record some useful facts in a preliminary lemma.
The proof would follow straightforwardly from the arguments presented in
Section

6.2
so we omit it.

Lemma 7.5

(Following the paths leading to the two marked leaves)
.

The following properties hold:

1.

The two processes
Z^{k}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{k}
have the same law.

2.

The process
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}
is a Markov chain.

3.

The divergence height
\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}
is a stopping time for the Markov chain
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{h\geq 0}
.

4.

Whenever
L^{k}\neq\widetilde{L}^{k}
, the two leaves
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}
belong respectively to one and the other subtree above
v_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}-1}
so that

\displaystyle(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}},W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})=(\widetilde{W}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}},\widetilde{M}^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}).

5.

Conditionally on
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
, the two processes
(Z^{k}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}+h})_{h\geq 0}
and
(\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}+h})_{h\geq 0}
are independent.

We then prove a first estimate, which roughly says that the explorations towards
L_{k}
and
\widetilde{L}_{k}
do not stick together for too long.

Lemma 7.6

(Divergence occurs quickly)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
k\geq A>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right)\leq\frac{A^{2}}{k}+\frac{C}{A}.

Proof.

Let
k\geq A>1
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right)=\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\leq A^{2}}}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}>A^{2}}}\right).

We show below that the first term is small because it would require picking the second leaf
\widetilde{L}^{k}
quite close to
L^{k}
.
For the second term, we show that such an event can only happen if the process
M
has an unlikely trajectory.

Assume that
L^{k}\neq\widetilde{L}^{k}
, meaning
\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}<\eta
. Note that
S_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=\oplus
because
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}
are leaves of the same maximal positive subtree.
Thus we have
M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}-1}
.
Recall from (
6.9
) the definition of the process
M
as a time-changed version of
M^{*}
,
so that by the remark above
M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
and
M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
are two consecutive values along the trajectory
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{M_{t}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}t\geq 0}}\right\}
.
Recall from (
6.28
), the random variable
H_{m}
which is such that
M(H_{m})=\sup(\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},m\mathclose{\rrbracket}\cap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{M_{t}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}t\geq 0}}\right\})
.
For any
m\geq 1
,
we can associate a node
v^{m}
in the original tree
T^{k}
, which corresponds to the lowest vertex on the spine from the root to the leaf
L^{k}
such that
T^{k,\lmax}
has at most
m
leaves (weakly) above
v^{m}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}(v^{m})
the set of leaves of
T^{k,\lmax}
above vertex
v^{m}
, which has cardinality
M(H_{m})
, that is at most
m
.

Consider the event
\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A,\,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\leq A^{2}\}
.
By the above discussion, if this event is realized, then necessarily the leaf
\widetilde{L}^{k}

belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}(v^{A^{2}})
(note that this is obvious when
L^{k}=\widetilde{L}^{k}
).
Since by definition this tree has
M(H_{A^{2}})\leq A^{2}
leaves,
by reasoning conditionally on
Z^{k}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A,\,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\leq A^{2}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{L}^{k}\in\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}(v^{A^{2}})}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{M(H_{A^{2}})}{k}}}\right]\leq\frac{A^{2}}{k}.

We now handle the second term.
By a similar discussion, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A,\,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}+W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}>A^{2}}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M(H_{A^{2}})<A}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{A-1}\pi(M(H_{A^{2}}-1),i)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{A-1}C\frac{A}{A^{2}}q(i)\leq\frac{C}{A}

for some constant
C
, by the second bound in
Item

1
of
6.12
.
∎

Recalling the first moment convergence provided by
7.4
, we now show that the second moment converges.

Lemma 7.7

(The second moment converges)
.

The following assertions are true:

1.

For any
k\geq A>1
we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]-\lambda^{2}}}\right|\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+3\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{\mathsf{k}})}{\mathsf{k}}}}\right]-\lambda}}\right|+7\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{A^{2}}{k}+\frac{C}{A}}}\right).

2.

We have the convergence

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\to\lambda^{2}\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty.

The precise form of the first item will be used to obtain quantitative bounds in
9.1
.

Proof.

Conditionally on
T^{k}
, let
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}
be two (possibly equal) independent leaves chosen uniformly at random among the
k
leaves of the leftmost maximal positive subtree
T^{k,\lmax}
.
Recall from (
7.3
) the random variable

F=\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h\geq 1}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\},

counting the number of forks along the spine towards the marked leaf
L^{k}
.
Denote by
\widetilde{F}
the analogous quantity for the marked leaf
\widetilde{L}^{k}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]=\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0,\widetilde{F}=0}}\right).

Recall that
\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}

is one plus the height of the highest common ancestor of
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}

and set

\displaystyle F_{{\leq}\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\coloneqq\sum_{m\geq 1}\sum_{h=1}^{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\mathds{1}\{S_{h}=\ominus\text{ and }M^{*}_{h}=W_{h}=m\},

\displaystyle F_{{>}\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\coloneqq F-F_{{\leq}\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{F}_{{>}\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\coloneqq\widetilde{F}-F_{{\leq}\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}},

where we note that
F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
is a function of
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
and
F_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
and
\widetilde{F}_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
are a function of
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{h\geq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}+1}
.
With this notation, we get

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]=\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0,F_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0,\widetilde{F}_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0}}\right).

(7.10)

By Lemma
7.5
,
we get that conditionally on
(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
, the processes
(Z^{k}_{h})_{h\geq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}+1}
and
(\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{h\geq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}+1}
are distributed as two independent versions of
(Z_{h})_{h\geq 1}
under the respective measures
\mathbb{P}_{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}}
.
18
18

18

Note that here we are using that the Markov property of
(Z^{k}_{h})_{h}
holds when conditioning either on the full state
Z^{k}_{h}=(S_{h},D_{h},M^{*}_{h},W_{h})
or just on
M^{*}_{h}
, since the transitions depend only on this third coordinate; see
Section

6.2.1
.

Introduce the notation

\epsilon(k)\coloneqq\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F=0}}\right)-\lambda=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]-\lambda.

This ensures that we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0,\widetilde{F}_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}(Z^{k}_{h},\widetilde{Z}^{k}_{h})_{0\leq h\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}}}\right)=(\lambda+\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))\cdot(\lambda+\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})).

Using the last display allows us to rewrite the right-hand side of (
7.10
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0,F_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0,\widetilde{F}_{>\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0}\cdot(\lambda+\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))\cdot(\lambda+\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))}}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{2}\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0}\cdot(\lambda\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})+\lambda\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})+\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))}}\right].

(7.11)

Let us first handle the term
\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1}}\right)
in (
7.1.2
).
For all
A>1
, we can write

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1}}\right)=\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A}}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right).

Now on the event
\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A\}
, we have
F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\leq F_{A}
, where we recall that
F_{A}
was introduced in (
7.4
).
Hence, by Markov’s inequality,

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{A}\geq 1}}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}].

We conclude that

\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1}}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq 1,M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right).

Now let us control the last term in (
7.1.2
).
We have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{F_{\leq\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=0}\cdot(\lambda\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})+\lambda\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})+\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))}}\right]}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lambda|\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})|+\lambda|\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})|+|\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})|}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A,W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A\}}\cdot(\lambda|\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})|+\lambda|\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})|+|\epsilon(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}})\epsilon(W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}))|}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+3\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A,W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A\}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3\sup_{m\geq A}|\epsilon(m)|+3\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A,W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A\}^{\mathrm{c}}}}\right),

where we used the two bounds
\lambda\leq 1
and
|\epsilon(\cdot)|\leq 1
.
Finally, using a union bound and the fact that
M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
and
W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}=\widetilde{M}^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}
have the same distribution by symmetry of
L^{k}
and
\widetilde{L}^{k}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\{M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A,W_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}\geq A\}^{\text{c}}}}\right)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right).

Putting everything together and using Lemma
7.6
, we get

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]-\lambda^{2}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+3\sup_{m\geq A}|\epsilon(m)|+7\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M^{*}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\divv}}<A}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+3\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{\mathsf{k}})}{\mathsf{k}}}}\right]-\lambda}}\right|+7\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{A^{2}}{k}+\frac{C}{A}}}\right).

Hence,
Item

1
is proved.

The convergence in
Item

2
is a consequence of
Item

1
, together with
7.3
and
7.4
, by taking
A
going to infinity with
k
, with
A=o(\sqrt{k})
.
∎

7.4
and
7.7
entail our law of large numbers in
7.2
.

Proof of
7.2
.

The result follows from Chebyshev’s inequality since, for any
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigg|\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\lambda\bigg|\geq\delta}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bigg(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\lambda\bigg)^{2}}}\right]\to 0\qquad\text{as }k\to\infty,

by
7.4
and
7.7
.
∎

7.2
Good regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q

With the law of large numbers of
7.2
in hand, we can now show the good regularity estimates for the sequences
q
and
Q
stated in
7.1
.

Recall, from (
5.3
), the a.s. finite stopping times

\tau_{k}\coloneqq\min\{n\geq 1\mid X_{n}=k\},\qquad k\geq 1.

We first state a preliminary lemma, which improves the estimates in (
5.8
) of
5.3
and constitutes the main input towards
7.1
. The constant
\lambda
was introduced in
7.2
.

Lemma 7.8

(Asymptotics for the sequence
q(k)
)
.

As
k\to\infty
, we have

q(k)\sim\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}\lambda k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right].

Before proving the lemma, we show how it implies
7.1
.

Proof of
7.1
given
7.8
.

The proof is basically the same as the end of the proof of
5.1
on page
5.20
. By (
5.7
) in
5.3
and
7.8
,

\frac{Q(k+1)}{Q(k)}=1-\frac{q(k)}{Q(k)}=1-\frac{1}{2\lambda k}+o\bigg(\frac{1}{k}\bigg)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda k}+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(7.12)

We first show the claim on
Q
. Let
x\geq 1
. Multiplying the estimates (
7.12
) over a telescopic product, we have as
k\to\infty
,

\frac{Q(xk)}{Q(k)}=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=k}^{xk-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda i}+o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\sum_{i=k}^{xk-1}\frac{1}{i}+o(1)}}\right)\to x^{-\frac{1}{2\lambda}}.

(7.13)

By Karamata’s characterization theorem
[
BGT89
, Theorem 1.4.1]
, we obtain the existence of a slowly varying function
\Phi
such that
Q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\lambda}}\Phi(k)
. In particular,
5.1
implies
\lambda=\alpha
, which proves the part of
7.1
on
Q
.

Finally, by comparing
7.8
with (
5.7
) in
5.3
, we have

q(k)\sim\frac{1}{2\lambda k}Q(k)=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\Phi(k)k^{-1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\quad\text{as }k\to\infty,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
7.8
.

Proof of
7.8
.

We divide the proof into five steps. The core of the proof is Step 3, where we refine our proof of the lower bound on
q(k)
in
5.3
. The improvement will come from the precise asymptotics for
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})
obtained in
7.2
.

Step 1
: Truncation.

We fix a constant
K\geq 1
(to be thought of as a large constant to be tuned at the end of the proof in Step 5) and for the rest of the proof we assume that
k>K
. The proof starts in the same way as the proof of
5.3
: by the second equality in (
5.11
), for all
k\geq 1
, we have

q(k)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)}}\right].

In view of
5.3
and (
5.15
) say, we expect
\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}
to be typically of order
\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}
.
Therefore, we split the sum in two, according as
n\geq\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor
or not. If
n\geq\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor
, note that by the asymptotics in
3.3
,

r(n)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)r(\tau_{k+1}),

that is, there is a constant
C_{K}
depending on
K
(but not on
k
) such that
|r(n)-r(\tau_{k+1})|\leq\frac{C_{K}}{k}r(\tau_{k+1})
. This implies

q(k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbbm{1}_{n<\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor}\,r(n)}}\right].

(7.14)

Roughly speaking, we will estimate the first term by applying the strategy of the lower bound of
5.3
in a more accurate way, where the additional precision will come from
7.2
. The second term will be bounded crudely by the same proof as the upper bound of
5.3
. Our main goal will be to show that all the error terms that will appear are small relative to
q(k)
.

Step 2
: Neglecting the second term of
Equation

7.14
.

Since it is easier, let us start with the second term.
We note that if
n<\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor
, then
\tau_{k+1}>n+\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\rfloor
.
Using the monotonicity of
r(\cdot)
from
3.3
, we can bound
r(n)
by
r(\tau_{k})
and condition on
\tau_{k}
to get

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbbm{1}_{n<\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor}\,r(n)}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k})\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>n+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)}}\right].

(7.15)

Using the change of variables
j=n-\tau_{k}+\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\rfloor
, we can now use (
5.14
) to obtain, almost surely,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>n+\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\Big\rfloor\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>\tau_{k}+j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{eqn:bound_tail_increment_sigma}}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j\geq\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\rfloor}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{j}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\lfloor K\tau_{k}/k\rfloor-1}^{+\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{t}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}\mathrm{d}t.

Now for
k
large enough so that the integral is finite, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\lfloor K\tau_{k}/k\rfloor-1}^{+\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{t}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{-p(k-1)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau_{k}}{p(k-1)-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\rfloor-1}{\tau_{k}}}}\right)^{1-p(k-1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau_{k}}{p(k-1)-1}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-pk+1+p}}\right)\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{K-2}{k}}}\right)}}\right),

where for the bound in the
\log
we used that
\tau_{k}\geq k
.
We conclude that there exists a constant
C>0
(independent of
K
) and a threshold
A_{K}
such that for
k\geq A_{K}
,

\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}>n+\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\Big\rfloor\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}}}\right)\leq C\frac{\tau_{k}}{k}\mathrm{e}^{-pK}.

By integrating in
\tau_{k}
, we finally obtain that for
k\geq A_{K}
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n\geq\tau_{k}}\mathbbm{1}_{n<\tau_{k+1}-\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\rfloor}\,r(n)}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:2nd_term_cond_tau_k}}}{{\leq}}C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-pK}}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k})\tau_{k}}}\right]\,\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{eq:asympt r(m)}}}}{{\leq}}\,\,C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-pK}}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:q_sigma_lower_and_upper}}}{{\leq}}C\mathrm{e}^{-pK}q(k),

(7.16)

for some other constant
C
. This bounds the second term of (
7.14
).

Step 3
: Estimating the first expectation in
Equation

7.14
.

For the first expectation in (
7.14
), we will be more precise and rely on
7.2
. Recall the notation
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(t)
for the set of leaves contained in all maximal positive subtrees of
t
. Let
\lambda\in(0,1)
be the constant appearing in
7.2
. We now take
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
(to be thought of as a small constant to be tuned at the end of the proof in Step 5) and denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
the set of finite decorated trees
t
such that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(t)}{\mathrm{LIS}(t)}-\lambda}}\right|\geq\varepsilon.

In particular,
7.2
means that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathrm{LIS}(T)=k}}\right)\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. For future reference, observe that by definition of
\tau_{k}
and since
T
conditioned to have size
n
is distributed as
T_{n}
,
this implies that as
k\to\infty
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n={\tau_{k}}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)\mathds{1}_{T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}}}\right]=\sum_{n\geq 1}r(n)\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon},X_{n}=k}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon},\LIS(T)=k}}\right)=o(q(k)).

(7.17)

Since our goal is to estimate the term

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]}}\right]

in
Equation

7.14
, we will first be interested in estimating

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-m\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right),

(7.18)

which will be achieved in (
7.24
).
For this, we mimic (in a more precise way) the proof of the lower bound on
q(k)
in
5.3
.

For the rest of this step of the proof we fix
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
.
We start by looking at the event we condition on in (
7.18
). Let
n>k
.
We recall from
Section

2.1
that conditionally on
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, the decorated tree
T_{n-1}
is obtained by removing a uniformly chosen leaf
L_{n}
of
T_{n}
.
Therefore, if
X_{n}=k
, we have
\tau_{k}=n
if and only if
L_{n}
belongs to the intersection of all maximal positive subtrees of
T_{n}
,
i.e.
almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}=n\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})}{n}\mathds{1}_{X_{n}=k}.

By distinguishing whether
T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
or not, and in the case
T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
, using the crude bound
0\leq\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T_{n})\leq X_{n}
and that
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we obtain for all
n>k
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}=n\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\right)-\lambda\frac{k}{n}\mathds{1}_{X_{n}=k}}}\right|\leq\frac{k}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon+\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}}}\right).

(7.19)

We now turn to (
7.18
). For all
i,n\geq 1
such that
i\leq n-2
and
n-i>k
, the events
\{\tau_{k+1}=n\}
and
\{\tau_{k}\leq n-i\}
belong to
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n-i}
and have positive probability, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}=n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n-i}}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right].

In addition, note that on the event
\{\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i\}
, we have that
X_{n-i}=k
. Therefore,
using the bound in (
7.19
) with
n-i
in place of
n
, for all
i,n\geq 1
such that
i\leq n-2
and
n-i>k
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)-1+\lambda\frac{k}{n-i}}}\right|\\
\leq\frac{k}{n-i}\Big(\varepsilon+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)\Big).

(7.20)

We now also assume
i\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and define, for all
i,n\geq 1
such that
i\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-i>k
,

x_{i}^{k,n}\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)-1+\lambda\frac{k}{n}.

Recalling that we fixed
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
, our last bound and the triangle inequality entail that if
1\leq i\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-i>k
,

\displaystyle|x_{i}^{k,n}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k}{n-i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)}}\right)+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{k}{n-i}-\frac{k}{n}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3k}{n}\Big(\varepsilon+\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)\Big),

(7.21)

where for the last inequality we used, for the first term, that
n-i\geq n(1-K/k)\geq n(1-\varepsilon/2)\geq n/2
since
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and for the second term, that
\frac{k}{n-i}-\frac{k}{n}=\frac{k}{n}\frac{i}{n-i}\leq\frac{k}{n}\varepsilon
since
i\leq K\frac{n}{k}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n
.
By writing a telescopic product, for
j\geq 0
and
n-j>k
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{n}+x_{i}^{k,n}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{n}}}\right)^{j}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x_{i}^{k,n}}{1-\lambda k/n}}}\right)}}\right),

(7.22)

where, when
j=0
, we interpret the empty product as
1
and the empty sum as
0
.
Therefore, for
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-j>k
, we write

y_{j}^{k,n}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{j}\frac{|x_{i}^{k,n}|}{1-\lambda k/n}.

(7.23)

For later convenience, we also set
y_{j}^{k,n}\coloneqq 0
for all
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
with
n-j\leq k
.

Since
n>n-j>k
, the denominators
1-\lambda k/n
are bounded away from
0
(recall that
\lambda<1
). We deduce from (
7.21
) that for
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-j>k
, the sum
y_{j}^{k,n}
is bounded by a constant that depends only on
K
, and thus so is the sum

\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{j}\log\bigg(1+\frac{x_{i}^{k,n}}{1-\lambda k/n}\bigg)\bigg|\leq y_{j}^{k,n}.

Therefore, by the mean value inequality, we can find a constant
C_{K}>0
that depends only on
K
such that for all
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-j>k
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x_{i}^{k,n}}{1-\lambda k/n}}}\right)}}\right)-1}}\right|\leq C_{K}y_{j}^{k,n}\leq C_{K}y_{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}^{k,n}.

We conclude that for all
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<n-j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)=\mathds{1}_{n-j>k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{n}}}\right)^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}^{k,n}}}\right)}}\right),

(7.24)

since the probability on the left-hand side is zero when
n-j\leq k
.
The latter estimate gives us that for
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
, almost surely

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)>j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\big\rfloor-1}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}<\tau_{k+1}-j\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{J_{K}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right),

with
J_{K}\coloneqq\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor,\tau_{k+1}-k}}\right)
.
This is a geometric sum, so we end up with

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]=\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{J_{K}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)}}\right).

We now simplify the exponent
J_{K}
above. Notice that if
\tau_{k+1}-k<K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}
, then we must have that

\tau_{k+1}<\frac{k}{1-\frac{K}{k}}
. Since
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, this would imply
\tau_{k+1}<2k
. Hence, if
\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k
, then
J_{K}=\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor
. Besides, if
J_{K}=\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\lambda\frac{k}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)^{J_{K}}=O(\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}).

Therefore, we can combine these two observations into

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}}}\right]\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}\\
=\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}}}\right)}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}}}\right)}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}.

Integrating against
r(\tau_{k+1})
, we conclude that there is an absolute constant
C>0
(that may change from one inequality to the next) and a constant
C_{K}>0
depending on
K
such that for all
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]}}\right|\\
\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:asympt r(m)}}}{{\leq}}C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}}{\lambda k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:q_sigma_lower_and_upper}}}{{\leq}}C\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}q(k)+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right].

(7.25)

Although the indicator
\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}
is
1
with high probability as
k\to\infty
, we decide to keep it, as it will be useful in the next step of the proof.

We now estimate the first term on the right-hand side of (
7.14
) using (
7.25
): there is an absolute constant
C>0
and a (possibly different) constant
C_{K}>0
depending on
K
such that for all
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}}}\right]}}\right|\\
\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}q(k)+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}<2k}}}\right].

(7.26)

Finally, we may further bound the last term as follows:

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}<2k}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:asympt r(m)}}}{{\leq}}C\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}{k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}<2k}}}\right]\leq C\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}{k}\frac{2k}{\tau_{k+1}}}}\right]\leq 2C\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-3/2}}}\right].

By (
5.6
) and (
5.8
), we deduce that the last term of (
7.26
) is
o_{K}(q(k))
as
k\to\infty
. We have reached the following bound: for all
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k},\Big\lfloor K\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\Big\rfloor}}\right)}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}}}\right]}}\right|\\
\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda K}q(k)+o_{K}(q(k))+C_{K}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right].

(7.27)

The next step will be to bound the final error term in the above expression.

Step 4
: Estimating the final error term in (
7.27
).

Most of the remaining work is to show that the last term above is small compared to
q(k)
. By definition of
y^{k,n}_{j}
in (
7.23
) and the bound (
7.21
), this ultimately boils down to bounding the probability that
T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
for various values of
n
. Therefore, we will rewrite this error term in such a way that we can apply (
7.17
).

We start by fixing
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
.
By definition of
y^{k,n}_{j}
in (
7.23
), recalling in particular that
y_{j}^{k,n}=0
for all
0\leq j\leq K\frac{n}{k}
and
n-j\leq k
, the last term of (
7.27
) can be written

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\frac{n}{\lambda k}\frac{1}{1-\lambda k/n}\mathds{1}_{n-\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor>k}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}|x_{i}^{k,n}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\frac{n}{k}\mathds{1}_{n-\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor>k}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}|x_{i}^{k,n}|.

First, we note that the above indicator is always
1
. Indeed, using
K/k\leq\varepsilon/2<1/2
and
n\geq 2k
we get
n-\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor>k
.
Now, using the upper bound (
7.21
), we get that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]\leq\frac{3\varepsilon K}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\frac{n}{k}\\
+\frac{3}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right).

(7.28)

We now take care of the two sums separately.
For the first sum we have that

\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\frac{n}{k}=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{k}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:asympt r(m)}}}{{\leq}}\,\,\frac{C}{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k+1}^{-1/2}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn:q_sigma_lower_and_upper}}}{{\leq}}Cq(k)

(7.29)

for some absolute constant
C>0
that may change from one inequality to the next.

We now turn to the second sum in (
7.28
).
We write it as

\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)\\
=\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\frac{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon},\tau_{k}\leq n-i,\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)}.

(7.30)

Let us now bound the denominators from below when
n\geq 2k

and
1\leq i\leq\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor
, recalling that we fixed
k\geq 2K/\varepsilon
. These bounds guarantee that
\frac{k}{n-i}\leq\frac{1}{1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}}\frac{k}{n}<1
. Therefore, we can use the crude bound (
5.17
) to obtain

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{k}{n-i}}}\right)^{i}\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1}{1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}}\frac{k}{n}}}\right)^{i}.

Using
\log(1-x)\geq-\frac{x}{1-x}
for
0<x<1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k}\leq n-i\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)\geq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{i\tfrac{k}{n}}{(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2})-\tfrac{k}{n}}}}\right)\geq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{2K}{1-\varepsilon}}}\right),

where, in the last inequality, we used that
i\leq\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor
and
n\geq 2k
.
Then, setting
C_{K,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2K}{1-\varepsilon}}}\right)
, (
7.30
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k+1}=n,\tau_{k}\leq n-i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{n=2k}^{\infty}r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon},\tau_{k}\leq n-i,\tau_{k+1}=n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}=n}\,r(n)\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor Kn/k\rfloor}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k}\leq n-i}\mathds{1}_{T_{n-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=C_{K,\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k}\leq\tau_{k+1}-i}\mathds{1}_{T_{\tau_{k+1}-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\sum_{j=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}\mathds{1}_{T_{j}\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}}}}\right].

Since
r
is decreasing by
3.3
, the last expectation is
o_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q(k)}}\right)
as
k\to\infty
by (
7.17
).
Hence, the second sum in (
7.28
) is
o_{K,\varepsilon}(q(k))
as
k\to\infty
.
Since the first sum in (
7.28
) was bounded by
Cq(k)

in (
7.29
), we conclude (taking into account the additional constants in (
7.28
)) that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}y_{\lfloor K\tau_{k+1}/k\rfloor}^{k,\tau_{k+1}}\mathds{1}_{\tau_{k+1}\geq 2k}}}\right]\leq\varepsilon C_{K}q(k)+o_{K,\varepsilon}(q(k))\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(7.31)

Step 5
: Conclusion.

Let us come back to the formula (
7.14
) for
q(k)
. Recall the parameters
K\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
that we have introduced before. We bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
7.14
) using (
7.16
), and estimate the first one with (
7.27
) (with its last error term bounded by (
7.31
)). We obtain for
k
large enough (where “large enough” depends on
K
and
\varepsilon
):

q(k)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}}}\right]+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+O_{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}}}\right)}}\right)E(\varepsilon,K,k),

(7.32)

where the error term
E(\varepsilon,K,k)
is bounded by

|E(\varepsilon,K,k)|\leq(C\mathrm{e}^{-CK}+\varepsilon C_{K})q(k)+o_{K,\varepsilon}(q(k))

(7.33)

for some absolute constant
C>0
and some constant
C_{K}>0
depending on
K
.
We stress that the estimate (
7.32
) and the bound (
7.33
) hold for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
K\geq 1
.
We now tune the parameters
\varepsilon
and
K
. Let
\delta>0
. There exist
K=K(\delta)
(large enough) and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta)\in(0,1)
(small enough) such that the first term on the right-hand side of (
7.33
) is bounded by
\delta q(k)
. The values of
K
and
\varepsilon
are now fixed (and only depend on
\delta
). These values being fixed, we can now take
k_{0}=k_{0}(\delta)
large enough so that by (
7.33
), for all
k\geq k_{0}
,

|E(\varepsilon,K,k)|\leq 2\delta q(k).

This estimate holds for all
\delta>0
. Together with (
7.32
), this implies that

q(k)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}}}\right]+o(q(k))\quad\text{as }k\to\infty,

which means that
q(k)\sim\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[r(\tau_{k+1})\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\lambda k}}}\right]
as
k\to\infty
.
We conclude, using once more
3.3
and (
5.19
), that

q(k)\sim\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}\lambda k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau_{k}^{-1/2}}}\right]\quad\text{as }k\to\infty,

which establishes
7.8
.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

From now on, we know that
\lambda=\alpha
in
7.2
, as identified in the proof of
7.1
on page
7.13
; see in particular the discussion below (
7.13
).

8
The value of the exponent
\alpha
from the good regularity estimates for
q
and
Q

The goal of this section is to complete the proof of
2.2
, determining the exact value of the exponent
\alpha
. We stress that so far
\alpha
is only defined through
2.1
, but we also know that the good regularity estimates in
7.1
hold for
the same
exponent
\alpha
.

The latter estimates ensure that there are two slowly varying functions
\varphi,\Phi
and an exponent

\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:gamma-alpha-rel}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2\alpha}+1\in\Big(\frac{3}{2},2\Big)

(8.1)

such that

\displaystyle q(k)=k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Q(k)=k^{1-\gamma}\Phi(k).

(8.2)

We will use these estimates to deduce the relation of
2.2
between the values of
\alpha
and
p
.

8.1
The exact value of the exponent
\alpha

To identify the exact value of
\alpha
, we first rewrite the recursive relation in
2.4
as

\displaystyle q(k)=\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right).

(8.3)

Because we are not able to tackle this equation on the sequence
q
directly, we take interest in some related
inequations
that can be satisfied for a test sequence
\hat{q}
, namely

\displaystyle\hat{q}(k)\geq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right),

(8.4)

and

\displaystyle\hat{q}(k)\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)}}\right).

(8.5)

We will deduce
2.2
from the following two key ingredients:

1.

A comparison lemma for test sequences (
8.1
) which ensures that if we can check that one of the inequalities (
8.4
) or (
8.5
) holds for
k
large enough for some sequence
\hat{q}
, then we can compare the values of
q
and
\hat{q}
. This ingredient is partially inspired by
[
AC17
]
.

2.

The good regularity estimates in
7.1
(see (
8.2
) for a closer reference) and their consequences in
8.2
below.

We start by stating our main tool to compare the values of
q
and
\hat{q}
.

Lemma 8.1

(Comparison lemma for test sequences)
.

Let
(\hat{q}(k))_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of positive numbers.

1.

If
\hat{q}(k)
satisfies (
8.4
) for
k
large enough, then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall k\geq 1,\qquad q(k)\leq C\hat{q}(k).

2.

If
\hat{q}(k)
satisfies (
8.5
) for
k
large enough, then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall k\geq 1,\qquad c\hat{q}(k)\leq q(k).

We proceed by stating the consequences of the good regularity estimates that we need here. Recall the exponent
\gamma\in(3/2,2)
related to
\alpha
through (
8.1
), and the slowly varying function
\Phi
appearing in (
8.2
).

Lemma 8.2

(Asymptotic expansion lemma for test sequences)
.

For
\hat{q}(k)=k^{-\beta}
with
\beta\in\mathopen{(}\frac{3}{2}\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},2\mathclose{)}
, we have as
k\to\infty
,

•

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)}}\right)-\hat{q}(k)=k^{1-\gamma-\beta}\Phi(k)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-2^{\gamma+\beta-1}+\beta\int_{0}^{1/2}t^{1-\gamma}(1-t)^{-1-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t+o(1)}}\right).

•

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)\hat{q}(k)\sim\frac{1-p}{p}k^{1-\gamma-\beta}\Phi(k).

•

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\,\mathrm{even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}=o(k^{1-\gamma-\beta}\Phi(k)).

We will also need the following simple technical fact.

Lemma 8.3

(Monotonicity)
.

The function
f:\gamma\mapsto\frac{4^{\gamma-1}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}
is (strictly) decreasing on
(\frac{3}{2},2)
. Moreover, for any
x\in(3/2,2)
, we have
\frac{4^{x-1}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(2-x)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-x)}=2^{2x-1}-x\cdot\int_{0}^{1/2}t^{1-x}(1-t)^{-1-x}\,\mathrm{d}t
.

We now prove
2.2
from Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
.

Proof of
2.2
(assuming Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
).

For
\beta,\nu\in\mathopen{(}\frac{3}{2}\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},2\mathclose{)}
we write

\displaystyle g(\beta,\nu)=-2^{\nu+\beta-1}+\beta\int_{0}^{1/2}t^{1-\nu}(1-t)^{-1-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Note that
g(\beta,\beta)=-f(\beta)
for
f
defined in
8.3
.
Recall the exponent
\gamma\in(3/2,2)
in (
8.1
) and suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
g(\gamma,\gamma)>\frac{1-p}{p}
.
Then by continuity, we also have
g(\beta,\gamma)>\frac{1-p}{p}
for some
\beta\in(3/2,\gamma)
.
By the asymptotics of
8.2
, taking
\hat{q}(k)=k^{-\beta}
with this choice of
\beta
we then have, for
k
large enough,

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)\hat{q}(k)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)}}\right)-\hat{q}(k),

so that (
8.5
) is satisfied.

Thanks to
Item

2
in
8.1
, there exists
c>0
such that
ck^{-\beta}=c\hat{q}(k)\leq q(k)
for all
k\geq 1
. Yet by
7.1
,
q(k)=k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)
.
This is absurd because
\beta<\gamma
.

Symmetrically, if
g(\gamma,\gamma)=-f(\gamma)<\frac{1-p}{p}
, then by continuity, we also have
g(\beta,\gamma)<\frac{1-p}{p}
for some
\beta>\gamma
.
The same argument as above shows that (
8.4
) is satisfied for
k
large enough if
\hat{q}(k)=k^{-\beta}
,
so that by
8.1
, we have
Ck^{-\beta}=C\hat{q}(k)\geq q(k)
for all
k\geq 1
, for some constant
C>1
.
Because
\beta>\gamma
, this again contradicts the behavior of
q(k)=k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)
established in
7.1
.

In the end, we proved that necessarily
g(\gamma,\gamma)=-f(\gamma)=\frac{1-p}{p}
.
Since by
8.3
the function
f
is strictly decreasing on
\mathopen{(}\frac{3}{2}\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},2\mathclose{)}
, this ensures that this equality determines
\gamma
uniquely as we wanted. Substituting
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:gamma-alpha-rel}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2(\gamma-1)}
, we get that the equivalent equation
1/f(\gamma)=\frac{p}{p-1}
is exactly the equation in (
1.3
) for
\alpha
.
∎

In the following subsections, we give the missing proofs of Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
.

8.2
The comparison lemma for test sequences

We give here the missing proof of
8.1
.

Proof of
8.1
.

We start with the proof of
Item

1
.
Assume that a sequence of positive numbers
(\hat{q}(k))_{k\geq 1}
satisfies (
8.4
) for
k
larger than some value
k_{0}\geq 1
.
Let
C>1
be a constant chosen large enough so that

\displaystyle C\hat{q}(k)\geq q(k)

(8.6)

for all
1\leq k\leq k_{0}
, which exists because we assumed
\hat{q}
to be positive.
We now prove by induction that this inequality also holds for all values of
k\geq 1
.
Let
k\geq k_{0}+1
, and assume the bound
C\hat{q}(j)\geq q(j)
holds for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
.
Using sequentially the inequality (
8.4
) that holds for any
k>k_{0}
, the induction hypothesis, and the recursion equation (
8.3
) for
q
, we get

\displaystyle C\hat{q}(k)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\cdot C\hat{q}(k-i)+C\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+C\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right)=q(k).

Hence, the induction step is proved, so (
8.6
) holds for all
k\geq 1
, and
Item

1
is proved.

The proof of
Item

2
follows the exact same steps: Assume that a sequence of positive numbers
(\hat{q}(k))_{k\geq 1}
satisfies (
8.5
) for
k
larger than some value
k_{0}\geq 1
.
Let
c>0
be a constant chosen small enough so that
c\hat{q}(k)\leq q(k)
for all
1\leq k\leq k_{0}
, which exists because
q
is positive.
We now prove by induction that this inequality in fact holds for all values of
k\geq 1
.
Let
k>k_{0}
, and assume the bound
c\hat{q}(j)\leq q(j)
holds for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
.
Using sequentially the inequality (
8.5
) that holds for any
k\geq k_{0}
, the induction hypothesis, and the recursion equation (
8.3
) for
q
, we get

\displaystyle c\hat{q}(k)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\cdot c\hat{q}(k-i)}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\cdot c\hat{q}(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-p}{p}Q(k)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)q(k-i)+\frac{1-p}{2p}q(k)^{2}+\mathds{1}_{k\text{ even}}\frac{1}{2}q(k/2)^{2}}}\right)=q(k).

This proves
Item

2
.
∎

8.3
The asymptotic expansion lemma for test sequences

We give here the missing proof of
8.2
.

Proof of
8.2
.

The second estimate is in fact an equality.
The third one is immediate from the equality
q(k)^{2}=k^{-2\gamma}\varphi(k)^{2}
shown in
7.1
, and the fact that
\gamma>\beta-1
(since
\beta,\gamma\in(3/2,2)
). Therefore, the rest of the proof is devoted to the proof of the first estimate.

By Abel summation, the sum in the first estimate is

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}Q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)-\sum_{i=2}^{(k+1)/2}Q(i)\hat{q}(k-i+1)

\displaystyle=Q(1)\hat{q}(k-1)-Q\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\Big\rfloor\Big)\hat{q}\Big(\Big\lceil\frac{k+1}{2}\Big\rceil\Big)+\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}Q(i)(\hat{q}(k-i)-\hat{q}(k-i+1)).

Since
\hat{q}(k)=k^{-\beta}
, we have

\hat{q}(k-i)-\hat{q}(k-i+1)=\beta(k-i)^{-1-\beta}+O((k-i)^{-2-\beta}).

Using this estimate, together with the fact that
Q(1)=1
and
Q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\rfloor}}\right)\sim\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\Phi(k)
thanks to
7.1
,
we can rewrite the above sum as

\sum_{i=1}^{(k-1)/2}q(i)\hat{q}(k-i)=k^{-\beta}(1+O(k^{-1}))-\Phi(k)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}}}\right)^{1-\gamma-\beta}(1+o(1))\\
+\beta\Phi(k)k^{1-\gamma-\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}}}\right)+O(k^{-1-\beta}),

(8.7)

where for the last error term we used that

\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}Q(i)(k-i)^{-2-\beta}\leq\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}(k-i)^{-2-\beta}=O(k^{-1-\beta}).

We now focus on the sum on the right-hand side of
(
8.7
).
We split it into two parts: one for small values of
i
and one for large values of
i
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. For the values of
i\geq\varepsilon k
, by the uniform convergence theorem recalled in
Item

1
of Lemma
3.1
, we have that
\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}=1+o(1)
as
k\to\infty
, uniformly for all
i\in\mathopen{\llbracket}\varepsilon k\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},(k-1)/2\mathclose{\rrbracket}
. Hence, by Riemann sum approximation,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor}^{(k-1)/2}\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor}^{(k-1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}t^{1-\gamma}(1-t)^{-1-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We now focus on the values of
i<\varepsilon k
. Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter to be fixed later.
Using the Potter bounds recalled in
Item

2
of Lemma
3.1
, there exists a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\delta}\qquad\text{for all }i,k\geq 1\text{ such that $i<\varepsilon k$}.

Therefore, again by Riemann sum approximation,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=2}^{\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor-1}\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=2}^{\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma-\delta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}

\displaystyle=C(1+o(1))\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}t^{1-\gamma-\delta}(1-t)^{-1-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\gamma<2
, we can always choose
\delta>0
small enough so that
1-\gamma-\delta>-1
, ensuring that the integral above is finite.

Putting together the estimates for small and large values of
i
and sending
\varepsilon
to zero, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=2}^{(k-1)/2}\frac{\Phi(i)}{\Phi(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-1-\beta}=(1+o(1))\int_{0}^{1/2}t^{1-\gamma}(1-t)^{-1-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Substituting this last estimate in (
8.7
), and noting that

O(k^{-1-\beta})=o(k^{1-\gamma-\beta}\Phi(k))
since
\gamma<2
and
k^{\gamma-2}=o(\Phi(k))
, we obtain the first estimate of
8.2
.
∎

8.4
The monotonicity lemma

We finally give the missing proof of
8.3
.

Proof of
8.3
.

We first prove that the function
f:\gamma\mapsto\frac{4^{\gamma-1}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\gamma)}
is strictly decreasing on
(\frac{3}{2},2)
.
Since
\frac{4^{\gamma-1}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(2-\gamma)}{\Gamma(3/2-\gamma)}<0
for all
\gamma\in(3/2,2)
, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-f(\gamma)}}\right)>0\qquad\text{for all $\gamma\in(3/2,2)$}.

(8.8)

We have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-f(\gamma)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(4^{\gamma-1})+\log(\sqrt{\pi})+\log(\Gamma(2-\gamma))-\log(-\Gamma(3/2-\gamma))}}\right)

\displaystyle=\log(4)-\psi(2-\gamma)+\psi(3/2-\gamma),

where
\psi(\cdot)
denotes the Digamma function
\psi=\Gamma^{\prime}/\Gamma
. Using that
\psi(x)=\psi(x+1)-1/x
and that for all
x>0
,
\log(x)-1/x\leq\psi(x)\leq\log(x)-1/(2x)
(see e.g.
[
BE53
, Chap. 1.7.1]
and
[
Alz97
]
respectively), we get the bound

\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-f(\gamma)}}\right)\geq\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4(5/2-\gamma)}{2-\gamma}}}\right)+\frac{1}{4-2\gamma}-\frac{1}{3/2-\gamma}-\frac{1}{5/2-\gamma}.

Then a standard analysis of the function on the right-hand side of the above equation shows that (
8.8
) holds.

Finally, the second part of the lemma statement can be directly proved using basic properties of incomplete beta functions. More precisely, the integral that we want to express is nothing but the incomplete beta function

B_{1/2}(2-x,-x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1/2}t^{1-x}(1-t)^{-1-x}\mathrm{d}t,\quad x<2.

The integration by parts relations for incomplete beta functions (see e.g.
[
BE53
, Chap. 2.5.3]
) give

I_{1/2}(a,b)=I_{1/2}(a,b+1)-\frac{x^{a}(1-x)^{b}}{bB(a,b)},

where
B
denote the usual beta function and
I_{1/2}(a,b)\coloneqq\frac{B_{1/2}(a,b)}{B(a,b)}
. Using twice these relations, we are able to express
B_{1/2}(2-x,-x)
in terms of (usual) beta functions and
B_{1/2}(2-x,2-x)
. But by symmetry
B_{1/2}(a,a)=\frac{1}{2}B(a,a)
, so we are able to express
B_{1/2}(2-x,-x)
in terms of beta functions, and hence ultimately of gamma functions.
The formula claimed in
8.3
then comes from standard bookkeeping.
∎

9
Scaling limit results

The main goal of this section is to establish the scaling limit result for
X_{n}=\mathrm{LIS}(T_{n})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, stated in
2.3
.
This will be carried out in Sections
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
.
It is worth emphasizing that this result does not require any knowledge of the precise value of the exponent
\alpha
, which was determined in the previous section.
Then, in
Section

9.5
, we present an argument that completes the proof of
1.1
, and in
Section

9.6
, we establish a scaling limit result for the tree
T^{k,\lmax}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, proving
2.6
.

9.1
The scaling limit of the LIS: proof outline

We start with an outline of the proof of
2.3
.
Recall the notation
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}=\sigma(T_{m},\ m\geq n)
for the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the tree process after time
n
, as defined by Rémy’s algorithm.
We recall that for every
n\geq 1
, the tree
T_{n}
is obtained by removing a uniform leaf
L_{n+1}
from
T_{n+1}
.
We write

X_{n}=X_{n+1}-\mathds{1}_{L_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})},

so that for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{n}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=X_{n+1}-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}{n+1}

\displaystyle=X_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{n+1}}}\right)+\frac{1}{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha X_{n+1}-\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}}\right),

with the idea that the second term on the right-hand side of the last display should be negligible in some sense, as suggested by
7.2
.
From there, it is quite natural to introduce the sequence
(c_{n})_{n\geq 1}
defined as

\displaystyle c_{n}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{i}}}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{i-\alpha}{i}=\frac{\Gamma(n+1-\alpha)}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(1-\alpha)},

(9.1)

so that the rescaled process

\mathcal{X}_{n}:=c_{n}X_{n}

(9.2)

satisfies

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]=\mathcal{X}_{n+1}+R_{n+1},\quad\text{with}\quad R_{n+1}:=\frac{c_{n}}{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha X_{n+1}-\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}}\right).

(9.3)

Now, if the remainder terms
R_{n}
were identically zero, then the process
(\mathcal{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
would be a backward martingale and would thus converge almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Since, by properties of the Gamma function, it is easy to check that

\displaystyle c_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}n^{-\alpha},

(9.4)

this would show that
n^{-\alpha}X_{n}
converges almost surely, as stated in
2.3
.

Following this intuition, the strategy of our proof consists of showing that the remainder terms
R_{n}
are small in some quantifiable way.
For this, we rely on
the quantitative version of
7.2
stated below, where we recall that
T^{k}
is a
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
conditioned on the event
\{\LIS(T)=k\}
.
We recall that we identified at this point the constant
\lambda
in
7.2
with the exponent
\alpha
in
2.1
; see
Remark

7.9
.

Theorem 9.1

(Quantitative version of
7.2
)
.

There exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that
