\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\alpha}}\right|}}\right]

\displaystyle=O(k^{-\varepsilon}).

(9.5)

This theorem ensures that for a
p
-signed critical binary Bienaymé–Galton–Watson tree
T
conditioned on having
\LIS(T)=k
,
the number of leaves
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T)
in the intersection of all its maximal positive subtrees is close to
\alpha\LIS(T)=\alpha k
.
In our case, we can see from (
9.3
) that
R_{n}
is rather defined as (a constant times) the corresponding quantity for
T_{n}
, where we recall that
T_{n}
has the distribution of
T
conditioned on
\{|T|=n\}
. Nevertheless, with some additional work, we manage to translate the information of
9.1
under this different conditioning into some usable result on the remainders
R_{n}
.

Below, we first prove
9.1
in
Section

9.2
. We then turn it into
9.2
, which is a quantitative result about the remainders
R_{n}
, through a series of technical lemmas (Lemmas
9.3
,
9.4
,
9.5
and
9.6
) in
Section

9.3
.
Based on that, we will be able in
Section

9.4
to make rigorous sense of the martingale arguments sketched above, and to conclude the proof of
2.3
.

9.2
A quantitative version of the law of large numbers

This section is devoted to the proof of
9.1
. Recall the definition of good scales from
6.10
.
The proof of
9.1
relies on the fact that the good regularity estimates from
7.1
guarantee (as explained in
Remark

6.11
) the existence of a constant
C>0
such that every integer satisfies the good scale condition from
Item

1
in
6.10
.

Thus, in what follows, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{GoodScales}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{10^{m}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}m\geq 0}}\right\}

to be our new set of good scales.

Proof of
9.1
.

From
7.4
and
7.7
we know that the first and second moments of
\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}
converge towards respectively
\alpha
and
\alpha^{2}
(recall that
\lambda=\alpha
). We hence write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]=\alpha+\delta_{1}(k)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]=\alpha^{2}+\delta_{2}(k),

(9.6)

with
\delta_{1}(k),\delta_{2}(k)\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.
This ensures that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\alpha}}\right)^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]-2\alpha\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]+\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}(k)-2\alpha\delta_{1}(k),

and hence by Jensen’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\alpha}}\right|}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\alpha}}\right)^{2}}}\right]}=\sqrt{\delta_{2}(k)-2\alpha\delta_{1}(k)}.

(9.7)

From there, it is clear that if we obtain that
\delta_{1}(k),\delta_{2}(k)=O(k^{-\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
,
i.e.
that the convergence (
9.6
) of the first and second moments happens at least with polynomial speed, then we get the result claimed in the theorem.
We fix
k\geq 1
and take care of the two terms separately.

First, we bound
\delta_{1}(k)
.
Item

1
in
7.4
ensures that for any
k^{\prime}\geq k
and any
A>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k^{\prime}})}{k^{\prime}}}}\right]}}\right|\leq 2\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]+\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}\leq A}}\right).

(9.8)

We fix
A=k^{1/4}
and estimate the two terms on the right-hand side of (
9.8
) in turn.
First, by
7.3
, for some constant
C>0
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[F_{A}}}\right]\leq C\cdot Q(A)=C\cdot Q(k^{1/4})=k^{-\frac{1}{8\alpha}+o(1)},

(9.9)

where the last equality comes from the rough estimate on
Q
in
5.1
.
Second, the number of good intervals included in

\mathopen{\llbracket}k^{1/4}\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},k\mathclose{\rrbracket}
is
s(k^{1/4},k)=\frac{3\log k}{4\log 10}(1+o(1))
. Therefore, by
Item

1
of
6.7
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{\mer}\leq k^{1/4}}}\right)\leq(1-c)^{s(k^{1/4},k)}=(1-c)^{\frac{3\log k}{4\log 10}(1+o(1))}\leq k^{\frac{3\log(1-c)}{4\log 10}(1+o(1))}\leq k^{-\varepsilon+o(1)}

as
k\rightarrow\infty
, for some
\varepsilon>0
.
Since we have upper bounded the two terms on the right-hand side of (
9.8
) by quantities that do not depend on
k^{\prime}
, we can then take
k^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
in that inequality and get, for
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small enough,

\displaystyle|\delta_{1}(k)|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right]-\alpha}}\right|\leq k^{-\frac{1}{8\alpha}+o(1)}+k^{-\varepsilon+o(1)}=O(k^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}).

(9.10)

Second, we bound
\delta_{2}(k)
. We use
Item

1
of
7.7
: for any
k\geq A>1
, we have

\displaystyle|\delta_{2}(k)|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]-\lambda^{2}}}\right|\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}[F_{A}]+3\sup_{\mathsf{k}\geq A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\delta_{1}(\mathsf{k})}}\right|+7\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{A^{2}}{k}+\frac{C}{A}}}\right).

We again take
A=k^{1/4}
and use (
9.9
) and (
9.10
) to obtain

\displaystyle|\delta_{2}(k)|\leq k^{-\frac{1}{8\alpha}+o(1)}+O(k^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}})+O(k^{-\frac{1}{4}})=O(k^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}})

(9.11)

for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small enough.

Finally, substituting the bounds (
9.10
) and (
9.11
) into (
9.7
) yields the claimed result.
∎

9.3
Control of the remainders

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following lemma, which allows us to control the sum of the remainder terms
R_{n}
introduced in (
9.3
).

Lemma 9.2

(Control on the remainders)
.

The random sum
\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}|R_{n}|

is almost surely finite.
Furthermore, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that almost surely, for
n_{0}
large enough,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}|R_{n}|\leq n_{0}^{-\varepsilon}.

We point out that in this section, as in
9.2
, whenever we write “almost surely, for
n
large enough,” the threshold for “large enough” may be random.
The proof will rely on four intermediate lemmas, which we will state and prove as we proceed.
The proof of
9.2
will then be provided at the end of this section.
Recall from
Section

5
the notation
\tau_{k}
for the hitting time of
k
by
X_{n}
introduced in (
5.3
), as well as the probability
r(n)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=n}}\right)
from
3.3
.
For clearer expressions, we introduce the following notation: for a finite sign-decorated binary tree
t
, we write

f(t):=\lvert\alpha\LIS(t)-\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(t)\rvert.

Note that since
|R_{n}|=\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha X_{n}-\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n})}}\right|=\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n})
, we can write for any
n_{0}\geq 2
,

\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert R_{n}}}\right\rvert=\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n}).

(9.12)

Looking at the form of the expression (
9.12
), we can already see some similarity with the quantities appearing (in expectation) in the following rephrasing of
9.1
.

Lemma 9.3

(Rephrasing of
9.1
)
.

There is
\varepsilon>0
such that we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)\cdot f(T_{n})}}\right]=O(k^{1-\varepsilon}q(k))\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

(9.13)

Proof.

First,
9.1
tells us that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(T^{k})}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lvert\alpha\LIS(T^{k})-\#\mathcal{L}_{\cap}^{\max}(T^{k})\rvert}}\right]=k\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T^{k})}{k}-\alpha}}\right|}}\right]=O(k^{1-\varepsilon}).

Then, recalling that
r(n)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=n}}\right)
, we can rewrite the left-hand side as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(T^{k})}}\right]=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|T|=k}}\right)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(T)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\LIS(T)=k}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(k)}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}r(n)\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(T_{n})\mathds{1}_{\LIS(T_{n})=k}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q(k)}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)\cdot f(T_{n})}}\right].

Combining the last two displays finishes the proof.
∎

The next lemma aims to upper bound the sum of the remainders
R_{n}
in terms of the quantities controlled (in expectation) by the previous result.

Lemma 9.4

(Upper bound on the sum of remainders)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, almost surely, for any
n_{0}\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}|R_{n}|\leq C\cdot\sum_{k=X_{n_{0}}}^{\infty}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right).

Proof.

Using (
9.12
), we write

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}|R_{n}|=\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=\tau_{X_{n_{0}}}}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=X_{n_{0}}}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n})}}\right).

(9.14)

Since
c_{n}\sim\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}n^{-\alpha}
by (
9.4
) and
r(n)\sim cn^{-3/2}
thanks to
3.3
,
we bound the term appearing in front of
f(T_{n})
in the sum as

\displaystyle\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}\leq Cn^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}r(n),

for some constant
C>0
.
We substitute this into (
9.3
) and additionally use the fact that if
n\geq\tau_{k}
then
n^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\leq(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}
because
\frac{1}{2}-\alpha<0
(recall
1.2
). We get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n-1}}{n}f(T_{n})\leq C\cdot\sum_{k=X_{n_{0}}}^{\infty}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right),

which finishes the proof.
∎

In the next lemma, we use the estimates from
9.3
to further control the upper bound in
9.4
.

Lemma 9.5

(Polynomial tail control on the random sum)
.

The random sum

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)

is almost surely finite.
Furthermore, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that almost surely, for
k_{0}
large enough,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)\leq k_{0}^{-\varepsilon}.

Proof of
9.5
.

Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter to be fixed later. By (
2.2
) in
2.1
, there is a constant
C>0
such that, for all
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{n}}}\right]\leq Cn^{\alpha+\delta/2}
.
Using Markov’s inequality for
X_{2^{i}}
, for all
i\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2^{i}}\geq(2^{i-1})^{\alpha+\delta}}}\right)\leq(2^{i-1})^{-\alpha-\delta}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X_{2^{i}}}}\right]\leq C2^{-i\delta/2}

for some (other) constant
C>0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2^{i}}\geq(2^{i-1})^{\alpha+\delta}}}\right)<\infty.

By the Borel–Cantelli lemma and the fact that
X_{n}
is non-decreasing, we conclude that, almost surely,
X_{n}\leq n^{\alpha+\delta}
for all
n
large enough, so that
\tau_{k}\geq k^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\delta}}
for
k
large enough.
We let

K=1+\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k\geq 1\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau_{k}<k^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\delta}}}}\right\},

and note that the argument above proves that
K<\infty
almost surely.

The definition of
K
ensures that on the event
\{k\geq K\}
we have
\tau_{k}\geq k^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\delta}}
so that,
for any
k\geq 1
, noting that
1/2-\alpha<0
by
1.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}k^{\frac{1-2\alpha}{2\alpha+2\delta}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq k^{\frac{1-2\alpha}{2\alpha+2\delta}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right].

By

9.3

this can be bounded as

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)}}\right]\leq Ck^{\frac{1-2\alpha}{2\alpha+2\delta}}\cdot q(k)\cdot k^{1-\varepsilon}

for some constants
C,\varepsilon>0
.
Now, using the fact that
q(k)=k^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1+o(1)}
, thanks to
5.1
, we find that by taking
\delta
small enough, the last expression is
smaller than
Ck^{-1-\varepsilon/2}
.
Summing over
k\geq 2^{i}
we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{k=2^{i}}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)}}\right]=O(2^{-i\varepsilon/2})\quad\text{as }i\to\infty,

which is summable.
Hence, by Markov’s inequality,

\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k=2^{i}}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)>2^{-i\varepsilon/4}}}\right)<\infty.

Therefore, by the Borel–Cantelli lemma and monotonicity, with probability one we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k\geq K}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)\leq 2k_{0}^{-\varepsilon/4}

for
k_{0}
large enough. Finally, by taking
k_{0}
large enough, we can assume that
k_{0}\geq K
, which yields the claim of
9.5
.
∎

Lastly, because the sum appearing on the right-hand side of
9.4
starts from the random variable
X_{n_{0}},
which can, in principle, be small, we state the next lemma to ensure that this quantity grows at least polynomially fast in
n_{0}
almost surely.

Lemma 9.6

(Almost sure polynomial lower bound on
X_{n}
)
.

There exists an almost surely positive random variable
M
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle X_{n}\geq Mn^{\frac{p}{2}}\quad\text{almost surely}.

Proof of
9.6
.

We use a greedy construction to get a positive subtree
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n}
of
T_{n}
.
The way this is done is reminiscent of the proof of
5.1
.
We let
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{1}=T_{1}
.
Then at every step
n\geq 1
of the forward Rémy’s algorithm (
Section

2.1
):

(i)

If the edge
E_{n+1}
that is picked is adjacent to one of the leaves of
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n}

and
the created vertex is a
\oplus
, then we add the newly added leaf to
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n}
to get
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n+1}
.

(ii)

Otherwise we let
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n+1}\coloneqq\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n}
.

Now let us consider the dynamics of the number
Y_{n}
of leaves in
\underline{T\mkern-4.0mu}\mkern 4.0mu_{n}
.
Conditionally on
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
we have for all
n\geq 1
,

Y_{n+1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} Y_{n}+1\qquad&\text{with probability }\frac{pY_{n}}{2n-1},\\
Y_{n}\qquad&\text{with probability }\frac{2n-1-pY_{n}}{2n-1}.\\
\end{aligned}}}\right.

From these transitions, we note that the sequence of vectors
(pY_{n},2n-1-pY_{n})_{n\geq 1}

follows
the same dynamics as the (non-necessarily integer) amount of black and white balls in a generalized Pólya urn where:

•

At time
1
, the urn contains
p
black balls and
1-p
white balls.

•

At every step, a color is drawn at random from the urn with a distribution corresponding to its current composition. Then if the sampled color is black, we add
p
black balls and
2-p
white balls to the urn. Otherwise, we add
2
white balls to the urn.

Such an urn is called
balanced
because the number of balls added at each step is constant, and
triangular
because the corresponding replacement matrix is triangular.
From general results on triangular balanced Pólya urns
(e.g.
[
Agu09
, Theorem 2]
),
we get that
n^{-\frac{p}{2}}\cdot Y_{n}\rightarrow L
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
L>0
almost surely.
Hence, for
M\coloneqq\min_{n\geq 1}Y_{n}n^{-\frac{p}{2}}
we get the result stated in the lemma, because by definition of
X_{n}
we have
X_{n}\geq Y_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.
∎

We can now prove
9.2
by combining all of the above.

Proof of
9.2
.

Using consecutively the results of Lemmas
9.4
,
9.5
and
9.6
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}|R_{n}|\leq C\cdot\sum_{k=X_{n_{0}}}^{\infty}(\tau_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{n=\tau_{k}}^{\tau_{k+1}-1}r(n)f(T_{n})}}\right)\leq CX_{n_{0}}^{-\varepsilon}\leq CM^{-\varepsilon}n_{0}^{-p\varepsilon/2}.

Taking
n_{0}=2
shows the first claim of the lemma.
Then taking
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{p\varepsilon}{4}
ensures that for
n_{0}
large enough (the threshold being random), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}|R_{n}|\leq n_{0}^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}.

This concludes the proof of
9.2
.
∎

9.4
The scaling limit result

We can now establish the scaling limit result of
2.3
for the sequence
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

Proof of
2.3
.

Recall the notation
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}=\sigma(T_{m},\ m\geq n)
and denote
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\infty}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
.
Recall from
Section

9.1
that we defined, for all
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle c_{n}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{i}}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{X}_{n}=c_{n}X_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{n+1}=\frac{c_{n}}{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha X_{n+1}-\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}}\right).

We can now introduce the
auxiliary
(\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n})
-adapted process
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as

\mathscr{X}_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}_{n}-\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}R_{i},\quad n\geq 1,

(9.15)

which is almost surely finite thanks to
9.2
.

Step 1:
The process
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
has martingale increments.

Using
X_{n}=X_{n+1}-\mathds{1}_{L_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}
and
c_{n+1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{n+1}}}\right)c_{n}
along with the expression
R_{n+1}=\frac{c_{n}}{n+1}\big(\alpha X_{n+1}-\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})\big)
, we can write the increment of the auxiliary process as

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{n+1}=(\mathcal{X}_{n}-\mathcal{X}_{n+1})-R_{n+1}

\displaystyle=c_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}{n+1}-\mathds{1}_{L_{n+1}\in\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}}}\right).

From this expression, it is clear that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{n+1}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]=0,

(9.16)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{n+1})^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=(c_{n})^{2}\cdot\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}{n+1}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})}{n+1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(c_{n})^{2}=O(n^{-2\alpha}),

(9.17)

where, in the inequality, we applied the deterministic bound
\#\mathcal{L}^{\max}_{\cap}(T_{n+1})\leq n+1
, and then we used the asymptotics (
9.4
) for the sequence
(c_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

We have just proved that the
increments
of the process
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are those of an
L^{2}
backward martingale.
Unfortunately, we actually do not know at this point whether
\mathscr{X}_{n}
is in
L^{1}
or not,
so we cannot claim that it is a backward martingale.

Step 2:
Introducing related backward supermartingales.

We introduce

\uptau\coloneqq\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{n\geq 2\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sum_{i\geq n}|R_{i}|>n^{-\varepsilon}}}\right\},

(9.18)

where
\varepsilon>0
is as in
9.2
and we take the convention that
\uptau=+\infty
if no such
n
exists.
By the second part of
9.2
, we have
\uptau<+\infty
almost surely.
Then the negative part
\mathscr{X}^{-}_{n\vee\uptau}\coloneqq-\min(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau},0)
is bounded for all
n\geq 1
, since

\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}^{-}\leq\sum_{i>n\vee\uptau}|R_{i}|\leq({n\vee\uptau})^{-\varepsilon}\leq n^{-\varepsilon}\leq 1\qquad\text{almost surely.}

(9.19)

Moreover, using that
\{\uptau\leq n\}
is
\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}
-measurable, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}-\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathds{1}_{\uptau\leq n}(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}-\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\uptau\leq n}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{n+1}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]=0.

Hence, the increments of
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau})_{n\geq 1}
have vanishing (conditional) expectation.
Now for any
K>0
, the process
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K)_{n\geq 1}
is bounded between
-1
and
K
so it is in
L^{1}
.
Noting that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the function

y\mapsto(x+y)\wedge K

is concave,
we use Jensen’s inequality to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}+(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}-\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}))\wedge K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}-\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{n+1}}}\right]}}\right)\wedge K

\displaystyle=\mathscr{X}_{(n+1)\vee\uptau}\wedge K,

which ensures that the process
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K)_{n\geq 1}
is a backward supermartingale.

Step 3:
Proving the almost sure convergence of
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and hence of
(n^{-\alpha}X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

For any
K>0
, the process
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K)_{n\geq 1}
is a bounded backward supermartingale.
By standard results on backward supermartingales (see e.g.
[
Dur19
, Section 5.6]
19
19

19

Although technically the reference only deals with backward
martingales
, the argument readily extends to supermartingales since it only relies on Doob’s upcrossing lemma.
), this implies that
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K)_{n\geq 1}
converges almost surely as
n\to\infty
towards a limit
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{K}
and that for all
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\infty}}}\right]\leq\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{K}.

By monotonicity in
K
, we get that
(\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau})_{n\geq 1}
converges almost surely towards a (possibly infinite) limit
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{K}
.
Now since for all
n\geq 1
we have
\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\geq-1
, we can use the monotone convergence theorem when we pass to the limit
K\rightarrow\infty
in the previous display and get

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\infty}}}\right]=\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\wedge K\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overleftarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{\infty}}}\right]\leq\lim_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{X}_{\infty}^{K}=\mathscr{X}_{\infty}.

(9.20)

Since
\uptau<\infty
almost surely, we deduce that
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
also converges almost surely to the same
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
.
Note that the definition (
9.15
) of
(\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, the asymptotic
c_{n}\sim n^{-\alpha}/\Gamma(1-\alpha)
and its a.s. convergence towards
\mathscr{X}_{\infty}
ensure that we have the almost sure convergence

\displaystyle n^{-\alpha}X_{n}\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}X\qquad\text{where}\qquad X\coloneqq\Gamma(1-\alpha)\mathscr{X}_{\infty}.

(9.21)

Step 4:
Proving that
X<\infty
almost surely.

First, we prove that
X<\infty
almost surely relying on
L^{2}
estimates.
By (
9.4
), the random variables
(\mathscr{X}_{n+1}-\mathscr{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are all in
L^{2}
and by the martingale property (
9.16
), they are uncorrelated, hence orthogonal in
L^{2}
.
By the Pythagorean theorem, for any
1\leq N_{0}\leq N
we have, now using the second moment inequality (
9.4
),

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mathscr{X}_{N_{0}}-\mathscr{X}_{N})^{2}}}\right]=\sum_{n=N_{0}}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{n+1})^{2}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:second moment increment auxproc}}}{{=}}\sum_{n=N_{0}}^{N-1}O(n^{-2\alpha})\leq\sum_{n=N_{0}}^{\infty}O(n^{-2\alpha})=O((N_{0})^{1-2\alpha}),

where we used that
\alpha>1/2
, which is guaranteed by
1.2
.
This ensures that the sequence
(\mathscr{X}_{n}-\mathscr{X}_{1})_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}
so it converges in
L^{2}
(hence in probability) towards some almost surely finite random variable
Y
.
Thus
\mathscr{X}_{n}\rightarrow X=\mathscr{X}_{1}+Y
in probability, which is then almost surely finite.

Step 5:
Proving that
X
is almost surely positive.

Secondly, we argue that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X}}\right]>0
, or equivalently
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{\infty}}}\right]>0
.
By (
9.20
),

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{\infty}}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathscr{X}_{n\vee\uptau}}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}_{n\vee\uptau}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{i>n\vee\uptau}|R_{i}|}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}_{n\vee\uptau}}}\right]-n^{-\varepsilon},

(9.22)

where the last inequality follows by definition of
\uptau
in (
9.18
).
By the convergence in probability in
2.1
,
we know that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}_{n}\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2}}}\right)\geq 1/2
for
n
large enough.
In addition, since
\uptau
is almost surely finite, we also have
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\uptau>n}}\right)<1/3
for
n
large enough.
This implies that for
n
large enough,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}_{n\vee\uptau}}}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{X}_{n\vee\uptau}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{n}\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2}}\mathds{1}_{\uptau\leq n}}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}_{n}\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2},\uptau\leq n}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2}\Big(\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{X}_{n}\geq n^{-\varepsilon/2}}}\right)-\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\uptau>n}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{6}n^{-\varepsilon/2}.

Therefore we deduce from (
9.22
) that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X}}\right]>0
.

Now let us prove that
X>0
almost surely.
Picking two independent uniform leaves
L_{n}^{(1)}
and
L_{n}^{(2)}
of
T_{n}
, we break the tree into three regions:
T_{n}^{(1)}
and
T_{n}^{(2)}
are the two subtrees above the highest common ancestor of
L_{n}^{(1)}
and
L_{n}^{(2)}
containing the leaves
L_{n}^{(1)}
and
L_{n}^{(2)}
respectively, and
T_{n}^{(3)}
is the rest of the tree.
According to
3.8
we have
(|T_{n}^{(1)}|-1,|T_{n}^{(2)}|-1,|T_{n}^{(3)}|)\sim\mathrm{DirMult}(n-2;1/2,1/2,1/2)
and conditionally on those numbers, the trees
T_{n}^{(1)}
and
T_{n}^{(2)}
are independent
p
-signed uniform binary trees with these sizes.
We then write

\frac{X_{n}}{n^{\alpha}}\geq\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{|T_{n}^{(1)}|}{n}}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(1)})}{|T_{n}^{(1)}|^{\alpha}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{|T_{n}^{(2)}|}{n}}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(2)})}{|T_{n}^{(2)}|^{\alpha}}}}\right).

(9.23)

By
3.6
,
\frac{1}{n}(|T_{n}^{(1)}|,|T_{n}^{(2)}|,|T_{n}^{(3)}|)\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})
where
(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1/2,1/2,1/2)
. Besides, Step 3 above implies that, jointly with the last convergence, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(1)})}{|T_{n}^{(1)}|^{\alpha}},\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(2)})}{|T_{n}^{(2)}|^{\alpha}}}}\right)\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}}(X^{(1)},X^{(2)})

(9.24)

as
n\to\infty
, where
X^{(1)},X^{(2)}
are copies of
X
, independent from each other and from
(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})
.
Taking
n\rightarrow\infty
in (
9.23
), we get that

\displaystyle X\succeq\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{1}^{\alpha}\cdot X^{(1)},D_{2}^{\alpha}\cdot X^{(2)}}}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X=0}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X^{(1)}=X^{(2)}=0}}\right)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X=0}}\right)^{2},

so that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X=0}}\right)\in\{0,1\}
.
We know since
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[X}}\right]>0
that this probability cannot be
1
, so we must have
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X=0}}\right)=0
.

Step 6:
Proving that
X
is not deterministic.

Finally, we justify using similar arguments that
X
cannot be a finite constant almost surely.
Assume by contradiction that
X=b\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},\infty\mathclose{)}
almost surely.
Then, considering the same decomposition as above, we write
S_{n}^{(1,2)}
for the sign in
\{\ominus,\oplus\}
of the highest common ancestor of the leaves
L_{n}^{(1)}
and
L_{n}^{(2)}
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{\LIS(T_{n})}{n^{\alpha}}\geq\mathds{1}_{\{S_{n}^{(1,2)}=\oplus\}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{|T_{n}^{(1)}|}{n}}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(1)})}{|T_{n}^{(1)}|^{\alpha}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{|T_{n}^{(2)}|}{n}}}\right)^{\alpha}\cdot\frac{\LIS(T_{n}^{(2)})}{|T_{n}^{(2)}|^{\alpha}}}}\right).

(9.25)

Now, from our assumptions and using again Step 3 above, the left-hand side converges in law towards a constant
b
.
The right-hand side converges by (
9.24
) towards a random variable

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ber}(p)\cdot b\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{1}^{\alpha}+D_{2}^{\alpha}}}\right),

where
(D_{1},D_{2},D_{3})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1/2,1/2,1/2)
and
\mathrm{Ber}(p)
denotes a Bernoulli random variable with parameter
p
independent of everything else. Hence,
b\succeq\mathrm{Ber}(p)\cdot b\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{1}^{\alpha}+D_{2}^{\alpha}}}\right)
.
Since
\alpha<1
, the random variable on the right takes values strictly greater than
b
with positive probability.
This is a contradiction, so
X
is not deterministic.
∎

9.5
The limiting random variable
X
is a measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton

We prove the only remaining claim of
1.1
,
i.e.
that
X
is a deterministic measurable function of the Brownian separable permuton. The same explanation works for the companion remaining claim in the case of the Brownian cographon in
1.3
.

Recall from
Remark

3.2
that the permutations
\sigma_{n}
in
1.1
can be obtained from the
p
-signed uniform binary trees
\widetilde{T}_{n}=t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;U_{1},\dots,U_{n})
, where
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are i.i.d. uniform random variables in
[0,1]
and
(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p)
is the signed Brownian excursion used to construct the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
.
In particular, the trees
(\widetilde{T}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are coupled as in Rémy’s algorithm.

Fix
m\geq 1
and introduce, for all
n\geq 1
, the tree
\widetilde{T}^{(m)}_{n}=t(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p;U_{m+1},\dots U_{m+n})
.
Note that, since the two collections of points
\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}
and
\{U_{m+1},\dots,U_{m+n}\}
have all but
2m
points in common,
by definition of
\LIS(\cdot)
, we have for all
n\geq 1
the deterministic bound

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\LIS(\widetilde{T}^{(m)}_{n})-\LIS(\widetilde{T}_{n})}}\right|\leq 2m.

As a consequence, from
2.3
, we have the almost sure convergence

\displaystyle X=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-\alpha}\LIS(\widetilde{T}_{n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{-\alpha}\LIS(\widetilde{T}^{(m)}_{n}).

(9.26)

Hence, the limiting random variable
X
can be written as a deterministic measurable function of
((\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p),(U_{i})_{i>m})
.
Since this is true for any
m\geq 1
, we get that
X
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for
\mathcal{F}=\bigcap_{m\geq 1}\sigma((\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p),(U_{i})_{i>m})
.
Since in addition the random variables
U_{i}
for
i\geq 1
are i.i.d., by Kolmogorov’s
0
-
1
law, the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{G}=\bigcap_{m>1}\sigma((U_{i})_{i\geq m})
is trivial – in the sense that it only contains events of probability
0
or
1
. Since
\mathcal{F}=\sigma(\mathcal{G}\cup\sigma(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p))
, this ensures that
X
is almost surely a deterministic measurable function of
(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{s},p)
,
i.e.
of the Brownian separable permuton
\bm{\mu}_{p}
.

9.6
Scaling limit of the leftmost maximal positive subtree of
T^{k}

We prove in this final section the only remaining result,
i.e.

2.6
. Recall from (
6.1
) that we introduced the more compact notation
T^{k,\lmax}=(T^{k})^{\lmax}
.

Proof of
2.6
.

We first notice that
6.1
provides a recursive construction of
T^{k,\lmax}
as follows.
Let
\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}
be the set of leaves of
T^{k,\lmax}
.
Let
v_{1},v_{2}
be the two children of the root of
T^{k}
and let
K_{1},K_{2}
be their respective number of descendants in
\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}
.
We choose to order
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in such a way that
K_{1}\geq K_{2}
. We also set the convention
K_{2}=0
if the root has only one child in
T^{k,\lmax}
, and
K_{1}=K_{2}=0
if it has none (which implies
k=1)
. Then conditionally on
(K_{1},K_{2})=(k_{1},k_{2})
, the two trees of descendants of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
with leaves in
\mathcal{L}^{k,\lmax}
are independent, each distributed as
T^{k_{1},\lmax}
and
T^{k_{2},\lmax}
(taking the convention that
T^{0,\lmax}
is empty). Moreover, the law
20
20

20

The notation
q_{k}
is chosen to be consistent with
[
HM12
]
.

q_{k}
of
(K_{1},K_{2})
is given for
k\geq 1
by

\displaystyle q_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k_{1},k_{2})}}\right)

\displaystyle=p\frac{q(k_{1})q(k_{2})}{q(k)}

\displaystyle\mbox{if $k_{1}+k_{2}=k$ and $1\leq k_{2}<\frac{k}{2}$},

\displaystyle q_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k_{1},k_{2})}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p\frac{q(k_{1})q(k_{2})}{q(k)}

\displaystyle\mbox{if $k_{1}=k_{2}=\frac{k}{2}$},

\displaystyle q_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k,0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1-p)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q(k)}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}q(i)}}\right)

,

\displaystyle q_{k}(0,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2q(k)}\mathbbm{1}_{k=1}.

In the terminology of
[
HM12
, Section 1.2.1]
, this means that
T^{k,\lmax}
is a Markov branching tree with splitting distributions
q_{k}
(more precisely, we are in the case where the splitting is binary,
i.e.

q_{k}
is supported on partitions of
k
with
1
or
2
parts). Therefore, in order to conclude by
[
HM12
, Theorem 5]
, it is sufficient to check that the splitting distributions
(q_{k})
satisfy the assumption (H) of
[
HM12
]
, with the measure
\nu=\nu_{\gamma}
on binary partitions of
1
given by (
2.24
).

Let us now state this assumption (H) explicitly. We first define the normalized version
\overline{q}_{k}
of
q_{k}
as the measure on
[0,1]^{2}
such that, for any measurable function
f:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

\int f\,\,\mathrm{d}\overline{q}_{k}=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}q_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k_{1},k_{2})}}\right)f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k},\frac{k_{2}}{k}}}\right).

Then assumption (H) with the measure
\nu_{\gamma}
of (
2.24
) states that there is a slowly varying function
\ell(\cdot)
such that for any continuous function
f:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

k^{\gamma-1}\ell(k)\int(1-s_{1})f(s_{1},s_{2})\,\,\overline{q}_{k}(\mathrm{d}s_{1},\mathrm{d}s_{2})\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\int_{0}^{1/2}f(1-x,x)x^{1-\gamma}(1-x)^{-\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(9.27)

Note that the parameter
\gamma
of
[
HM12
]
corresponds to our
\gamma-1
.
It turns out that we can choose
\ell
to be
1/\varphi
with
\varphi
as in Theorem
7.1
,
i.e.

\varphi
is slowly varying and
q(k)=k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)
. Using the explicit probabilities
q_{k}
given above, the left-hand side of (
9.27
) then rewrites for
k\geq 2
as

\frac{k^{\gamma-1}}{\varphi(k)}p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor}\frac{q(k-i)q(i)}{q(k)}\frac{i}{k}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k-i}{k},\frac{i}{k}}}\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{\text{$k$ even}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{q(k/2)^{2}}{q(k)}\cdot\frac{1}{2}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}}\right)}}\right).

Note first that
\frac{q(k/2)^{2}}{q(k)}=4^{\gamma}k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)(1+o(1))
, so the second term is negligible. After replacing
q(k)
by
k^{-\gamma}\varphi(k)
, the last equation becomes

p\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\frac{\varphi(k-i)\varphi(i)}{\varphi(k)^{2}}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k-i}{k},\frac{i}{k}}}\right)+o(1).

We now fix
\varepsilon>0
, and split the sum according to whether
i<\varepsilon k
or not. First, by
Item

1
of Lemma
3.1
, the ratio
\frac{\varphi(k-i)\varphi(i)}{\varphi(k)^{2}}
converges to
1
as
k\to\infty
uniformly in
i\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\varepsilon k,\frac{k}{2}}}\right]
. By Riemann sum approximation, this implies

p\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=\varepsilon k}^{\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\frac{\varphi(k-i)\varphi(i)}{\varphi(k)^{2}}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k-i}{k},\frac{i}{k}}}\right)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}p\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}f(1-x,x)x^{1-\gamma}(1-x)^{-\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(9.28)

Now let us now handle the terms where
i<\varepsilon k
. We fix
\delta>0
small enough so that
\gamma+\delta<2
(recall from (
2.7
) that
\gamma<2
). Again, by
Item

1
of Lemma
3.1
, the ratio
\frac{\varphi(k-i)}{\varphi(k)}
is bounded by an absolute constant which does not depend on
\varepsilon
. So are the values of
f
and the factors
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma}
. On the other hand,
Item

2
of Lemma
3.1
shows
\frac{\varphi(i)}{\varphi(k)}=O\Big(\Big(\frac{i}{k}\Big)^{-\delta}\Big)
uniformly in
i<k
, so we can write

p\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{\varepsilon k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\frac{\varphi(k-i)\varphi(i)}{\varphi(k)^{2}}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k-i}{k},\frac{i}{k}}}\right)=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{\varepsilon k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{i}{k}}}\right)^{1-\gamma-\delta}}}\right)=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon^{2-\gamma-\delta}}}\right).

Combined with (
9.28
), this shows that (
9.27
) is satisfied and concludes the proof of Theorem
2.6
.
∎

Appendix A
Numerical simulations for the limiting random variable
X

We present in
Figure

12
and
Table

13
some numerical simulations for the limiting random variables
X(p)
appearing in
1.1
. Recall the notation
\sigma_{n}(p)\coloneqq\Perm[\bm{\mu}_{p},n]
from (
1.1
).

For the diagram on the left-hand side of
Figure

12
, we sampled
100\,000
independent copies of
\sigma_{n}(p_{i})\coloneqq\Perm[\bm{\mu}_{p_{i}},n]
of size
n=1\,000\,000
, for each
p_{i}=\frac{i}{10}
with
i\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},9\mathclose{\rrbracket}
. We then estimated the density function of (recall
1.1
)

\frac{\LIS(\sigma_{n}(p_{i}))}{n^{\alpha(p_{i})}}\approx X(p_{i})\qquad\text{for all }i\in\mathopen{\llbracket}1\mathclose{}\mathpunct{},9\mathclose{\rrbracket},

using the function SmoothKernelDistribution from Mathematica.

For the diagram on the right-hand side of
Figure

12
, we sampled
100\,000
independent uniform separable permutations
\overline{\sigma}_{n}
of the same size
n=1\,000\,000
, and estimated the density function (recall
1.4
) of

\frac{\LIS(\overline{\sigma}_{n})}{0.901\cdot n^{\alpha(1/2)}}\approx X(1/2),

again using SmoothKernelDistribution. The constant
c=0.901
was calibrated so that the two estimated densities on the right-hand side of
Figure

12
would be as close as possible. More precisely, we chose the constant
c
as the minimizer of

\int_{0}^{100}|f_{c}(x)-g(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
f_{c}(x)
is the approximate density function of

\frac{\LIS(\overline{\sigma}_{n})}{c\cdot n^{\alpha(1/2)}}
and
g(x)
is the approximate density function of
\frac{\LIS(\sigma_{n}(1/2))}{n^{\alpha(1/2)}}
. At
c=0.901
, the above integral takes the value
0.055
.

Finally, in
Table

13
we show the approximated values for the mean and variance of the random variables
X(p_{i})
.
